<meta>This page introduces the translator's purpose and methodology for translating the "Ikhwánu-s Safá" from Hindustani into English.</meta>

# TRANSLATOR'S PREFACE.

---

This translation has been made to supply a need that has often been expressed to me. *The Ikhwánu-s Safá* <note>original: "The Brethren of Purity," a 10th-century philosophical work originally in Arabic, later translated into Hindustani</note> is a standard Hindustani work, which is used as a textbook both in India and England. Those who must prepare themselves for examinations in this subject are often placed in situations where no competent instruction can be obtained. A translation, therefore, that is at once accurate and exact—with which the student may compare and correct his own work—will undoubtedly prove to be a great help and advantage. 

Since this is the object of the translation, I have maintained a close adherence to the text throughout, as strict as the necessity of making the English clear and intelligible would allow. Consequently, the version is not as smooth as it might have been made, but it will give the English reader a clear idea of the style and manner of the original.

When I commenced this work, I was not aware that any previous translation existed; however, there are at least three different versions. One, attributed to Mr. James Atkinson—the translator of parts of the *Sháh-náma* <note>original: "The Book of Kings," the Persian national epic</note>—was published in an Indian newspaper and reprinted in volume 28 of the *Asiatic Journal* for 1829. This translation is accurate and spirited, but it is imperfect, and for all practical purposes it—

<meta>Text continues on next page.</meta>

<summary>The translator explains that this version of the Ikhwánu-s Safá was created as a literal study aid for students, prioritizing accuracy over literary flair, and acknowledges previous incomplete translations.</summary>
<keywords>Ikhwánu-s Safá, Hindustani language, translation methodology, James Atkinson, Sháh-náma, Asiatic Journal, language examinations</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the Translator's Preface, detailing the history of previous translations and the specific sources used for this edition.</meta>

# Translator's Preface.

may be considered buried. I had nearly completed the work when a translation by Mr. T. P. Manuel came to my notice. That version was published at Calcutta in 1860 and is not very generally known. It gives a very fair idea of the work, but the translation is not sufficiently close to answer the purpose for which this work is intended. I am told there is another translation, published in India by a Muhammadan gentleman, but I have not met with it.

The original Arabic work has been translated into German by Professor Dieterici of Berlin. Our Hindustani translator, in his Preface, tells us something of the great work from which it is derived. It is a kind of Encyclopedia of considerable extent. Notices of the work have been published by Professor Flügel in volume 13, and by Professor Dieterici in volumes 15 and 18 of the *Journal of the German Oriental Society* <note>original: "Zeitschrift des Deutschen Morgenlandische Gesellschaft"</note>.

The present translation has been made from the excellent edition of the text published by Doctors Forbes and Rieu, and the chapters and paragraphs follow the arrangement observed there.

The style of the Hindustani translation is considered very pure and elegant, and the language is generally clear and easy to understand <note>original: "perspicuous"</note>, but it contains a very large proportion of Arabic words. The nature of the subject rendered the introduction of many Arabic scientific terms a matter of necessity; however, these foreign words are often employed even when pure local <note>original: "vernacular"</note> words might have been used with more benefit. This, however, is the general fault of these translations.

<summary>The translator discusses previous English, German, and Hindustani versions of the text, noting that his version is based on the Forbes and Rieu edition and aims for greater literal accuracy than its predecessors.</summary>
<keywords>T. P. Manuel, Professor Dieterici, Professor Flügel, Hindustani translation, Arabic scientific terms, Forbes and Rieu edition, Journal of the German Oriental Society</keywords>

# ORIGINAL PREFACE.

---

->IN THE NAME OF GOD, THE MOST MERCIFUL.<-
<note>This is the English translation of the "Basmala" (*Bismillah ar-Rahman ar-Rahim*), the traditional opening invocation used in Islamic texts.</note>

Infinite praise is due to the Self-Existent One <note>original: "Self-existent"; a philosophical term for God as the being who exists by His own nature, independent of any cause</note> who, despite the shared nature of physical matter, bestowed various forms upon all possible bodies. Having distributed the human race into various categories and species, He gave distinct powers to every individual. Boundless thanks are owed to that Creator, who brought the human species from the secret storehouse of non-existence into the vast expanse of life, and granted humanity the honor of superiority over all other creatures. Having adorned the human race with the jewel of speech, He clothed us in the garment of knowledge. What ability does a poor, weak human possess to express gratitude for these blessings? What power does an imperfect <note>original: "broken"</note> pen have to fulfill this obligation in writing?

->POETIC VERSES<-
<note>original: "COUPLETS"</note>

Ah! How shall we offer Him praise when even the tongues of the prophets have proven inadequate? Here, all people who appreciate the feast of His divine wisdom can only exclaim—"Who can truly know You?" How has a mere mortal obtained the—

<vocab>God, Self-Existent, non-existence, speech, knowledge, prophets, poetic verses</vocab>

<summary>The preface begins with a formal hymn of praise to the Creator, thanking God for the gifts of existence, speech, and knowledge that distinguish humanity from other creatures. The author humbles himself, suggesting that even prophets and the finest writing cannot fully express the magnitude of divine glory.</summary>

# CHAPTER I.

*On the beginning of the creation of the human race <note>original footnote: Sons of Adam</note>—on their conflict with the animals—on their seeking justice in the presence of the wise King Biwárásb, and on that sage summoning the men before him.*

Writers have recorded the circumstances of the first appearance of the human race in this way: As long as humans were few in number, they would run away and hide in caves out of fear of the animals; they took refuge in the hills and mountains because they were terrified of wild beasts. There was so little security that not even three or four people could gather to practice agriculture and find food. There was certainly no possibility of them weaving clothes or covering their bodies. In short, they ate whatever fruits and vegetables of the jungle they could find, and they covered their bodies with the leaves of trees. In cold weather, they lived in warm places, and during the heat of summer, they chose to live in cooler districts.

When a short period had passed in that condition, and their

<keywords>human origins, survival, King Biwárásb, wild animals, primitive life, Sons of Adam</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the history of the early human race, transitioning from their primitive survival to the establishment of civilization and their subsequent mastery—and mistreatment—of the animal kingdom.</meta>

-># Oppression of the Animals<-

Once their offspring had multiplied, the anxiety about animals and wild beasts <note>original: *Dám o dad*—harmless and predatory beasts</note> which had filled everyone’s hearts entirely disappeared. Afterward, they founded many forts, cities, villages, and towns, and began to live there in comfort. They prepared the tools for carrying out agriculture, and every person engaged in their own occupation. By catching animals in snares, they began using them for riding and carrying heavy loads, and for farming and cultivation. 

The elephants and horses, camels and donkeys—and many other animals that used to roam freely through the woods and wilderness like uncontrolled camels <note>original: *Shutur i bemahár*—A camel without a nose-ring for control; used here to mean "independent" or "refractory"</note>—grazing on fresh green plants wherever they wished without anyone interfering with them, now had their shoulders rubbed raw by their labor day and night. Sores formed on their backs, and although they screamed and cried out in pain, when did these human masters ever listen to them? 

Fearing capture, many animals fled into remote deserts and forests. The birds also abandoned their nesting places, took their young with them, and flew away from the lands of men. Every man imagined that the animals were his slaves, and they used all sorts of tricks and traps to hunt them down.

This conflict lasted for a long time, until God Almighty sent Muhammad Mustafa, the last of the Prophets (may the peace and blessings of God be upon him!) <note>original: "peace of God and rest be upon him"; a translation of the Arabic honorific *Sallallahu Alayhi Wa-Sallam*</note> to guide humanity. The true Prophet showed all sinners the path of righteousness. Many Jinn <note>original: Jins; supernatural spirits in Islamic tradition</note> also received the blessing of the true faith and the honor of Islam. After another age had passed, Biwarasb, the sage of the Jinn,

<keywords>domestication, animal cruelty, agriculture, Prophet Muhammad, Jinn, Biwarasb, Islam, civilization</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the story of the conflict between humans and animals. After fleeing human settlements, the animals have found refuge in the kingdom of the Jins, only for a group of shipwrecked humans to arrive and begin the cycle of exploitation once more.</meta>

# The King of the Jins

Whose name <note>original: "cognomen"</note> was “Brave King,” became the monarch of the Jins <note>Supernatural spirits in Islamic mythology, often known as genies</note>. He was so just that during his reign the tiger and the goat used to drink water at the same watering place <term>ghāt</term>—what possibility could there be then of any swindler <term>thag</term>, thief, cheat, or rogue living in his dominions? The island called Balásághún, which is situated near the equator, was the capital <term>Takht-gáh</term>—literally "throne-place"—of that just king.

It happened that a ship carrying men, being in distress due to unfavorable winds, reached the shore of that island. All the merchants and learned men who were in the ship landed and began to explore the country. They saw that it was a place of wondrous beauty; flowers and fruits of all kinds were hanging on the trees; streams were flowing on every side; the animals grazing and browsing on the lush green grass were fat and healthy, and were playing together. The climate of that place was exceedingly fine, and the land was abundantly fertile; so no one truly desired to leave. In short, after building various kinds of houses, they began to live on that island. By catching the animals in traps, as was their custom, they went about their respective jobs.

When the animals realized that there was no safety for them even there, they fled to the desert. The men still believed that all these animals were their slaves; so, making various kinds of traps according to their old habits, they were determined to capture them. When the animals realized the humans held this arrogant assumption, they gathered their leaders, appeared in the Hall of Justice, and told King Bíwarásb the full story of the oppression they had suffered at the hands of man. When the King had heard all the animals' details, he instantly issued a command, saying, “Very well! Let messengers be sent quickly, and let them bring the men—”

<summary>A group of shipwrecked humans settles on the island of the Jins, where they immediately begin trapping the local animals. Seeking relief from this renewed oppression, the animals petition King Bíwarásb for justice.</summary>
<keywords>King Bíwarásb, Jins, Balásághún, animal rights, human oppression, Hall of Justice, colonization</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the shipwrecked humans who have traveled to the court of the King of the Jins to settle their dispute with the animal kingdom.</meta>

## Appeal to the King

"...into my presence." Accordingly, seventy of them—inhabitants of various cities who were exceptionally eloquent and well-spoken—immediately answered the King’s summons. A very fine house was provided for their stay. After two or three days, once the exhaustion of their journey had faded, he summoned them to his presence. When they saw the King on his throne, they offered their blessings, bowed respectfully, and took their places in their assigned ranks.

This King was exceptionally just and righteous; in his liberality and generosity, he surpassed all his peers and equals. The poor and needy of that time who came to him received support. Nowhere in his territory could any bully or tyrant oppress a person of lower status. Acts forbidden by Law were entirely absent <note>original: "rose up, went away"</note> from his reign. Nothing was ever dearer to his heart than pleasing and satisfying God. He questioned the men with great politeness, saying, "Why have you come into my kingdom? There has never been any correspondence between us; what has caused you to travel so far?"

One among them, who was worldly and experienced <note>original: "seen the world"</note> and eloquent, bowed and began to speak, saying, "We have heard of the King’s justice and fairness, and so we have come into his presence. To this day, no seeker of justice has ever left this seat of prosperity disappointed. Our hope is that the King will grant us redress." 

The King asked, "What is your desire?" 

He replied humbly, "O just King! These animals are our slaves. Some of them are rebellious <term>recalcitrant</term>, and others, though they are forced to submit, still resent our authority." 

The King inquired, "Is there any proof of this claim? For a claim without evidence is not heard in a court of justice."

<summary>Seventy eloquent human representatives appear before the King of the Jins to claim that animals are their rightful slaves. The King, noted for his extreme justice, demands evidence to support their claim of ownership.</summary>
<keywords>King of the Jins, justice, human-animal conflict, slavery, legal evidence, eloquence</keywords>

# The Appeal of the Men

He said, “O King, there are many logical and traditional proofs for this claim.” The King replied, “State them.” A man among them, who was of the lineage of the Honorable Abbas <note>original: Hazrat 'Abbás (the uncle of the Prophet Muhammad); may God be pleased with him!</note>, then stepped up to the podium and began to speak this oration with eloquence and fluency:

“Praise is due to that true Creator who has prepared so much to sustain the world and has placed so many things upon the expanse <note>original: 'Arsa—area, plain, or expanse</note> of the earth. For the sake of weak <note>original: Za'ífu-l bunyán—frail or weak in constitution</note> humanity, He has created such a variety of animals. Happy is the state of those who, by pleasing Him, are traveling the path to the afterlife. What shall be said of those people who, through disobedience, have unrighteously turned away from Him? 

"And blessings without limit are due to the True Prophet, Muhammad the Chosen, whom God Almighty sent after all the other prophets to guide humanity, and whom He made the leader of them all. He alone is the king of all Jinn <term>supernatural spirits in Islamic mythology</term> and men, and the support and refuge for everyone on the Day of Judgment. Benedictions and peace be upon his pure <note>original: Pák—pure or holy</note> offspring, through whom religion and the world were regulated, and through whom the Islamic faith was spread <note>original: Riwáj páya—obtained currency or became widespread</note>.

“In short, thanks are always due to that incomparable Creator who made Adam from a single drop of water and, by His omnipotence, made him the father of children. Having formed Eve from him, He populated the earth with thousands of human beings and gave humanity a rank above all created things. He made man the ruler over all the land and sea and fed him with all kinds of pure food. Accordingly, He Himself has said in the Quran <note>This refers to an Arabic passage meaning:</note> ‘All animals have been created for you; take your..."

-># Reply of the Mule<-

“...benefit from them, eat them, and make warm clothing from their skins and hair; in the morning send them to the pasture, and in the evening bring them back again to their shelters; they are a source of beauty and ornament for you.” <note>original: Qur'an, Surah 16:5-6</note> And in another place he has said: “On the dry land and on the sea, ride upon camels and in ships.” <note>original: Qur'an, Surah 23:22</note> And in another place he has said: “Horses, mules, and donkeys were created so that you might ride on them;” <note>original: Qur'an, Surah 16:8</note> and again he has said: “Ride upon their backs, and remember the blessings of God.” <note>original: Qur'an, Surah 43:12-13</note> Besides this, many other verses of the Qur'an have been revealed on this subject, and it is also understood from the Bible and the New Testament that animals were made for our sake. In every way, we are their masters and they are our slaves.”

The King then turned toward the animals and said, “This man has brought forward verses of the Qur'an to support his claim. In reply to this, speak whatever comes to your minds.” 

Having heard this, the Mule, with a ready tongue, delivered this speech:

“Praise is due to the majesty of the One, holy, ancient of days, and free from want, who existed before the foundation of the world, not confined by time nor by place. By the utterance of a single ‘BE,’ <note>original: Kun—the divine command of creation in Islamic theology</note> He made all creatures manifest from behind the veil of obscurity <note>original: Chaos</note>. Having fashioned the sky from water and fire, He gave it the dignity of loftiness. Having brought forth the race of Adam from a drop of water, He sent them here and there in the world so that they might engage in its cultivation, but not to ruin it. They were meant to protect the animals in a proper manner and benefit from them, not to tyrannize over them and oppress them.”

After this, the Mule proceeded to say, “It is not to be understood...”

## CHAPTER II.

->*Describing the efforts made by the King of the Jinn to resolve the dispute between Humans and Animals.*<-

When the King had heard the animals' account, he became determined to settle the dispute. He ordered the judges, lawyers, and all the high-ranking officials and nobles of the Jinn to attend. Following his order, they all immediately gathered at the royal court. Then the King said to the humans, "The animals have fully presented their account and their complaints about your tyranny. What answer do you have for them now?"

One man from among them bowed respectfully and began his statement: "O Refuge of the world! <note>original: "Asylum of the world," a formal title for a monarch indicating he is a protector of all people.</note> These animals are all our slaves, and we are their masters. It is right for us to exercise authority over them and to demand whatever service we require from them. Whoever among them has agreed to obey us is accepted by God, and whoever has rejected our authority has, in effect, turned away from God."

The King replied, "A claim without evidence is not heard in a court of justice. Provide some title-deed or proof."

The man said, "Our claim is established by many <term>rational and traditional proofs</term>." <note>In the philosophical context of this text, "rational proofs" refer to logical arguments, while "traditional proofs" refer to evidence from scripture or historical tradition.</note>

The King asked, "What are these proofs?"

Then the man continued, "Consider the perfection <note>original literal meaning: "purity"</note> with which God Almighty has formed our bodies, making every part of us..."

<summary>The King of the Jinn convenes a formal court to hear the dispute between humans and animals. A human representative argues that humans are the divinely appointed masters of animals, citing their physical perfection and scriptural authority as evidence.</summary>

# Pleadings.

[Page 16]

...was made beautiful, his posture upright, his hands and feet excellent, and all with the most perfect elegance.” Yet one more meaning is apparent in this verse: <note>original Arabic: *Alladhī khalaqaka fasawwāka faʿadalaka*—"Who created you, fashioned you, and perfected your design."</note> “God Almighty created man in the happy medium <term>Hadd i itidál: a state of perfect balance or moderation</term>—He did not make him very tall nor very short.” <note>Koran, Sura 82, verse 7.</note>

The King said this degree of symmetry and the suitability of the limbs is enough to establish superiority. The animals replied, “This is also the case with us. God Almighty gave every one of us limbs in the balanced proportions that were suitable; in this superiority, we and humans are equal.” The men replied, “Where is this suitability of limbs among you? Your figures are extremely disgusting, your posture clumsy, and your hands and feet ugly. Consider the camel: it has a large body, a long neck, and a tiny tail. And there is the elephant, whose form is very large and heavy, with two long tusks sticking out from its mouth, very broad ears, and very small eyes. Bulls and buffaloes have large tails, thick horns, and no upper teeth. The horns of the fat-tailed sheep <term>dumba: a Persian word for a breed of sheep known for its large, fatty tail</term> are heavy and its hindquarters are fat. There is the goat, which has a long beard and no hindquarters. The hare is small in size but has large ears. In this way, there are many meat-eating and grass-eating <term>graminivorous: an animal that feeds on grass</term> beasts, and birds whose form and posture are clumsy, where one limb has no harmony with the others.”

Upon hearing this speech, one animal began to say, “Alas! You have not understood the Divine art at all. We are His creatures; the beauty and perfection of our limbs come from Him. Therefore, to find faults in us is, in fact, to point out His deficiencies. Do you not know that God Almighty in His wisdom created each individual thing for some specific good? No one, however, knows this mystery, except for Him and people of science.”

That man said, “If you are a wise person <term>sage: a profoundly wise person</term> among the animals...”

<summary>In this passage, humans argue that their physical symmetry proves their superiority over animals, while the animals defend their various forms as purposeful products of Divine wisdom.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the debate between humans and animals before a King. An animal representative explains the divine wisdom behind the physical anatomy of various creatures, refuting the human claim that their forms are "clumsy" or "ugly."</meta>

# Pleadings.

“...then you must explain what advantage there is in the length of the camel’s neck.” 

He replied, “Because its legs were long; therefore, if its neck had been short, it would have been difficult for it to graze on grass. For this reason, He made its neck long so that it might graze with ease, lift itself up from the ground using the strength of that neck, and be able to reach its lips over its entire body to rub and groom itself. In the same way, He has given the elephant a long trunk instead of a neck, and He has made its ears large so that it may drive away flies and mosquitoes, preventing them from getting into its eyes or mouth; for because of its tusks, its mouth always remains open and cannot shut. Furthermore, its tusks are long so that it may defend itself against the ferocity of predatory <note>original: "rapacious"</note> animals. As for the hare, its ears are long because its body is very delicate <note>original: "tender"</note> and its skin is thin—it can wrap those ears around itself in cold weather and spread them out in the heat.

“In short, God Almighty has granted every animal the body parts He deemed suitable. Thus, He spoke through the holy Moses, saying, ‘God gave a unique form <term>Khilkat: literally "creation," "form," or "outward appearance"</term> to every thing, and after that, He prescribed their respective ways of life.’ <note>Original Arabic from the Quran, Sura 20, Verse 50: "Our Lord is He who gave to each thing its form and then guided it."</note> The meaning of this is that He gave to every creature the limbs that were appropriate for them and showed them the path of righteousness.

“What you consider beauty and boast about—and when, in your vanity, you think, ‘We are the masters and they are the slaves’—is entirely a mistake. Beauty, in every species, is simply that which makes its members attractive to their own kind, and through which they show affection to each other; and...”

<summary>The animals defend their physical forms as divinely designed for survival and utility, arguing that beauty is subjective to each species and that God provides every creature with exactly what it needs.</summary>

# Pleadings.

...this is the cause of procreation and the continuation <note>original: "propagation"</note> of offspring. For the beauty of one species is not appealing <note>original: "agreeable"</note> to another species. Every animal fixes its heart only upon a female of its own species, and does not desire the female of any other animal, however much "better" she may be than its own. In the same way, humans also have an affection for their own particular species. Those people who are black are not attracted to people with white bodies, and those who are white do not set their hearts upon those who are black. Therefore, your beauty is no ground for superiority, nor should you consider yourselves to excel us.

> “And when you say that you have great sharpness <note>original: "acuteness"</note> of sense, that is also a mistake. Many animals have better understanding and senses than you. For example, there is the camel, who has long legs and a long neck, and whose head reaches high into the air <note>original: "contends with the air"</note>; yet despite this, on dark nights he discerns <note>original: "descries"</note> the place on which to set his feet and travels along roads that seem impassable, while you require torches and lamps. And the horse hears the footsteps <note>original: "tramp"</note> of a traveler from far away; it has often happened that, having heard the steps of an enemy <note>original: "adversary"</note>, he has alerted <note>original: "aroused"</note> his rider and saved him from his foe. If a person takes an ox or a donkey <note>original: "ass"</note> even once along an unfamiliar <note>original: "unseen"</note> road and then sets it loose, it escapes from that place and returns safely to its own home; it does not forget the way at all. But if you have traveled a road several times, whenever you need to travel it again, you are still confused <note>original: "bewildered"</note> and forgetful. Sheep and goats give birth to hundreds of young in a single night, and in the morning they go out to the pasture; when they return from there in the evening, the young ones individually <note>original: "severally"</note> recognize their mothers, and the mothers recognize their young. But if one of you stays away for some time and then returns home, he forgets his mother and sister, father and brother. Where then is the judgment and sharpness of sense about which you boast so much?”

## CHAPTER IV.

->*Presenting the grievance which every animal brought forward individually against humanity.*<-

The King, turning toward the men, said, “You have heard his answer; now say whatever else remains for you to say.” They replied, “There are still many proofs remaining by which our claim is established. These are some of them: buying and selling animals, feeding them and giving them water, providing them with coverings <note>original: "putting garments on them"</note>, protecting them from cold and heat, overlooking their faults, saving them from the violence <note>original: "ferocity"</note> of wild beasts, and tenderly giving them medicine when they are sick. We show them these kindnesses <note>original: "Sulúk"; meaning treatment or conduct, often used to imply kind treatment</note> with the intent <note>original: "Ba-nazr"</note> of compassion and mercy. The same practice prevails among all masters, who keep an eye of compassion and mercy upon their slaves.”

Having heard this, the King ordered the animal to give him an answer, and he said, “This man claims that they buy and sell animals, but this practice also exists among humans. For instance, the people of Persia, when they win a victory over Turkey <note>original: "Rúm"; historically referring to Rome, the Byzantine Empire, or the Ottoman Empire/Asia Minor</note>, sell the Turks as slaves. When the Turks prevail over Persia, they treat the Persians in the same way. The people of India follow the same practice toward the—”

<summary>The humans argue that their care for and commerce in animals proves their rightful ownership, likening it to the relationship between a master and a slave. The animal representative counters by pointing out that humans also buy, sell, and enslave one another during wars, suggesting that commerce does not prove natural superiority.</summary>

## Complaint of the Camel, and others

...troubles; never do they listen to the cries and wails of these injured animals. There, without giving them grain or water, they slaughter them, tear off their skins, rip open their bellies, break open their skulls, tear out their livers, carry them to the butchers’ shops, and cut them up with knives, stick them on spits, and bake them in ovens. We, seeing all these evils, remain silent and say nothing.”

The camel said, “When we become captives in their hands, this is our state: having put ropes through our noses, the camel-drivers pull on them. Then, placing very heavy loads upon our backs, they take us on dark nights along hilly and mountainous roads. In short, our backs are rubbed raw <term>original: "galled"; skin sores caused by rubbing</term> by the jolting of the saddles, and the soles of our feet are wounded by the stones. Hungry and thirsty, they drive us wherever their heart desires, and we poor wretches, being helpless, submit to them.”

The elephant said, “When we become their captives, they put ropes around our necks and chains on our feet. Taking an iron goad <note>original: "goad of iron"; a spiked tool used to prod or guide large animals</note> in their hands, they beat us on the right side, on the left, and on the head.”

The horse said, “When we are in bondage to them, they put bits in our mouths, saddles on our backs, and cinches <note>original: "girths"; the straps that hold a saddle in place</note> around our waists. Wearing their mail and armor, they ride us into battlefields. Hungry and thirsty, our eyes filled with dirt and dust, we go into the battle; there we receive sword-cuts on our faces, and javelins and arrows in our chests, and we swim in a sea of blood.”

The mule said, “When we become subject to them in slavery, we endure the most astounding hardships. They fasten ropes to our feet and put bridles and bits into our mouths. They do not release us for one instant, even so that we may go to our mates and satisfy our natural desires. The grooms and stablemen put pack-saddles on our backs and ride us.”

# The Pig

"...plant-eating <term>original: herbivorous</term> but to the meat-eating <term>original: carnivorous</term> animals. Do you not know that his teeth protrude, and that he also eats rotting flesh <note>original: carrion</note>?" The other replied, "He is a plant-eater, for he has hooves and he also eats grass." A third said he is both meat-eating and plant-eating, and is related to cattle in the same way that the giraffe <note>original: camelopard</note> is related to the bull, camel, and leopard; he is also like the ostrich, whose form blends the features of a bird and a camel.

The pig said to the camel, "I know nothing about it; what can I say, or to whom shall I complain? There is a great deal of variety in the way humans treat me. Muslims <note>original: Musulmans</note> consider us deformed and accursed; they think our appearance is detestable and our flesh unclean, and they even avoid mentioning us. The Greeks in Turkey <note>original: Turkish Greeks</note> eat our flesh with great eagerness <note>original: avidity</note>; they consider it holy, and they believe that sacrificing us is a very meritorious act. The Jews have a great aversion and hatred toward us; they abuse and curse us inoffensive animals because they are at odds with the Christians <note>original: Nazarenes</note> and the Greeks. The Armenians value <note>original: esteem</note> us as much as they do oxen and goats; in fact, they prize us even more because of the fatness and thickness of our meat and the large size of our litters <note>original: abundance of our offspring</note>. Greek doctors <note>original: physicians</note> use our fat in many of their remedies; indeed, they even include it in their medicines. 

Herdsmen and stable hands <note>original: grooms</note> keep us in their stables and pastures near their livestock and horses because they believe our presence protects the other animals from many evils. Sorcerers <note>original: Enchanters</note> and magicians cover their books and magical tools with our skins. Saddlers and shoemakers pull out the bristles from our necks and snouts <note>original: whiskers</note> with great eagerness, for these are very useful to them. We are bewildered and have nothing to say; whom should we praise, and whom should we blame?"

When the pig <note>original: hog</note> had finished saying all this, the donkey <note>original: ass</note> looked toward the hare, who was standing near the camel, and said—

# The Hare

Someone said to him, “Recite <note>original: rehearse</note> in the presence of the King all the cruelty <note>original: tyranny</note> which your species has suffered from humans. Perhaps the King, taking pity, will grant freedom <note>original: deliverance</note> from their hands to us captives.” 

The hare said, “We live far away from them; having stopped living in their lands, we have chosen to make our home in valleys and jungles; through this, we are saved from their cruelty <note>original: oppression</note>. But we are terribly troubled by dogs and birds of prey <term>original: *Shikári-jánwar* — hunting animals such as falcons and hawks</term>, who help humans for the purpose of catching us, and lead them toward us. They hand over as captives into the hands of men all the deer, oxen, camels, goats, and plant-eating <term>original: herbivorous</term> animals of our brotherhood <note>original: fraternity</note> who have taken refuge in the hills.”

Then the hare said, “The hunting dogs are excusable in this. They ought to help them, for they also are very fond of eating our flesh, and they do not belong to our species, but on the contrary are meat-eating <term>original: carnivorous</term> animals. But the horse belongs to the grass-eating <term>original: graminivorous</term> animals and does not eat our flesh; why should he assist them, unless it is entirely due to his stupidity and folly?”

### CHAPTER 5.

### *Account of the Horse.*

When the man heard all these words from the hare, he said, “Enough, be silent! You have insulted <note>original: reproached</note> the horse enough. If you only knew that he is the best of all animals and that he is obedient to man, you would not have babbled so foolishly <note>original: stupidly</note>.” 

The King asked the man what made the horse superior, and he answered, “Your Majesty, the horse’s good qualities and excellences are numerous. His form is good, every limb is well-proportioned <note>original: appropriate</note>, his appearance is handsome, his instincts are sound, and his color is fine. He excels in intelligence and is a swift runner. He is obedient to his rider, for he quickly turns right or left, forward or backward, or wherever his rider directs him. 

He does not hesitate <note>original: flinch</note> to gallop, and he is so well trained that while his rider is seated on his back, he neither urinates <term>original: stales — an archaic term for animals urinating</term> nor drops manure <note>original: dungs</note>. If his tail ever gets wet in mud or water, he does not shake it, so that his rider is not splashed. His strength is like an elephant’s, for he trots along carrying his rider—who wears a helmet, chainmail <note>original: mail</note>, and armor—along with his own saddle, bridle, and armor. This is a total load of five hundred maunds <term>original: *maund* — a traditional unit of weight in South Asia, varying by region but often around 80 pounds</term>.¹ 

He is so patient and enduring that on the battlefield, even if he receives wounds from spears and arrows in his chest <note>original: breast</note> and internal organs <note>original: vitals</note>, he remains calm. His speed is such that the wind cannot overtake the dust of his heels. In his...”

In his proud gait he is like a majestic bull, and his leaping is like a leopard’s. If his rider makes a bet, he runs swiftly and brings his rider in first. In what creature besides the horse can all these virtues be found?”

> “He is very foolish <note>original: stupid</note> and ignorant, and does not know friend from foe. If an enemy mounts him <note>original: passes under the thighs of an enemy</note>, he becomes submissive to him. On the battlefield, at the command of this enemy—his new master—he will charge and attack the man in whose house he was born and by whom he was cared for <note>original: nourished</note> all his life. This trait of his is like the nature of a sword; however, a sword is inanimate and unable to distinguish between friend and foe. Just as it cuts its enemy and opponent, so too when it falls upon the neck of its owner or maker, it severs his head from his body without hesitation, knowing no difference between friends and strangers.
> 
> “The same character trait is found in humans, who wage war against their parents, brothers, sisters, and relatives, employing all kinds of tricks and schemes <note>original: stratagems</note>. They treat their friends with the same hostility they should reserve for their enemies. In infancy, they drink the milk of their parents and are nurtured in their embrace <note>original: bosoms</note>, but in early adulthood <note>original: early manhood</note>, they become their enemies. In the same way, they drink the milk of animals and benefit from making clothes out of their skin and hair, but in the end, they slaughter those same animals, strip off their hides, rip open their bellies, and make them taste the heat of the fire. In their barbarity and cruelty, they completely forget the kindnesses <note>original: obligations</note> and benefits they received from them.”

<header>Speech of the Ass.</header>

...is not fitting <note>original: seemly</note>. Who is the person whom God Almighty has blessed with so many virtues and favors, yet has withheld from them one particular favor that surpasses all others? Or who is the person He has denied all these blessings, yet to whom He has given one unique blessing that He has granted to no one else? No one in the world receives every talent and blessing. The gifts of the great, impartial <note>original: disinterested</note> Giver are not limited to a single species. His mercy extends over all His works, but to one He gives much, and to another little. To one He has granted the rank of a lord <term>lordship</term>, yet upon that same person He has also placed the mark of being a subject. 

Consider the dignity He has given to the sun and moon—light, visibility <note>original: conspicuity</note>, greatness, and height. He has granted them all these excellencies and distinctions to such a degree that some ignorant people have even thought them to be gods. Yet, He has not protected them from the weakness <note>original: infirmity</note> of eclipses. This serves as proof for intelligent people; for if they were gods, they would never go dark or fade away <note>original: wane</note>. In the same way, He has given light and brightness to all the stars. But at the same time, He has arranged it so that they are lost in the brilliant radiance <note>original: effulgence</note> of the sun, and they remain in constant motion <note>original: revolution</note> day and night, so that their status as created beings <note>original: marks of the creature</note> may be clear for all to see. 

The condition of the Jinn <term>Jins</term> <note>Supernatural spirits in Islamic and Middle Eastern mythology, created from smokeless fire.</note>, humans, and angels is exactly the same; for even if one of them possesses great talents, there is still some small flaw. Perfection belongs only to God, and to no one else.”

When the ass finished this speech, the bull said, “When God has given someone great blessings that He has not given to others, it is right <note>original: behoves</note> for that person to show gratitude for them—that is, they ought to let others share in those blessings. For example, God Almighty has given light to the sun, and through its light, it brings comfort to all of creation, and

When the bull finished speaking, all the animals gnashed their teeth, wept, and began to say, "O just King, have mercy on us, and grant us deliverance from the tyranny of these oppressors!" When the King heard this, he looked toward all the scholars <note>original: doctors; meaning experts in law or theology</note> and sages of the Jinn <term>Jinn</term> <note>original: Jins; in Islamic theology, supernatural beings created from smokeless fire</note> who were present, and asked if they had heard the animals' account of the tyranny, cruelty, and violence of men.

They replied, "We have heard, and it is all true. We see it by night and by day. Their tyranny is not hidden from any intelligent or sensible person. Because of humanity's bad conduct and evil character, the Jinn have entirely stopped visiting inhabited places. But even with all this, we find no escape from their malice. They are so suspicious and mistrustful of us that if any child, woman, or man is foolish, senseless, or sick, they immediately say that the 'evil eye' or the 'shadow of a Jinn' has fallen upon them. Their hearts are always full of mistrust, and they pray for protection against the malice of the Jinn. Yet, no one has ever seen a Jinn kill a man, wound him, rob him of his clothes, commit a theft, break into anyone's house, pick pockets, slash sleeves <note>original: tear sleeves; a historical pickpocketing technique where a thief would cut the sleeve or hanging pocket of a garment to steal its contents</note>, break the lock of a shop, murder a traveler, rebel against a king, plunder, or take anyone captive. On the contrary, all these habits are found among humans, and they remain focused on one or another of them day and night."

## CHAPTER VI.

### *Consultation between the King and the Wazir.*

When the King left his assembly, he spoke privately to Bedar, his wazir <term>wazir</term> <note>a high-ranking political advisor or minister</note>, saying, “You have heard all the arguments and counter-arguments <note>original: statements and rejoinders</note> of both the men and the animals; what advice do you have to offer now? How should this matter be settled? In your opinion, what is the best course of action?” 

The wazir was a very wise and cautious man. After bowing respectfully and offering his blessings, he said, “I believe it would be better if the King summoned all the judges, lawyers, and wise men of the Jinn <note>original: Jins; supernatural spirits in Islamic theology who, in this story, act as the mediators and judges of the world</note> to his presence to consult with them about the case. This is a serious conflict, and it is not clear which side is in the right. In such matters, consultation is essential, because a solid plan is often decided upon through the combined counsel of three or four people. Wise and far-sighted men should be careful never to engage in such difficult matters without seeking advice and counsel.”

Following this advice, the King ordered all the chiefs and nobles of the Jinn to attend. They all assembled according to their specific expertise: judges of the lineage of Jupiter; lawyers of the lineage of Venus; wise men of the children of Biran; sages of the lineage of Luqman <note>Luqman is a legendary figure in Islamic tradition renowned for his profound wisdom and fables</note>; experienced people of the children of Haman; intelligent people of the children of Kaiwan <note>the Persian name for the planet Saturn, traditionally associated with deep intellect and discipline</note>; and people of common sense of the children of Bahram <note>the Persian name for the planet Mars</note>. The King addressed them, saying, “These humans and beasts have come

A learned sage from the lineage of Venus <note>In this context, the Jinn are classified by astrological lineages; those of Venus were traditionally associated with law and beauty.</note> who was present said, "In my opinion, the proper course of action is for the animals to write out a statement of their circumstances and the oppression they have endured at the hands of men. If a means of deliverance can be settled for them, the judges and lawyers will issue a decree stating whether the men must sell them, set them free, or show some kindness and lessen their harsh treatment. If the men do not obey this decision of the judges and the animals then flee from their tyranny, it will be no fault or sin of theirs."

When the King heard this, he asked the council what they had to say about it. They all replied that it was an excellent proposal and well-suited for the occasion. However, the **Sahib-i 'azimat** <term>Sahib-i 'azimat</term> <note>original: "a resolute or practical person." The lawyer said, "The King."

Upon hearing this, the advisor asked where the King could get so much money. The lawyer replied that it would be paid from the public treasury. The **Sahib-i 'azimat** then asked, "Is there enough wealth in the public treasury to cover the price of all of them? Furthermore, some men may refuse to sell them. They have a great need for these animals and do not care about the money. For example, there are kings, ministers, and many gentlemen who cannot move about without an entourage <term>equipage</term> <note>original: a carriage and horses with attendants</note>. They will never consent to sell them and will oppose this decree."

# CHAPTER 7.

## *The Conflicts Between Humans and the Jinn.*

In obedience to the King's orders, the sage set out the facts of the matter:

In the earliest age, before God had created Adam, the **Jinn** <term>Jinn</term> <note>original: "Jins"; in Middle Eastern and Islamic mythology, these are supernatural beings created from smokeless fire, often possessing free will like humans.</note> lived across the entire face of the earth—the woods, fields, and waters were all under their rule. As many days passed, they received prophecy and law, religion and authority <note>original: "dominion"</note>, and numerous blessings. But they began to show disobedience and error; they ignored the testaments and teachings <note>original: "precepts"</note> of the prophets and spread wickedness across the entire earth. Because of their tyranny, the earth and all those living upon it went complaining to the court of God, making their pleas and lamentations.

When another age had passed and their hostility <note>original: "animosity"</note> and tyranny increased daily, God Almighty sent an army of angels down to the earth. They arrived and, having defeated the **Jinn**, expelled them, taking many as prisoners and captives. The angels then began to live upon the earth. 

It so happened that **Azazel** <note>original: "’Azázíl"; in Islamic tradition, this was the original name of Iblis (Satan) before his fall from grace.</note>—the accursed devil who deceived the patriarchs Adam and Eve—was among those captives. At that time he was very young and knew nothing. He was raised among those angels and adopted all their rules and customs. When he had acquired their knowledge <note>original: "science"</note> and reached maturity, he was made the head and chief of the tribe, and he used to issue his decrees <note>original: "edicts"</note> of command and prohibition.

<summary>The sage recounts the pre-Adamic history of the Jinn, explaining how their wickedness led God to send an army of angels to expel them from Earth and how the young captive Azazel rose to lead them.</summary>
<keywords>Jinn, Azazel, angels, creation myth, disobedience, Adam and Eve, ancient history</keywords>

# Adam in Paradise.

When another age had passed after those events, God Almighty said to the angels who were living on the earth, "I will create a ruler for the earth who is not one of you, and I will call you up to heaven." The angels, who had lived here for a long time, considered this a most unpleasant command because they would have to depart. They replied to God, "Will our Lord create a person who will cause evil and bloodshed on the earth, just as the **Jinn** <term>Jinn</term> <note>original: "Jins"; supernatural beings in Islamic mythology created from smokeless fire.</note> were doing, while we praise You and honor You as holy?" <note>Quran, Surah 2, verse 28.</note> God Almighty said, "I know of benefits that you know nothing about <note>Quran, Surah 2, verse 28.</note>, and I have sworn an oath to myself not to keep any angel, Jinn, or animal on the earth after Adam and his offspring."

Ultimately, when God had created Adam and breathed a soul into his body, and when He formed Eve from him, He commanded all the angels to gather and pay him homage. In obedience to the divine command, they paid him homage and became subject to Adam. But **Azazel** <note>original: "’Azázíl"; the name of Iblis (Satan) before his fall.</note> did not bow down; through his stubbornness and envy, he opposed the commands of God. He thought to himself, "Previously I was the leader and master; shall I now become his subject?" So, through envy and malice, he became the enemy of Adam.

God Almighty then directed the angels to bring Adam into the Garden of Eden. When Adam entered Paradise, a command was given by the Divine Majesty <note>This refers to an Arabic verse from the Quran, Surah 2, verse 33</note>: "O Adam, dwell with your wife in this garden and eat whatever your heart desires for your pleasure; only do not go near this tree, for if you do go near it, you will be a sinner." This Paradise, which God Almighty gave to the patriarch Adam as a home, is a garden toward the East, on the Ruby Mountain. No one has the strength to climb it. The land in that place is good, the air is mild <note>original: "temperate"</note>, and the days of spring there are eternal;

Many streams flow there; the trees are very green, fruits ripen in abundance, and all kinds of flowers bloom. The animals there do not hurt anyone, and beautiful birds of sweet song, and of all kinds, sit upon the branches and keep up a continual warbling.

Adam and Eve went there and began to live in comfort. The hair of their heads was very long and hung down to their feet. Their entire bodies were covered with hair, and because of this, their beauty and loveliness were very great. <note>In some historical and Islamic traditions, it was believed that Adam and Eve were originally covered in a protective layer of light or hair that was lost after the Fall.</note> They used to stroll at their pleasure among the flowerbeds upon the banks of the streams, eating all kinds of fruits and drinking water from the rivers. Everything was attainable without toil or labor. Plowing, cultivating, grinding grain, baking, spinning, weaving clothes, and washing—not one of these troubles was known to them, unlike the way their descendants are involved in these hardships today. Just as other animals lived there, these two passed their days in full security and comfort. There was no sorrow. 

The Lord Almighty made known to Adam the names of all the plants and animals that were there. When the angels were asked their names, they did not know; being confounded, they held their peace. <note>This refers to the Quranic account (Surah 2:31-33) where God demonstrates Adam’s superior intellect to the angels by teaching him the names of all things.</note> When Adam was asked, he instantly told the names of all of them and set forth the uses and defects of every one. When the angels saw this fact, they all became subject to him and knew Adam to be better than themselves.

When **Azazel** <note>original: "’Azázíl"; the name of Iblis (Satan) before his fall from grace.</note> saw this dignity of Adam, his malice and envy increased even more, and he meditated on how he might in some way ruin him through fraud and trickery. So one day, pretending to be a friendly counselor, he went to them and said, “God Almighty has bestowed upon you the great distinction of eloquence and speech, a blessing which He has never yet given to anyone else. If you eat a little from this tree, then your knowledge and excellence will be greatly increased, and you will live here forever in perfect ease and comfort; death will...”

# Fall of Adam.

“...will never come, and you will always enjoy happiness.” In that hour when that accursed one <note>original: "accursed one" refers to Iblis or Satan</note>, having sworn an oath, said, “I advise you well,”¹ they fell into his snare. With greed, they transgressed and ate from that tree which God Almighty had forbidden them to touch. The celestial garments they were wearing instantly fell from their bodies. Taking leaves from the trees, they began to cover themselves. The long hair upon their heads also fell off, and they became naked. From the heat of the sun, their complexions changed and became dark. In short, they were ruined.

When the animals saw them in this miserable state, they felt a physical dislike for them and fled in disgust. Adam and Eve fell into a most wretched condition. The angels then received an order to expel them from Paradise and cast them down from the mountain. The angels placed them in a spot where there were no fruits or leaves at all. Somehow or other they arrived upon the earth, and for a long time they wept incessantly in grief and sorrow, feeling great shame for their conduct. When a long period had passed in this sorrow and remorse, God Almighty took compassion on them; accepting their repentance, He forgave their sin. He sent an angel down to the earth, who came and taught them how to dig the earth, to plow and sow, to reap, grind grain, make leaven <term>leaven: a substance like yeast used to make bread rise</term>, and bake bread, as well as how to weave cloth and sew garments.

When their descendants became numerous, the Jins <term>Jins: also known as Jinns or Genies; in Islamic tradition, supernatural beings created from smokeless fire</term> also came and associated with them. They taught the humans how to plant trees, build houses, and practice many useful arts. Friendships sprang up between them, and for a long time they lived their lives this way. But whenever the fraud and treachery of the accursed devil was mentioned, every person felt a sense of malice and envy coming from the Jins. When Cain murdered Abel, a suspicion sprang up...

# Solomon

...among the children of the latter that the Jins had incited him. Because of this, their hostility and hatred toward the Jins increased even more. In their bitterness, they practiced and were constantly focused on magic, incantations, curses <note>original: "imprecations"</note>, charms, imprisoning them in bottles, and many other dark arts¹ by which the Jins might be harassed.

When God Almighty sent the prophet Enoch, he came to this place, and having established peace between humans and Jins, he showed all of them the way of peace. The Jins entered the territories of the men, and by socializing with them, they lived together. In this way, matters went on well until the Great Flood <note>original: "second deluge"</note>, and even after that, until the time of the patriarch Abraham, the friend of God. When Nimrod threw Abraham into the fire, a suspicion arose in the minds of men that the Jins had taught Nimrod how to build the catapult <note>original: "sling"; in the legend of Nimrod and Abraham, a giant engine or catapult was used to launch Abraham into the fire</note>. And when Joseph’s brothers threw him into the pit, they thought that this was also caused by the trickery <note>original: "guile"</note> of the Jins. When the prophet Moses came into the world, he also brokered peace between them, and many Jins became followers of his religion.

When Solomon, the son of David, was made king of the whole world by Almighty God, and authority over all the kings of the earth was given to him, all the Jins and men became his subjects. Then the Jins boastfully claimed that Solomon had gained his empire with their assistance; they argued that if the Jins had not helped him, he would have been just like any other king. They were always pretending to have a knowledge of the secrets of fate, which caused fear among men. However, when His Highness Solomon died, the Jins had no knowledge of it; they were all in a state of confusion, asking what had become of Solomon. The men then felt certain that if the Jins truly were seers <term>seers: people who can see the future or perceive hidden supernatural truths</term>, they would not have been so bewildered.

# Queen of Sheba

When news about Bilqis (the Queen of Sheba) reached Solomon through the hoopoe bird <note>The hoopoe is traditionally depicted in Islamic and Jewish lore as Solomon's messenger</note>, he asked everyone if there was anyone who could bring Bilqis's throne to him before her arrival. A Jinn <note>original: "Jin"</note> named Astus, son of Aiwan, boastfully said that he would bring it so quickly that His Majesty would not even have time to rise from his seat. Solomon said that he wanted it done even faster. Asaf, son of Barkhiya <note>Solomon’s vizier, often credited in tradition with knowing the secret name of God</note>, who knew the "Word of Power," said that he would bring it in the blink of an eye. And indeed, he brought it. 

When Solomon saw the throne, he was amazed and worshiped God. It then became clear to the Jinn that humans were superior to them. Ashamed and humiliated, the Jinn turned away, and all the men followed them, mocking and hooting at them. Greatly degraded, the Jinn fled and became rebellious. Solomon sent an army after them to capture them. He taught many hidden <note>original: "occult"</note> arts for taking them prisoner and gave instructions on how to shut the Jinn up in bottles <note>This refers to the "Seal of Solomon" legends where genies are trapped in vessels</note>. In fact, he wrote a book on those mystical practices, which became public after his death.

When the Lord Jesus came into the world and offered the invitation to Islam <note>In this historical context, "Islam" refers to the universal submission to God's will as taught by all prophets</note> to all Jinn and humans, he taught everyone the way of salvation; he showed the way to heaven and how to achieve fellowship with the angels. Many Jinn then joined his religion, and by becoming devout and disciplined, they began to ascend to heaven. Hearing news of heaven, they returned to earth and shared what they heard with magicians.

When the Almighty created the last of the prophets <note>Muhammad</note>, the Jinn were prevented from entering heaven. They said:

> "We do not know whether this misfortune <note>original: "calamity"</note> has fallen upon us for the good of those who dwell on the earth, or whether God desires to provide a way of salvation for those earthly beings."¹

Many Jinn approved of the Muhammadan faith...

# Uncertainty of Fortune

...the race of the Sassanians <note>A major Persian dynasty (224–651 AD)</note> and the race of 'Adnán <note>The traditional ancestor of the North Arabian tribes, including the Prophet Muhammad</note> from the tyranny of the Greeks and the family of Ardashír <note>Ardashir I, the founder of the Sassanian Empire</note>. Fortune <note>Fate or destiny personified</note> does not treat anyone in a consistent way. Like the rotation of the celestial spheres, it turns perpetually over this world of created beings in accordance with divine commands. 

Whether it occurs once in a thousand years, or in twelve thousand years, or in thirty-six thousand years, or in three hundred and sixty thousand years, or even in a single day (which may be equal to fifty-five thousand years <note>A reference to the immense scale of divine time, often found in Islamic theology regarding the Day of Judgment</note>), it eventually changes. It is certainly true that the magic of this ever-changing fortune never keeps anyone in the same state forever.

# CHAPTER 8.

### *Consultation of the Men.*

While the King was consulting in private with his minister <note>original: wazír</note>, advisors, and nobles, the seventy men—citizens representing various cities—gathered in their own quarters to deliberate together. Everyone shared what was on his mind. One of them said, “You have all heard the debate that took place today between us and our slaves <note>In this allegory, "slaves" refers to the animals who are arguing for their rights against human ownership</note>, and the dispute is not yet settled. Has anyone discovered what the King has decided regarding our case?” Everyone replied, “How would we know? However, we do know this: the King is very troubled by the matter and will probably not appear in court tomorrow.” Another said, “I have found out that he will hold a private consultation with his minister tomorrow specifically about our case.” Another added, “He will assemble the scholars <note>original: doctors</note> and learned men to take their counsel.”

One of the men said, “We do not know what advice the scholars will give regarding our rights, but we do know that the King is inclined to favor us and has a good opinion of us.” Another said, “The minister is the one to be feared. I hope he will not turn against us and damage our legal standing.” Another suggested, “This is an easy matter to solve. We should give the minister some gifts to bring him over to our side. Yet, there is still one danger.” They all asked what that was, and he replied that there was a great deal to fear regarding the opinions of the judges and the legal experts.

# CHAPTER IX.

## *Consultation of the Animals.*

When the King rose from his council and everyone was dismissed, they all went to their respective homes. The animals also gathered and began to consult one another. One of them said, “You have heard the entire debate <note>original: "disputation"</note> that has taken place today between us and the humans, and the dispute is not yet settled. Now, what do you think is the best course of action?” 

One of them replied, “We will go tomorrow morning and weep before the King, complaining of the tyranny of the humans. Perhaps His Majesty may take compassion upon us and release us from bondage. Today, indeed, he was somewhat kind to us; however, it is not right for a king to issue a decree without hearing evidence and arguments, and evidence and arguments are made more effective through eloquent speech and fluent language. 

Thus, the Prophet has said: <note>original Arabic: "When you quarrel and come to me, and one (man) is more clever with his proofs and arguments than the other, I decide in his favour. Therefore, if unwittingly the right of one should fall to the other, he ought not to take it. If he does take it, I will appoint hell-fire for him." This is a reference to a Hadith (saying of Muhammad) regarding the responsibility of a judge to rule on the evidence presented, even if the truth is hidden.</note> ‘When you bring a dispute to me, and one person is more persuasive with their arguments than the other, I may decide in that person’s favor. Therefore, if I mistakenly grant someone what rightfully belongs to another, they should not take it. If they do, I will condemn them to the fires of hell.’ 

The humans certainly possess greater eloquence and fluency of language than we do, and so we are afraid that we will be defeated <note>original: "discomfited"</note> in our testimony and arguments by their persuasive speech <note>original: "plausibility"</note>, and that they will prevail over us. In your opinion, what is the best plan? Careful...”

The leopard said, "It is true; but nowadays the learned men and the theologians among men, having abandoned the path which they call 'angelic morality,' have adopted devilish dispositions. Night and day they continue in strife and contention, slandering and speaking ill of each other. The King said, "You speak truly; still, it is necessary that a king's messenger should be virtuous and noble; he should not deviate from his duty. Then who is the one whom we ought to send, in whom all the qualifications of a messenger may be found? Is there anyone in this assembly who may be worthy to go there?"

# CHAPTER XI.

### *A Description of a Messenger.*

The leopard asked the lion what kind of qualifications are required in a messenger. The king replied, “A messenger must be a wise man <note>The author here forgets his "apologue"—the fact that his characters are animals—and refers to the messenger as a "man."</note> and a skilled speaker. He should forget nothing that he hears, but retain it carefully in his memory. He should not reveal the secrets of his heart to anyone. He should fulfill the duties of his trust and agreement <note>original: "covenant"</note> exactly as they ought to be performed. He should not be overly talkative, nor should he overreach or exaggerate on any matter for his own benefit. He should speak only what he has been told. He should strive and show devotion to whatever serves the well-being of his master <note>original: "principal" or "sender."</note>. If the opposing side offers him a bribe <note>The original text uses the terms "tama" and "tama dena." The translator notes these terms for greed or bribery were not found in standard dictionaries of the time.</note>, he must not be the kind of person who, out of greed, would stray from the path of trustworthiness and righteousness, falling headlong into the pit of betrayal and ruin <note>original: "perdition"</note>. If he finds any pleasure or distraction while in a foreign city, he must not stay there for his own enjoyment; instead, he should return home quickly and report to his lord everything he has seen or heard. He should provide his master with everything required by the responsibilities of advice-giving and loyalty. He should not, for fear of anything...

<summary>The Lion defines the essential traits of an effective messenger, emphasizing wisdom, memory, discretion, and resistance to bribery. The text highlights the moral and professional requirements for someone acting as a diplomatic representative.</summary>

"...fail even slightly <note>original: "a jot"</note> in the duties of a messenger, because it is the responsibility of a messenger to deliver every part of his message.”

After this, he said to the leopard, “In your opinion, is there anyone in this assembly who possesses the ability for this task?” The leopard said, “There is no one better suited for this work than the brother of Kalila and Damna.” <note>1 Kalila and Damna were two crafty jackals celebrated in folklore.</note>

The lion said to the jackal, “You have heard what the leopard has declared regarding you; what do you say to it?” The jackal said, “The leopard speaks the truth; may God reward him well and grant him his heart’s desire.” The king said, “If you go there and represent <note>original: "contend on the side of"</note> your fellow creatures, you shall be promoted in rank and receive a reward upon your return.” The jackal said, “I am the king's obedient servant; however, there are many enemies of my species there—what plan can I make against them?” The king asked who they were. The jackal said, “Dogs have a deep-seated <note>original: "inveterate"</note> hatred for me. Is the king not aware that they are very friendly and close with humans, and help them catch wild animals?”

The king said, “What is the reason that they have become so attached to humans and attack wild animals? Why, having left their own kind, have they become the assistants <note>original: "co-adjutors"</note> of a different species?” No one knew anything about this matter except the bear, who said, “I know the reason for this.” The king ordered him to explain it. 

The bear said, “Dogs have formed a friendship with humans because they share similar dispositions and tastes; furthermore, they receive many good things to eat and drink there. In their nature, there is greed, selfishness <note>original: "avarice"</note>, and evil tendencies, just as there are in humans—this is an even stronger reason for their closeness. Wild animals avoid these vices. The reason for this is that dogs eat meat—raw or cooked, permissible or forbidden <note>original: "lawful and unlawful"</note>, fresh or dried, salted or un—”

...salted, good or bad, just as they get it. All these vices are found also in man, and because of this <term>affinity: a natural likeness or similarity</term>, the dogs have left their own kind and have joined themselves to men to provide them help and aid in catching wild animals.”

The king said, “Is there any other beast besides the dog who has formed a connection and friendship with man?” 

The bear said, “The cat is also very friendly with them.” 

The king asked, “What is the reason for that connection?” 

The bear said, “For this, there is the same reason: that their temperaments and those of men are <note>original: "conformable"; meaning consistent or similar in nature</note> alike. The cat also, like men, has a desire and longing for many various sorts of food.” 

The king asked, “What is the cat’s position among men?” 

The bear said, “She is somewhat better off than the dog because she goes into their houses and sleeps upon carpets; and when they dine, she sits upon the dinner cloth. Whatever they eat themselves, they give to her as well."

The bear said, “No others go by choice; but men violently seize leopards and monkeys and take them there, though these animals do not go willingly.”

# Troubles of Dogs and Cats

The bear said, “In this way, many dogs mate with a single female and get her pregnant. When she gives birth, she endures great distress and labor to deliver eight, ten, and sometimes even more young. Yet, no one has ever seen a very large pack of dogs in the town or the forest, even though no one ever slaughters <term>Zabh: the ritual slaughter of animals for food or sacrifice</term> them. 

But goats, despite the fact that they give birth to only one or two young in an entire year and are constantly being slaughtered, can still be seen in massive flocks in the towns and forests; indeed, it is impossible to count them all. The reason for this is as follows: many hardships come upon the young of dogs and cats because of their food. Due to the poor quality of their food, they are subject to various diseases which wild animals do not suffer from. Furthermore, through their own bad habits <note>original: "vice"</note> and the cruelty of humans, their own lives and the lives of their offspring are short; thus, they are miserable and wretched.” 

After that, the lion said to the jackal, “Now, depart—go into the presence of the King of the Jinn <note>original: "Jins"; supernatural spirits in Islamic mythology</note> and complete the task for which you have been appointed.”

# CHAPTER XII.

->*A description of the Second Messenger.*<-

When the second messenger went to the Shah-murgh <note>original Persian: "Royal bird"; a legendary bird in Persian mythology similar to the phoenix or simurgh</note>, the king of the birds, and explained the facts of the case, the king listened to the animals' situation. He then gave orders for all birds to come and present themselves. Accordingly, birds of every sort and kind—from the forests, the mountains, and the waters—obeyed his command. They assembled in such exceeding great numbers that no one but God could know their count. 

The Shah-murgh said, "The humans have made a claim <note>original: "pretension"</note> that all animals are slaves and they are the masters. Because of this, many animals are currently disputing with the humans before the King of the Jinn <note>original: "Jins"; supernatural spirits in Islamic theology</note>." 

After this, he said to the peacock, his minister, "Who among the birds is the most articulate and persuasive <note>original: "fluent and eloquent"</note>? Who is fit to be sent there to represent us and contend with the humans?" 

The peacock replied, "The entire society of birds is present; let whoever you please to command go forth." 

The Shah-murgh said, "Tell me the names of all of them, so that I may know them." 

The peacock said, "The hoopoe, cock, pigeon, partridge, bulbul <note>a songbird often identified as a nightingale</note>, the Greek partridge <note>original: "kabak"; Latin: *Tetrao coturnix*</note>, the ruddy shelduck <note>original: "surkháb"; Latin: *Anas casarca*</note>, the swallow, crow, crane, sandgrouse <note>original: "sang-khwára," literally "stone-eater"</note>, sparrow, dove, turtle-dove, wagtail, goose, heron, wild-duck, nightingale, ostrich, and others; they are all present here."

The Shah-murgh said to the peacock, "Show them to me one by one, so that I may see them and judge their abilities."

# The Bulbul, etc.

...through instruction and counsel, he cries out continually: “O destroyers of life and health <note>original: "constitution"</note>, you who plant trees in gardens and build houses in towns, you who sit in high places—why are you indifferent to the dangers of the age? If anyone follows an evil path, he will burn like dry waste in the fires of hell. Think of that day when God, having separated the unbelievers from the faithful, will cast the former into hell-fire but bring the faithful into paradise.’

## The Crow.

...right hand and to the left. With exceeding eloquence and a sweet voice, she composes her songs and stays in the gardens in close friendship with people—she even goes into their houses and talks with them. When they, forgetting to think about God, engage in pleasure and dissipation <note>original: "dissipation"; meaning wasteful or excessive indulgence</note>, she exclaims with a warning and advice: “Gracious God! How heedless you are! Deceived by this life that lasts only a few days, you show negligence in thinking upon God. Why are you not absorbed in the remembrance of Him? Do you not know that you were all born to die, nourished to decay <note>original: "moulder away"</note>, and brought together only to perish? You are building this house only for it to fall into ruin. How long, deceived by the delights of this world, will you continue to be occupied in pleasure and dissipation? In the end, tomorrow <note>original: "to-morrow"; here meaning very soon</note>, you must die and be buried in the earth.

“Be wise even now; do you not know what God Almighty did to the ‘Lord of the Elephants’? Abrah <note>This refers to Abraha, the historical ruler of Yemen who, according to Islamic tradition, led an army of elephants to destroy the Kaaba in Mecca in the year of Muhammad's birth.</note>, who was the leader of that band, sought by trickery and craft to demolish the house of God. Mounting many men upon elephants, he advanced toward the temple of God. But in the end, God made his cunning and craftiness fail; He appointed <note>original: *Musallat*—meaning appointed, set over, or sent as a ruler</note> swarms upon swarms of birds against them. The birds, picking up pebbles, poured down such a shower of stones that all of them, together with their elephants, were riddled with holes like worm-eaten leaves.” After that, she exclaims, “O God, preserve me from the mischief of boys, and from danger from all animals!”

“This is the crow, the soothsayer, who reveals hidden things. He is black in color and self-controlled <note>original: "temperate"</note>. He gives information about everything which has not yet happened. He is at all times occupied in meditation upon God, and always passes his days in travel and wandering. Going into every country, he learns about ancient histories <note>original: "annals"</note>. He threatens the thoughtless with the evils of neglect, and with advice and counsel he cries, ‘Practice self-control, and beware of...”
