# The Swallow.

...that day when you will rot in the grave, when because of the disgrace of your deeds your skin will be torn off. Now, through delusion, you prefer the life of this world over the next. Having fled from the commands of God, there is no resting place or escape for you. If you wish for salvation, engage in humble petition <note>original: "supplication"</note> and prayer; perhaps God, taking compassion, will preserve you from harm.”

“Next is the swallow, the wanderer of the sky. She is light in flight; her feet are small and her wings are large. She mostly lives in the houses of people, and there she raises her young. Always at dawn and at evening, she offers prayers and pleas for mercy. In her journeys, she travels great distances. In the days of heat, she chooses her home in cool places, and during the cold, she stays in warm places. In her praises and prayers, she repeats this refrain: ‘Holy is He who made the sea and land, who established the mountains and formed the currents of the streams—who, according to one's deserts, ordains <note>original: "predestines"</note> our food and our death, from which there is never any escape. He is the helper of travelers on their journeys and is Lord of the whole earth and of all creatures.’ After this praise and prayer, she says, ‘I have traveled into every country, have seen all the servants of God, and have come back to my own country. Holy is He who, having brought male and female together, bestowed upon them many children. Having brought them out from the state of nothingness <note>original: "nonentity"; meaning the state of not existing before being created</note>, He clothed them with the garments of existence. Praise is due to Him who is the creator of all creatures and the giver of all blessings.’

“The crane, the watchman, is he who stands in the field. His neck is long, his feet short, and in flight he reaches halfway to heaven. Twice during the night he keeps watch <note>original: "vigils"</note>, and in praise of God he offers his thanksgiving, saying, ‘Holy is that God who, by His power, created a pair of every species of animal so that, living together, they might multiply and have offspring, and might remember their Creator.’”

# The Nightingale

“And this is the *sang-khwára* <term>stone-eater; likely referring to the sandgrouse, a bird known to swallow grit to digest seeds</term>, an inhabitant of dry land. He always lives in woods and deserts. Morning and evening he repeats this prayer: ‘Holy is He who created the sky and the earth. He is the Maker of the heavens, of the constellations, and of the stars, and these all revolve by His command. The falling of the rain, the movement of the air, the appearance of thunder and lightning—all are His work. He alone raises the vapors from the earth through which the order <note>original: "economy"</note> of the world is maintained. He is a wonderful Creator who, after death, gives life again to old, rotten bones. Gracious God! What a Creator He is! The tongue of man is incapable <note>original: "incompetent"</note> of praising and honoring Him; how is it possible for human understanding to grasp His true nature?’

“The sweet-voiced nightingale is the one seated on the branch of a tree. His body is small, his movement swift, and his voice sweet. In this way, he melodiously sings his song in praise of God: ‘Praise be to that Creator who is the Lord of power and goodness. He alone is God, for He has no equal; the giver of favors <note>original: "boons"</note>, the provider of all blessings, both private and public. Like the sea, He generously honors every man with the gifts of His favor.’ And sometimes, with deep sorrow, the bird speaks in this way: ‘How happy was that time when I roamed among the flowers of the garden, and all the trees were heavy with many kinds of fruit.’”

At this point, the *Sháh-murgh* <term>King of Birds; in Persian mythology, the Simurgh</term> said to the peacock, “Of all these, who in your opinion is the most suitable bird to send there, so that he may go and argue with the humans and be a defender <note>original: "ally"</note> of his own kind?” The peacock replied, “All of these have the ability for the task because they are poetic and eloquent, but the nightingale is the most eloquent and melodious of them all.” The *Sháh-murgh* ordered the nightingale to depart and go there, putting his trust in God, who is a help and support in every emergency.

# CHAPTER XIII.

### *Concerning the Missions of the Third Messenger.*

The third messenger went to the king-bee, the chief of all the flies, and recounted to him all the affairs of the animals. As soon as the king of all insects heard this, he ordered all the insects to appear before him. Following his command, flies, mosquitoes, gadflies, gnats, fleas, wasps, moths—and, in short <note>original: "in fine"</note>, all creatures with small bodies and wings that live no longer than a year—arrived and attended the gathering. The king shared with them all the information he had received from the messenger and said, “Who among you will go there and dispute with the men on behalf of the animals?” They all asked in what way the men were boasting over them. The messenger said, “They boast about this: they say, ‘We are larger in size and stature, we have greater strength, and in every way we are superior to the animals.’” The chief of the wasps said, “I will go there and dispute with the men.” The chief of the flies said, “I will go there and <note>original: "aud," a typographical error in the source</note> act as the representative <note>original: "deputy"</note> of my tribe.” The chief of the mosquitoes said, “I will go there.” The chief of the locusts said, “I will go there; as an ally to my own species, I will debate the matter <note>original: "(the mat-ter)," hyphenated across a line break in the source</note> with the men.” In this way, everyone was ready for the task.

The king said, “How is it that all of you, without careful thought or consideration, plan to go there?” The assembly <note>original: "congregation"</note> of gnats replied, “O King, we rely upon...”

## Consultation of the Insects

...the help of God, and it is certain that by His aid we shall gain the victory over them. In former times there have been very tyrannical kings, but by the help of God we have always prevailed over them. Often there has been proof of this.” The king said, “State the circumstances.” The chief of the mosquitoes said, “Among men, Nimrod <note>Nimrod: A legendary biblical figure and king of Shinar, often depicted in literature as a powerful and arrogant tyrant who challenged God.</note> was a king of the highest status. He was exceedingly arrogant and perverse, and in his pomp, dignity, and splendor, he had no regard for any man. But a gnat of our race, who was very small and frail of body, destroyed such a great king. Despite his dignity and might, his power was of no use to him.” The king replied, “You speak truly.”

The wasp said, “When a man is fully equipped with arms—with sword, javelin, spear, knife, and arrow in hand—and is accoutred <term>accoutred</term> <note>original: "accoutred"; meaning dressed or equipped, typically for military duty.</note> for battle, if a wasp from our tribe goes and stings him, piercing him with a sting no larger than the point of a needle, how wretched his condition becomes! His body swells up, his hands and feet grow languid <note>original: "languid"; meaning weak or without energy.</note>; he cannot move, nor does he even pay any attention to his shield and sword.” The king said, “It is true.”

The fly said, “When a king of men sits upon his throne in great pomp and splendor, and his doorkeepers and guards are standing all around him with the utmost devotion and willingness so that no pain or annoyance may reach him—if at that time a fly comes out of his kitchen, or his necessary <term>necessary</term> <note>original: "necessary"; an archaic term for a toilet or privy.</note>, and, with its body all covered in filth, goes and sits upon his person or clothes and teases him, he can never find the power to escape from it.” The king said, “It is true.”

The mosquito said, “If any man is seated in society or in private behind his screen, or with mosquito curtains hung up, and one of our tribe goes and gets into his clothes and bites him, how restless he becomes, and what a rage he gets into! But he can do nothing to us; he can only thump his own head and slap his own cheeks.”

## CHAPTER XIV.

->*On the matters of the Fourth Messenger.*<-

When the fourth messenger went into the presence of the Phoenix, the king of the birds of prey, and explained the situation, the king gave orders for all the birds of his species to attend. In accordance with the order, vultures, phoenixes, hawks, falcons, kites, owls, parrots, and, in short <term>original: "in fine"; meaning "in conclusion" or "to sum up"</term>, all carnivorous birds possessing claws and beaks instantly presented themselves. The Phoenix told them the facts of the animals' dispute with the humans. After that, he said to his Vizier <note>original: "wazír"; a high-ranking minister or political advisor</note>, the Shunkar <note>original: "shunkár"; a species of black-eyed falcon highly valued in the Middle East for falconry</note>, “Is there any one of these birds who is fit for this task? Can we send someone who may go and confront the men and be an ally for his own race in this conflict?” The Vizier said, “There is no one but the owl who has the ability for this business.” The king asked what the reason was that no one besides him was fit for the task.

The Vizier said, “Because all birds of prey are afraid of men and fly from them; furthermore, they do not understand the human language. But the owl dwells near their villages, and often, indeed, in their old houses which have fallen into ruin. His self-restraint <note>original: "continence"; here meaning asceticism or self-control</note> and contentment are such as no other animal possesses. By day he fasts and weeps in fear of God, and at night he is absorbed in devotion and in waking those who are heedless. He laments over former kings who have died and repeats this verse, which is appropriate to their condition: ‘How many gardens and fountains they have left behind...’ <note>original: “We have left behind the gardens and foun-”; this is a translation of a Quranic verse (44:25) reflecting on the ruins of past civilizations and the fleeting nature of worldly power.</note>

->*Birds of Prey.*<-

The <term>shunkár</term> <note>original: "shunkár"; a large bird of prey, likely a gyrfalcon, often used in royal falconry</note> has also fled from their oppression and has gone to live in the forests, abandoning his home in his own country. Although we are so strong that any one of us could, if he wished, carry off several men and make prey of them, it is still not fitting for the good to commit such evil or to pay any attention to their wicked conduct. We deliberately <note>original footnote: "having seen and known all"</note> keep our distance and leave them to God's judgment, for in this world, there is no good to be found in fighting and arguing. They will reap the fruits and consequences of their actions in the next world.”

After that, he said, “How many ships have been in distress because of contrary winds, and we have helped them stay on their course? And how many poor men have had their ships broken by fierce winds, and when they began to sink and drown, we brought them to the shore? We did this so that God Almighty might be satisfied and pleased with us, and so that we might show our gratitude for His mercies in this way! For He has made us powerful in body and has granted us vigor and strength; He is our aid and helper in every circumstance.”

### CHAPTER XV.

#### *On the affairs of the Fifth Messenger.*

When the fifth messenger went into the presence of the king of the sea creatures <note>original: "aquatic animals"</note> and brought him the news of the dispute, the king also assembled all his subjects and followers. Accordingly, the fish, the frogs, the crocodiles, the dolphins, the turtles, and all kinds of aquatic animals of every variety and every form and shape gathered together in accordance with his command. The king set forth to them all that he had heard from the mouth of the messenger. After that, he said to the messenger, “If the men consider themselves superior to us in strength and courage, I will go immediately, and in one instant I will breathe fire <note>original: "spurt out fire"</note> on them; then, dragging them down by the force of my breath, I will swallow them.” The messenger replied, “They boast of none of these things. Instead, they consider themselves superior because they possess the greatest sense and wisdom—they are acquainted with every science and art and know very many crafts and plans, maintaining that there is no equal to them in intelligence and judgment.”

The king said, “Explain the facts of their science and art in detail so that we also may know them.” The messenger said, “What! Does the king not know that by their science and wisdom they go down into the ocean <margin>1. *Daryáe kulzum*: the ocean, or, in a more specific sense, the Red Sea.</margin> and bring up jewels from its bed? By their schemes and inventions...”

<summary>The fifth messenger informs the King of the Sea Creatures that humans claim superiority through wisdom and science rather than physical strength. -># Speech of the Dolphin.<-

They climb the mountains and, catching vultures and eagles, bring them down to the ground. In the same way, through their science and wisdom, they construct yokes of poles; placing these upon the shoulders of oxen, they load heavy goods upon their backs and lead them from east to west, and from west to east, crossing every forest and desert. Through thought and wisdom they build ships and, loading them with goods, transport them from sea to sea. Going into the mountains and hills, they dig and extract every kind of jewel from the earth: gold, silver, iron, copper, and a great variety of other things. If a man goes to the bank of any stream, river, or brook, he can, by the power of his knowledge, cast such a spell <note>original: "bind such a charm"</note> that even if a thousand crocodiles or dragons should afterwards go to that place, it would be impossible for them to enter. But before the King of the Jinn <term>Jinn: supernatural spirits in Islamic mythology, often called "genies" in the West, who are being asked to judge this dispute</term>, the discussion is about justice and fairness, argument and proof; there is no mention whatever of power and strength, or trickery and cunning.”

When the king heard all this from the messenger, he looked intently at everyone sitting around him and asked what they thought was the best course of action, and if there was anyone who would go and compete with the men. No one gave an answer except the dolphin, who lives in the salt sea and is exceedingly friendly with humans; for when a person is drowning, he pulls him from the water and carries him to the shore. 

He said, “Among the sea creatures <note>original: "aquatic animals"</note>, the fish is best suited for this task because she has a large body and a beautiful form; she has a fair face, white skin, and a straight body; she is swift in motion and surpasses everything in swimming. In numbers, she exceeds all other sea creatures, and her offspring are so abundant that every river, stream, sea, and lake is filled with them. She also holds a high position in the opinion of men because, on one occasion, she provided refuge in her belly to their prophet <note>A reference to the story of Jonah (Yunus) and the whale, which is common to the Bible and the Quran</note>, and afterwards brought him safely to his...”

# The Crab.

“...home in safety. All people believe that the entire earth rests upon her back.” <note>This refers to the "World Turtle" or "Cosmic Turtle," a common mythological motif in several ancient cultures where the world is supported by a giant turtle.</note>

The King asked the Fish what she had to say about it, and she replied, “I cannot go there by any means, nor can I argue with the humans, because I have no feet to get there, nor do I have a tongue to talk to them. I cannot endure thirst; if I am parted from the water for even an instant, my condition becomes miserable. In my opinion, the Turtle is better suited for this task, because he goes out of the water and stays on dry land as well; living in the sea or on the land is all the same to him. Besides this, his body is strong and his back is hard; he is exceedingly patient and can endure trouble and pain.”

The King asked the Turtle what he thought was advisable, and he said, “This work is also impossible for me. When I walk, my feet become heavy, and the journey is far. I am also a poor speaker, and making long speeches is impossible for me. The Dolphin is better suited for this, because he is very vigorous in his movements and has great powers of speech.”

The King asked the Dolphin what he thought was advisable. He said, “The Crab is best suited for this business, because he has many feet and is swift at walking and running. His talons are sharp, his claws are hard, and his back is as strong as if it were covered in armor.” The King spoke to the Crab, and he replied, “How can I go there? My body is ugly, my back is crooked, and my appearance is exceedingly bad. I would not like for people to laugh at me.” The King said, “Why should they mock you? What fault is there in you?” The Crab said, “When they see me, they will all say, ‘Who is this headless animal?’ I have eyes in my neck and a mouth in my chest; my cheeks are split open on both sides; I have eight legs and they are crooked; and I crawl along face-down as if I were made of lead <note>This describes the heavy, sideways, and low-slung gait of a crab as seen from a human perspective</note>. Everyone who sees me will crack jokes at my expense.” The King said, “Who then is better suited to go there?”

# The Crocodile.

The Crab said, “In my opinion, the Crocodile is very well-suited for this task, because his legs are very strong and he is very active. He is a swift runner, his mouth is large, his tongue is long, his teeth are numerous, and his body is hard. He is very patient; to achieve his goal, he waits a long time and shows no recklessness in anything.” The King asked the Crocodile, and he replied, “I am not at all suited for this work because I am full of strong passions. Leaping, jumping, and running away with whatever I can steal—these are all my defects. In short, I am a complete cheat and a fraud.” When the messenger heard this, he said, “For this mission, there is no need for strength, vigor, or trickery. Instead, wisdom and seriousness, justice and fairness, and eloquence and clear speech are the qualities required.”

The Crocodile said, “I possess none of these qualities or virtues. In my opinion, the Frog is the best candidate for this task because he is mild, patient, and devout. Day and night, he offers praise in remembrance of God, and morning and evening, he is dedicated to prayer and fasting. He also enters the houses of humans. Among the Children of Israel, he is held in high honor and esteem because he once showed them kindness: when Nimrod <note>Nimrod: A legendary king mentioned in the Bible and Quran, often portrayed as a tyrant who opposed the prophet Abraham.</note> threw Abraham, the friend of God <note>original: "friend of God" — This is a translation of the Arabic title *Khalilullah*, traditionally given to the prophet Abraham.</note>, into the fire, the Frog carried water in his mouth and squirted it onto the flames. Thus, the fire was extinguished and did not harm Abraham’s body.

On another occasion, when there was a conflict between Moses and Pharaoh, he assisted Moses <note>This refers to the plague of frogs; in this tradition, the frogs are seen as willing servants of God's will to help Moses against the Pharaoh.</note>. He is also eloquent; he speaks many words and is constantly engaged in praising, magnifying, and glorifying God. He wanders both on land and in the water; he moves on land and swims in the water, and is familiar with both. His physical features are also suitable: his head is round, his face is pleasing, his eyes are bright, and his hands and feet are large. He is swift in his movements, and he enters the houses of humans without showing any fear.”

# The Appointment of the Frog

<header>The Appointment of the Frog</header>

The King asked the Frog, “In your opinion, what is the best course of action?” 

The Frog replied, “I am ready, heart and soul <note>original: *ba sar o chashm* — literally 'with my head and eyes,' a Persian expression meaning 'most willingly' or 'with all my heart'</note>, and I am the King’s subject; whatever order he may give is acceptable to me. If it is decided that I should go there, I am perfectly willing. Once I have arrived and taken the side of my fellow creatures, I will argue against the humans. However, I hope the King will pray to God to grant me aid and assistance, because the prayers of kings on behalf of their subjects are heard.” 

In response to his request, the King prayed to God, and the entire assembly said, “Amen.” Then the Frog took his leave of the King and, traveling to that place, presented himself before the King of the Jinn <note>Jinn: Supernatural spirits in Islamic and Near Eastern mythology, often portrayed as having their own society and kings</note>.

## CHAPTER XVI.

### *The Description of the Sixth Messenger*

When the sixth messenger went to the dragon—the king of all reptiles and the chief of all insects <term>original: *kira* — a word meaning a worm, grub, or insect</term> and ants—and told him about the situation of the animals, the dragon commanded all the crawling creatures to attend him as soon as he heard the news.

Instantly, all the snakes, scorpions, chameleons, lizards, green lizards, spiders, lice, small ants, earthworms, and, in short, all the crawling things that are produced in filth or crawl upon the leaves of trees, came and gathered before the king. They assembled in such great multitudes that no one but God would have the power to count them. When the king saw their wonderful and extraordinary forms and shapes, he was amazed and remained silent for a moment.

Afterward, having carefully considered and looked upon them, he perceived that many of these creatures were very small and weak in body, and lacking in instinct and intelligence. He became serious and asked, “What can be done with these?” He said to his minister, the serpent, “In your opinion, is there anyone among them clever enough to be sent on the business of this dispute so that he may confront the men? For these creatures are, for the most part, unable to speak <note>original: "dumb"</note>, deaf, and blind. They have no hands or feet at all; there is neither hair nor feather visible on their bodies; they have no beaks or claws; and, for the most part, they are weak and feeble.”

<summary>The Dragon, king of reptiles and insects, assembles his subjects to choose a representative for the dispute with humans, but he expresses doubt because of their small size and perceived physical limitations.</summary>

At last, through the prayers of the king, all the creatures who were assembled there began to speak with great eloquence and fluency.

# CHAPTER XVII.

## *Report of the Locust's Speech.*

When the locust saw that the king showed great consideration and kindness to his subjects <note>original: "people"</note>, he climbed up the wall and tuned his voice <note>original: "Sáz-karná"—to attune or bring into harmony</note>. He began to sing in praise of God with very sweet notes, delivering this speech with great eloquence and expression:

"Praise and thanks are due to that True Benefactor who produced all sorts and kinds of blessings upon the earth. By His omnipotence, He brought the animals out from the corner of non-existence <note>original: "non-entity"</note> into the expanse of existence, bestowing various forms upon them. He existed before time and place, earth and sky. He was radiant with the light of His unity, free from the stain of dependent existence <note>original: "subordinate existence." This means He shone in His own self-existent glory, not deriving His existence from any other being</note>. From simple light, without any mixture of matter or form, He created the active intellect <note>The "active intellect" (Aql-e-Fa'al) is a concept in medieval philosophy describing the bridge between the divine mind and human reason</note>. Moreover, by the simple utterance of one command—'BE'—He brought it from behind the veil of nothingness and established it in the arena of existence."

After that, he said, "O king! Do not grieve <note>original: "sorrow not"</note> for the weakness and helplessness <note>original: "impotence"</note> of this species, because their Creator, who formed them and provided nourishment <note>original: "sustenance"</note> for them, is always mindful of them. Just as parents show tenderness and kindness to their children <note>original: "offspring"</note>, He also shows compassion for the state of these insects. For when God created the animals and designed different forms and shapes for them all..."

...extracting the juices, they make them the food for their bodies; and through the effect of those juices, strength remains in them.

"Other animals wander about in search of food and run away from danger; but these are spared that labor and pain because God has not provided them with hands and feet to walk about and obtain their daily food. After that, he asked, "Are you able to go there and argue against the men?" The locust replied, "I am ready, heart and soul. At the king's command, I will go there and help my brothers." Indeed, it is to such a degree that some men even criticize God, saying, 'Why did He create them? There is no good in them at all; on the contrary, they are injurious and hurtful from beginning to end.'"

The locust said, “Why do they say this?” 

He replied, “Because they say that there is poison in our mouths, and that we are of no use except to cause the destruction and death of animals. But they talk nonsense <note>original: "babble"</note> so foolishly because of their ignorance and silliness; they know nothing about the true nature and value of anything; consequently, God has troubled them with sufferings. In reality, however, they all have such a great need for these snakes that kings and nobles keep the poison of these animals in their rings so that it may be useful on certain occasions <note>Poison rings were historically used for quick suicide to avoid capture or, in folklore, for assassination.</note>. If they would carefully consider the matter, ascertain the facts and the uses of these animals, and learn the purpose of the poison in their mouths, then they would never ask why God made them or say there is no good in them; nor would they unfairly criticize <note>original: "cast absurd censure upon"</note> God. Although God has made the poison of these snakes the cause of death for animals, He has, on the other hand, made their flesh the means of curing that very poison <note>The author likely refers to "theriac," a famous ancient medical compound often made with snake flesh, believed to be a universal antidote.</note>.”

The locust said, “O wise one! Explain some of their other benefits.” 

The snake said, “When God created those animals of which you have made mention in your discourse, and gave to every species of animal the means and tools by which they might obtain what is beneficial and be secure from danger, to some He gave a warm stomach, so that the food, after being chewed <note>original: "masticated"</note> might be digested and become a part of their bodies. But the snake has no stomach in which to digest, nor any teeth strong enough to chew; instead, a hot venom is formed in his mouth by which snakes eat and digest. For when the snake takes the flesh of any animal into its mouth, he releases this hot venom onto it; then the flesh instantly dissolves, and he swallows it <note>The author reflects an early biological theory that snake venom functioned primarily as a digestive acid to break down whole prey.</note>. So, if God Almighty had not created this venom in their mouths, how would they have been able to eat anything? Nourishment would not have been in any...”

The locust said, “Explain this! What benefit do animals receive from these snakes, and what good comes from them being on the earth?” He said, “Just as benefit arises from the creation of other animals, so there is an advantage gained from these also.” The locust asked him to explain this matter fully, and he replied, “When God Almighty created the entire world, He perfected every individual thing according to His own pleasure. Throughout creation, He made some creatures for the sake of others, and according to His wisdom, He furnished them with the necessary tools. He did whatever He knew was for the welfare of the world, but now and then <note>original: "ever and anon"</note>, for one reason or another, harm <note>original: "mischief"</note> or injury happens to some. It is not that God Almighty involves them in this harm out of spite, even though the potential for harm and evil in everything is clear and open to His wisdom; but rather, it is not the habit or custom of the Creator to refrain from making something that provides comfort and happiness to the majority <note>original: "generality"</note> of the world just because it might be injurious to a few.

“The explanation of it is this: when God Almighty formed all the stars, among their number He made the sun to be a lamp for the world and caused its warmth to be the source of life for all creatures. In the whole universe, the sun occupies a position similar to that of the heart in the body. Just as natural heat is produced in the heart and, spreading through the whole body, is the cause of its life, so the heat of the sun also benefits all creation. If the sun ever causes injury or damage to a few beings, it would not be right for the Creator to set aside the sun and deprive the world in general of a universal blessing and perfect <note>original: "consummate"</note> benefit.

“The same is the case for Saturn, Mars, and all the stars, through whom prosperity and happiness come to the...”

<summary>The snake argues that God creates things for the greater good of the world even if they cause incidental harm to a few, using the sun's life-giving but occasionally damaging heat as a primary example. This philosophical defense addresses the "problem of evil" by suggesting that individual suffering does not outweigh universal benefit.</summary>

Everyone, according to their capacity, accepts His bounty.”

# CHAPTER XVIII.

### *Account of the Assembly of the Animal Representatives*

In the morning, the representatives of all the animals, having come from every country, gathered together. The King of the Jins <term>supernatural spirits in Islamic mythology</term> came and sat in the public hall to decide the dispute <note>original: "quarrel"</note>. The attendants, following his orders, made a proclamation: "Let all the complainants and those seeking justice <note>original: "redress"</note>, who have suffered from tyranny, come forward and attend. The King has taken his seat to pass a judgment on the case, and the judges and lawyers are present."

As soon as this notice was heard, all the animals and men who had gathered from every side fell into ranks and stood before the King. Making their bows <note>original: "obeisances"</note> and paying their respects, they began to offer their blessings <note>original: "benedictions"</note>. The King looked in every direction and saw that a vast multitude <note>original: "khilkat," literally meaning "a creation"</note> of every sort and kind, in great numbers, was in attendance. Stunned with wonder <note>original: "Lost in astonishment"</note>, he remained silent for a moment. After that, turning toward a wise man <note>original: "sage"</note> of the Jins, he said, "Do you see this wonderful and extraordinary gathering of beings?"

He replied, "O King, I see and behold them with the eye of my heart. The King, having seen them, is amazed, but I am marvelling at the wisdom and power of that all-wise Creator who created them, and formed for them shapes of so many varieties and kinds. He always nourishes them and gives them food <note>original: "sustenance"</note>. He preserves them from every danger; moreover, they are always present to His omnipresent know—" <note>The text likely continues with "knowledge" on the following page.</note>

And whatever was behind His veil of mystery, He brought forth into the field of visibility, so that people of perception, having seen it, might acknowledge His miraculous art, His uniqueness <note>original: "incomparability"</note>, might, and oneness <term>original: "unity," from the Persian/Urdu word *Yaktái*</term>,¹ and should not be in need of further proofs and arguments.

> "The forms visible in the physical <note>original: "corporeal"</note> world are the likenesses and counterparts of those forms that exist in the world of spirits. But the forms in that spiritual world are bright and transparent, while these physical ones are dark and dense. Just as in a painting every limb resembles the animal it portrays, in the same way, these physical forms bear a likeness to the forms in the spirit world. However, those spirit forms are the active powers that cause movement, while these physical forms are merely set in motion <note>The author distinguishes between the 'motive powers' (active) and 'passive' physical bodies</note>.² Those creatures lower in status than these lack instinct, motion, and speech <note>original: "tongue"</note>, whereas these possess instincts. Those forms in the eternal world live forever, but these are mortal and perishable."

After that, he stood up and delivered this speech:

> "Praise is due to that Creator who, by His almighty power <note>original: "omnipotence"</note>, made all creatures manifest and produced a creation of so many varieties and kinds in this world. Having brought into existence all these created things—which no created being's intelligence can fully grasp—He displayed the splendor of the light of His miraculous art to the eye of every discerning person. Having bounded the expanse of the world on six sides <note>This refers to the four cardinal directions plus up and down, encompassing the physical universe</note>, He established time and place for the comfort of His creatures. Having formed many levels <note>original: "degrees"</note> in the heavens, He appointed angels to each. To the animals, He..."

# Speech of the Greek

The King asked the Greek sage, “What is this he is saying?” and he replied, “It is true we have acquired many sciences—”

# Speech of the Khurásání

...from ancient sages, just as many people now learn from us. The way the world functions <note>original: *Kár-khána*—literally, "workshop"</note> is that benefit comes to one person from another. Thus, the learned scholars of Persia acquired the sciences of astrology and astronomy from the sages of India, and in the same way, the children of Israel obtained the sciences of magic and enchantment from Solomon, the son of David.”

After that, a person appeared in the back row. He was strong in build, wore a long beard, and looked toward the sun with earnest faith. The King asked who he was, and the *wazir* <term>wazir: a high-ranking government minister or political advisor</term> said he was a Khurasani <note>a person from Khurasan, a vast historical region that today covers parts of Iran, Afghanistan, and Central Asia</note>. The King ordered that he should be told to share some facts about himself.

Accordingly, in obedience to the command, he said, “Praise be to God, who has given us many kinds of blessings and honors. He has made our country surpass all others in its prosperity, and through the mouth of His prophet, He has introduced our praise into the Divine Word <note>referring to the holy scriptures</note>. Thus, there are many verses of the Quran <note>original: Kurán</note> which testify to our excellence and virtues.

Truly, praise is due to Him who has given to us a capacity for religion greater than any other men. This is because among us there are many who read the Bible and the Gospels, even if they do not understand their full meaning; furthermore, they consider the prophetic power of the Lords Moses and Jesus to be a truth <note>original: "verity"</note>. And some read the Quran, although they do not understand its full significance <note>original: "import"</note>; yet they still acknowledge in their hearts the religion of the prophet of the latter days <note>referring to Muhammad</note>. 

We wear mourning clothes to show our sorrow for Imam Husain <note>the grandson of the Prophet Muhammad, whose death at the Battle of Karbala is a central event of mourning in Islamic history</note>, and we demanded the payment of blood <note>original: "requital of blood"—meaning revenge or legal justice</note> from those who caused him to be killed. By His grace, we are hopeful that the appearance of the Imam of the latter days <note>original: Imám Mahdí; a messianic figure in Islamic tradition expected to appear at the end of time to bring justice to the world</note> will take place in our country.”

The King looked toward the sages and said, “What reply do you have to make to this man, who has boasted...

### CHAPTER XIX.

->*Regarding the Lion.*<-

On the third day, as all the men and animals were standing in their ranks before the King, he looked toward them all, and a jackal appeared at the front. He asked, “Who are you?” and the jackal replied, “I am the representative of the animals.” The King said, “Who sent you?” and he said, “The Lion, Abú’l Háris <note>original: Abú’l Háris; an Arabic kunya or honorific title for a lion, literally meaning 'father of the collector' or 'father of the hunter'</note>, sent me.” He asked, “What country does he live in? And who are his subjects?” The jackal said, “He lives in the woods and wilds, and all beasts and cattle are his subjects.” He asked, “Who are his allies?” The jackal said, “The hunting leopard, the hog-deer, the stag, the hare, the fox, and the wolf—all are his friends and allies.” 

The King said, “Describe his appearance and character.” The jackal said, “He is larger in form than all other animals and superior in strength; in awe and dignity, he surpasses everyone. His chest is broad, his waist is lean, his head is large, his joints <term>original: fetlocks; while usually referring to a horse's leg, here it describes the lion's powerful lower limbs</term> are strong, his teeth and claws are hard, his voice is loud, and his appearance is terrifying. No man or animal is able, through fear, to stand before him. He is upright in every matter and has no need of friends or allies for any business. He is so generous that when he has caught prey, he divides it among all the animals, and he himself eats only what he needs. When he sees a light from a distance, he goes and stands near it, and then his anger subsides. He harms neither woman nor child. He has a great liking and love for music. He is afraid of nothing except the ant, for this insect prevails over him and—

# CHAPTER XX.

->*An Account of the Dragon (<note>original: Súbán</note>) and the Sea-serpent (<note>original: Tinnín</note>).*<-

After this, the King looked to his right and to his left, when suddenly a voice reached his ear. He saw a locust fluttering its wings and singing in an exceedingly soft voice. He asked the locust who he was, and he replied, "I am the representative of all the worms and ants, and their king sent me here." The King asked who that king was and where he lived, and the locust answered, "His name is Dragon; he lives upon high hills and mountains near the frigid regions where there are no clouds or rain, and no plants at all. Animals die there from the severity of the cold." The King asked who made up his army and his subjects, and he said, "All snakes, scorpions, and similar creatures are his army and subjects, and they live in every place upon the face of the earth." 

The King asked why their king had separated from his army and subjects to live on such a steep slope <term>original: acclivity; an upward slope or hill</term>. He answered, "Because there is a poison in his mouth, and the heat from it makes his entire body burn. He lives in that icy region to find comfort in the cold." The King told the locust to describe the Dragon's appearance and character. He answered, "His appearance and character are exactly like those of the sea-serpent."

The King said, "Does anyone know the qualities of the sea-serpent well enough to describe them?" The locust said, "The frog, who is the ambassador for the water-dwelling <term>original: aquatic</term> animals, is present here; please inquire of him."

<summary>A locust appearing before the King describes the King of the worms and ants—a Dragon who lives in isolation in the frozen mountains to soothe the burning heat of his own venom—and refers the King to a frog to learn more about sea-serpents.</summary>

# The Sea-Serpent

The King looked toward him. The frog was standing on a small mound by the side of the sea and was busy with prayer and worship. The King asked him who he was, and he replied, “I am the ambassador of the king of the water-dwelling <term>original: aquatic</term> animals.” The King then commanded him to share his name and where he lived <note>original: *Nishán*—mark, sign; seat, address. *Nám o nishán*—name and address.</note>.

The frog said, “His name is the ‘Sea-Serpent’ <note>original: *Tinnín*</note>; he dwells in the salt sea. All the water animals—turtles, fish, frogs, and crocodiles—are his subjects.”

The King told him to describe the creature's form and appearance, and he said, “In size, he is larger than all other water animals. His appearance is extraordinary and his figure is terrifying. His body <note>original: *Kadd*—literally meaning stature.</note> is very long. All the animals of the sea are afraid of him. His head is massive, his eyes are bright, his mouth is wide, and he has many teeth. He swallows countless numbers of the water animals he catches. 

When he suffers from indigestion because of overeating, he arches his body like a bow and supports himself by the strength of his head and tail. By pushing the middle of his body out of the water, he raises it into the air. Then, the heat of the sun helps him digest the food in his belly. However, while in that state, he often faints, and then the clouds that rise from the sea pick him up and drop him onto dry land. He then dies and becomes food for wild beasts. 

But sometimes, after being raised high into the air with the clouds, he falls within the territory of Gog and Magog <note>Gog and Magog: In many historical and religious traditions, these are described as powerful, legendary peoples living in a distant land.</note> and serves as their food for several days. In short, all the animals of the sea are afraid of him and flee from him; but he is afraid of no one, except for one small animal about the size of a gnat, which he dreads greatly. For when this creature bites him, its poison affects his entire body. Eventually, he dies, and all the water animals gather to feed upon his flesh for a long time. Just as he devours other animals that are small, so do they all con—

->### Creatures Prey on Each Other.<-

[...con]gregate and eat him. The same is the practice of birds of prey and other birds. Sparrows and others eat gnats and ants, and sparrow-hawks and falcons make prey of them. Then the great hawks, eagles, and vultures prey upon the sparrow-hawks and falcons and devour them. And finally, when they die, all the worms, ants, and small creatures devour them.

"The same is true of humans, for they all eat the flesh of deer, hog-deer <note>The hog-deer (*Axis porcinus*) is a small deer native to South and Southeast Asia.</note>, goats, sheep, and birds; and when they die, little worms devour their bodies in the grave. It is the same throughout the whole world. Sometimes the big animals eat the little ones, and sometimes the little ones use their teeth upon the big ones. Hence the wise have said, 'The death of one is the gain of another.' And so God Almighty has said, <note>original: Arabic from the Koran</note> 'We cause these days of varying fortune to rotate among people in turns <note>original: "We make the times to change from vicissitude to vicissitude among men"</note>, and no one knows this fact except the wise.'" <sup>1</sup>

After that, he said, "I have heard that all humans suppose that they are the masters and that all the animals are their slaves. How is it that they do not perceive, after the account which I have given of the state of the animals, that all animals are equal?—there is no difference between them. Sometimes they feed, and sometimes they become the food of others. I cannot understand what humans have to boast about over the animals, for their position is just the same as ours: good and evil will become manifest after death, all will mingle in the dust, and in the end, they will return to God."

After that, he said to the King, "When humans advance their claim to be the masters and to have all the animals as their slaves, this trickery and false assertion of theirs causes great astonishment. They are exceedingly stupid to utter a statement so contrary to common sense. I am surprised how they can venture to declare that all predatory <term>original: rapacious</term>, grazing, and hunt—"

---

->## The Parrot.<-

[Craw]ling animals, dragons, crocodiles, serpents, and scorpions are their slaves. Do they not realize that if the wild animals were to come from the forest, the birds of prey from the mountains, or the crocodiles from the sea to attack them, not a single person would be left alive? These creatures would enter their country and destroy them all—not one man would escape with his life. Do they not consider it a mercy, and do they not give thanks that God has placed all these animals far away from their land? Yet, those wretched animals whom they keep in captivity at their homes are subjected to torture night and day. Because of this, humans are filled with <note>original: "conceit"</note> arrogance and, without proof or argument, make such a senseless claim of ownership.

After this, the King looked toward the front. The parrot, seated upon the branch of a tree, was listening to everyone’s words. The King said, "Tell me the details of that island."

The King told him to describe the form and appearance of the phoenix, and he replied, "In size, he is larger than all other birds; in flight, he is strong; his claws and beak are hard; his wings are exceedingly broad, and when he moves them in the air, they look like the sails of a ship. His tail is long, and when he flies, the mountains are moved by the force of his motion. He picks up and carries away elephants, rhinoceroses, and other large animals from the earth."

# Varieties of Men.

“...excellent, and at some time or other I will speak about it at greater length <note>original: "expatiate upon it"</note>.”

After that, the King looked towards the gathering <note>original: "congregation"</note> of men. These seventy persons of every variety of form, wearing garments of various <note>original: "divers"</note> kinds, were standing there. He said to them, “Reflect and ponder over your answer to all this that the animals have spoken.” Then he asked them, “Who is your king?” and they replied, “We have many kings, and each one dwells in his own kingdom with his army and people.”

The King asked, “What is the reason for this? There is only one king among the animals, despite <note>original: "notwithstanding"</note> their great numbers, while among you, despite your small number <note>original: "paucity"</note>, are there many kings?” An Iraqi <note>original: "’Irákí"</note> (Arab) belonging to the group of men answered, “Men have many wants; their positions are diverse; consequently, many kings are required for them. But this is not the case among animals; among them, the king is whoever is largest in form. Among men, the reverse of this often happens, for our kings are frequently weak, delicate, and thin. This is because what is required from kings is that they should be just, equitable, and kind <note>original: "benignant"</note>. They ought to have consideration and kindness for the circumstances of every man.

“Among mankind, there are great differences also among the royal servants. Some are soldiers bearing arms, who repel every enemy of the king. Because of these soldiers, thieves, knaves, rogues, and pickpockets <note>original: "cut-purses"</note> are unable to create uproar and disturbances in the cities. Some are ministers, financiers, and secretaries, by whose means the administration of the country is carried on and the funds are collected for the army. Others are those who produce grain by cultivation and farming <term>husbandry</term> <note>Husbandry refers to the specialized management and cultivation of crops and animals.</note>. Some are judges and lawyers, who enforce the commands <note>original: "mandates"</note> of the law among mankind; for religion and law are necessary even for kings, so that their subjects may...”

<header># Number of Kings</header>

...do not go astray. Some are merchants and tradesmen who carry on the business of buying and selling in every country, and some are destined for service only, like slaves and domestics. In the same way, there are many other classes of people who are absolutely necessary for kings; without them, the business of the state would be interrupted. For this same reason, men require many chiefs so that these leaders in every city may apply themselves to the government and regulation of their respective peoples, ensuring that no kind of mischief may spring up.

> “And it is not possible for one king to carry on the government of all mankind. For in the whole world, there are many countries, and in each country there are thousands of cities in which hundreds of thousands <note>original: "lacs"; a South Asian unit of measurement equaling 100,000</note> of people live—each person’s language is different, and their religions are various. It is impossible for one man to conduct the government of all countries; consequently, God Almighty has set up many kings over them. All these monarchs call themselves the vicegerents <term>A person appointed by a ruler or deity to act as an administrative deputy or representative.</term> of God, for God has made them the possessors of their countries and the masters of their subjects <note>original: "slaves"</note>, so that they may busy themselves with the prosperity of their kingdom and provide effectively for the protection of His servants. It is intended that they should have compassion and show kindness toward the condition of every person, enforce the mandates of the law among mankind, and restrain them from everything which God has prohibited. Verily, He is the guardian of everyone. He creates all and gives food to all.”

# CHAPTER XXI.

## *Concerning the affairs of the King of the Flies.*

When the man stopped speaking, the King turned his thoughts to the animals. Presently, a gentle voice fell upon his ear. He perceived the king-bee <note>original: "Yásúb"; in the original Arabic context, the leader of the bees was thought to be a male "king" rather than a queen</note>, the monarch of the flies, flying before him and singing the praise and glory of God. The King asked who he was, and the answer was, "I am the king of the insects." 

The King then inquired why he had come personally, and why he had not—like the other animals—sent one of his subjects or a member of his army as his messenger and representative. He replied that he felt such care <note>original: "solicitude"</note> and compassion for his subjects' well-being that he wished to ensure no trouble would reach any one of them. The King remarked, "This virtue is not found in any other animal; why then do you possess it?" 

He said, "God Almighty, out of His great favor and mercy, bestowed this virtue upon me. Besides this, He has given to me many other honors and superior qualities <note>original: "excellences"</note>." The King commanded him to describe some of these qualities so that he might understand them as well.

He said, "God Almighty has given many blessings to me and to my ancestors, and has not allowed any other animal to share in them. For example, He has bestowed upon us the honor of kingship <note>original: "sovereignty"</note> and the office of the prophet, and has passed down this inheritance to my ancestors from generation to generation. He has not given these two blessings to any other animal. Besides this, God Almighty has taught us the—"

# The Bee.

"...blessings upon us, to what extent shall we show our gratitude? It is for this reason that, having consideration and kindness for my subjects, I have taken all this trouble upon myself and have not sent any one of them in my place."

When the bee finished speaking, the King cried, “Bravo! A hundred times bravo! You are very fluent and eloquent. It is true that God has bestowed these blessings on no animal besides you.” After this, he asked, “Where are your people and your armies <note>original: "troops"</note>?” 

He replied, “Upon the hills and mountains, and upon the trees; wherever they find a convenient place, there they dwell. Some have gone into the lands of men and have selected their houses for their homes.” The King inquired how they found safety <note>original: "security"</note> from the hands of men, and he said, “Many hide themselves from them and so escape; but whenever men get an opportunity, they harass <note>original: "annoy"</note> them. Often they even break their honeycombs, destroy their young, and take away the honey and eat it among themselves.”

The King asked why they patiently endured <note>original: "submitted patiently to"</note> man’s tyranny. He said, “We bear all this tyranny; but sometimes we are so distressed that we depart from his territory. Then men, to make peace with us, bring forward many tricks <note>original: "devices"</note>. They send us all kinds of presents: attar <note>original: "itr," a fragrant essential oil</note> of roses, perfumes, and so on; they beat drums and tambourines, and, in short, they satisfy us by giving us all sorts of presents and rare gifts. In our nature there is no malice <note>original: "malignity"</note> or evil; we make peace with them and return to their homes. But for all this, they are not satisfied; without proof or reason, they assert that they are masters and we are slaves.”

## CHAPTER XXII.

->*An account of the obedience shown by the Jinn to their kings and chiefs.*<-

After the King Bee asked the King how the Jinn <note>original: "Jins." In Islamic mythology, these are supernatural beings created from smokeless fire, often possessing free will like humans.</note> showed obedience to their sovereign and chiefs, and begged him to state the details, the King said, “They all carefully show their obedience and submission to their chief. Whatever command he issues, they obey.” 

The King Bee asked him to provide more specifics. The King said, “In the family of the Jinn, there are good and bad, believers and unbelievers, just as there are among men. Those who are good show obedience and submission to their chiefs to a degree that is impossible for man. Indeed, the obedience and loyalty <note>original: "subserviency"</note> of the Jinn is like that of the stars. 

Among the stars, the sun holds the status of king, and all the other celestial bodies stand in the position of soldiers and subjects. In this hierarchy, Mars is the commander-in-chief, Jupiter is the judge, Saturn is the treasurer, Mercury is the minister <note>original: "minister," likely referring to the role of a vizier or secretary</note>, Venus is the king's consort, the moon is the heir to the throne, and the other stars are like soldiers and subjects. They are all subject to the sun and move according to his motion; when he stops, they all stop as well, and they never violate their customs or their limits.”

The King Bee asked where the stars had acquired this perfection of obedience and regularity. The King said, “The stars obtained this excellence from the angels, who are all the soldiers of the Almighty and render obedience to Him.” The King Bee then asked, “Of what character...”

# Skill of the Bee

"...are acquainted with many sciences and arts, and we excel all animals in wisdom and judgment; we carefully provide for the affairs of both this world and the next. From this, it is obvious that we are the masters and the animals are our slaves.”

Men have learned the rules of sovereignty and government from us; for we station porters and guards at our homes so that no one can approach our king without permission. We extract honey from the leaves of trees, and once we have collected it, we sit comfortably in our houses and eat it with our young. Whatever remains of our leftovers, these men gather up and take for their own use.

No one has taught us these arts; they are revealed to us by the Almighty so that, without the help or aid of teachers, we are skilled in so many crafts. If men have the arrogance <note>original: "conceit"</note> to think they are masters and animals are their slaves, then why do they eat our leftovers? It is not the custom for kings to eat the scraps <term>orts</term> <note>original: "orts," meaning food scraps or leavings.</note> of slaves. There are many matters in which men need us, but there is not one in which we have any need for them. Therefore, they cannot win this claim without proof.

If this man would only look upon the actions of the ant..."

Having previously argued that insects possess architectural and political skills superior to humans, the speaker now provides specific examples of the innate intelligence found in ants, locusts, and silkworms to prove that animals are guided by divine wisdom rather than being mere "slaves" to humans.</meta>

<page-num>113</page-num>

# Skill of the Ant, etc.

...how, despite its tiny size, it constructs all kinds of winding dwellings under the ground, built so that no matter how great the flood might be, no water can ever enter. It collects and stores grain for food, and if any of it gets wet, it takes it out and dries it in the sun. It even tears off the husks and breaks in half any grains that are likely to sprout <note>original: "germinate"</note>, preventing them from growing and spoiling the store. 

During the hot season, many ants assemble in large caravans and search everywhere for food. If an ant discovers something too heavy to lift, it takes a small piece and goes to inform its group. Then, their leader takes a small portion as a sample to recognize it and goes to the site. Afterward, they all gather and, with great effort and labor, carry it away. If any ant is lazy about its work, the others beat it and drive it off. Thus, if a person would only reflect, they would realize the great skill <note>original: "science"</note> and intelligence the ant possesses.

"In the same way, the locust eats and drinks during the spring harvest. When she grows fat, she digs a hole in soft ground and lays her eggs. She then covers them with earth and flies away. When it is time for her to die, the birds eat her, or she perishes from extreme heat or cold. The following year, when spring returns and the weather turns mild, a larva <note>original: "little young one... like a maggot"</note> is produced from the egg. It creeps on the ground and feeds on the grass. Once its wings have developed, it eats and drinks until it grows large, then in the regular way, it lays its eggs and hides them in the ground. In this way, new generations are produced year after year.

"Similarly, silkworms mostly live in the trees on the hills, especially the mulberry tree. When they grow fat during the spring, they spin their silk <note>original: "mucus"</note> on the trees and sleep inside in perfect comfort. When they wake up, they lay their eggs in that same place,"

<vocab>ant, locust, silkworm, mulberry tree, science (organized knowledge), intelligence, germination (sprouting)</vocab>

<summary>The representative of the insects describes the complex behaviors and life cycles of ants, locusts, and silkworms as evidence of their innate intelligence and "science." He highlights the ant's ability to prevent grain from sprouting and the communal labor of their "caravans" as examples of wisdom that rivals human understanding.</summary>

<page-num>114</page-num>

<header>Parental Care of Insects.</header>

...and then they depart. The birds then devour them, or they die on their own from heat or cold. The cocoons remain in safety all year, and in the next year the young ones are produced from them, and these crawl about upon the trees. When they become plump and strong, they lay eggs and produce young ones in the same way.

> “Wasps form their combs upon walls and trees, and deposit their eggs and young ones there. But they do not store <note>original: "amass"</note> any food; day by day they seek their sustenance, and in the wintertime they hide in holes and crevices and die. Their skins remain there all winter, and do not decay or melt away. In the spring season, by the power of God, life again comes into them, and they, in their usual manner, build their dwellings and produce their young ones. <note>The author describes hibernation as a literal death and resurrection, reflecting historical biological understanding.</note>

> “Thus all insects, having brought forth their young, nourish them entirely out of tenderness and kindness, not with the expectation of services to be rendered by them. This is different indeed from men, who always entertain the hope of receiving kindness and benefit from their children. Generosity and liberality, which are characteristics of the great, are never to be found in them <note>In this context, "them" refers to the humans who act only for future reward.</note>; then what do they have to boast about over us? Again, flies, gnats, mosquitoes, and other insects which lay eggs, feed their young ones, and build houses, do not do so merely for their own benefit, but so that after their death other insects may come and find comfort. Because every one of them has a full certainty <note>original: "assurance"</note> of its own death, and when its days of life are complete, it passes away with resignation and cheerfulness. In the following year, God in His power reproduces them. Truly <note>original: "verily"</note>, these do not under any circumstances deny Him, as many men deny that there is a resurrection and day of judgment. If man could but understand these facts about the animals, and how greatly they excel him in their plans for this life and the next, he would not boast of being master and having the animals for slaves.”

<page-num>115</page-num>

### Speech of the Nightingale.

When the representative of the flies finished his speech, the King of the Jinn <note>original: "Jins"; supernatural spirits in Islamic mythology</note> was very pleased and praised him. Then, turning to the assembly of men, he said, “You have heard all that he has said; do you have any response left?” 

Then one of them, an Arab, said, “There are many virtues and good qualities in us by which our claim of superiority is established.” 

The King directed him to mention them. 

He said, “Our lives pass with great pleasure. Good things of every kind and sort are available for our food and drink, which are not even seen by animals. We eat the pulp and kernel of the fruit; they swallow the rind and the pit. Besides this, we eat sweetmeats, cakes, and confections<gloss>varieties of sweets and candies</gloss><note>The original text gives the names of several specific sweets, but they are not easily translatable into English.</note> in great variety. For the restoration of our health, we have dancing and music, laughter and merriment, tales and stories. We wear beautiful garments and jewels of all kinds, and we lay down for our use felt, tapestry, <term>druggets</term><note>coarse, heavy woolen fabrics used as floor coverings</note>, cloth, and many different sorts of carpets. Where can the animals get all these articles? They always eat the grass of the forest, and being quite naked by night and by day, they are subject to toil and labor like slaves. All these things are proofs that we are masters and they are slaves.”

The nightingale, the representative of the birds, was sitting in front upon the branch of a tree and addressed the King: “This man, who is boasting about the varieties of his food and drink, does not perceive that all these are in reality troubles and torments to him.” 

The King asked how that was and told him to explain. 

He said, “In this way: they endure many hardships and troubles for the sake of these comforts. Digging the ground, plowing, constructing bridges, irrigating with water; sowing, reaping, weighing, and grinding the grain; kindling fires in ovens, cooking, wrangling with butchers about meat, keeping accounts with shop—”

<summary>A human representative argues that human luxury and comfort prove their mastery over animals, but the Nightingale counters that these luxuries are actually burdens that require endless, grueling labor.</summary>

<page-num>116</page-num>

### Speech of the Nightingale.

...keepers, enduring hard work for the sake of making money, studying science and art, causing pain to their own bodies, traveling to distant countries, and standing with their hands clasped in the presence of noblemen just to earn a few coins <note>original: "two farthings"; a farthing was a British coin worth a quarter of a penny, used here to represent a tiny amount</note>. In short, with all this labor and effort, they collect wealth and property which, after their death, falls to others. If it was gained honestly, a satisfactory account can be given of it; but if it was not, it results in torture and torment.

“We are protected from all these pains and torments because our food is simply plants; we make use of whatever grows from the earth without labor or trouble. We eat the various fruits which God Almighty, in His power, has created for us, and we always give Him our thanks. Worrying and searching for food and drink never enters our minds. Wherever we go, by the grace of God, everything we need is available. But these men are always twisting in anxiety about their food. And because they consume many different kinds of food, they suffer many pains and torments, and are prone to chronic diseases: depression <note>original: "vapours"; an archaic term for various physical or mental ailments thought to be caused by internal gases</note>, headaches, cholera, paralysis <note>original: "palsy"</note>, facial nerve pain <note>original: "tic-doloreux"; trigeminal neuralgia</note>, chills and fever <note>original: "ague"</note>, coughs, jaundice, fever, wasting fever <note>original: "hectic fever"</note>, boils, pimples, itching, ringworm, scrofula <note>original: "scrofula"; a form of tuberculosis affecting the lymph nodes of the neck</note>, stomach cramps, diarrhea, gonorrhea, elephantiasis, and polyps <note>original: "polypus"</note>—in short, they are subject to diseases of all kinds. For medicine and remedies <note>original: "physic"</note>, they run to the houses of doctors; yet despite all this, they have the nerve to declare, ‘We are the masters; the animals are our slaves.’”

The man replied, “Sickness is not unique to us; animals are also subject to most diseases.” 

The bird retorted, saying, “When animals fall sick, it is entirely through their association and closeness with you. Dogs, cats, pigeons, chickens, and other animals who are kept in captivity by you do not receive their natural food and drink; consequently, they fall sick. Those animals who roam freely in the wilds <note>original: "jungles"</note> are protected from every disease because their times for eating and drinking are regular, and they never eat too little or too much...”

# Luxuries of Men

[God taught] them how to till the ground, to sow, grind, cook, and to make garments. In short <note>original: "In fine"</note>, day and night they were subject to these troubles and labors. When many children were born to them and began to live everywhere, whether in woods or fields, then evil deeds began against the animals dwelling on the earth. Their homes were seized, some were made prisoners, and some fled. Making all sorts of snares and nets, humans pursued them in order to capture and confine them. At length, matters have reached such a point that now you stand there boasting, elaborating <note>original: "expatiating"</note> on your dignity, and ready for contending and quarreling.

> “And when you say that you have parties of pleasure, that you are occupied in music and dancing, and pass your days in luxury and enjoyment; that you wear fine clothes and jewels of all sorts, and that you have many things besides these which we cannot get—this is all true; but for every one of these things, you have an opposite of pain and torment from which we are exempt. Thus, instead of parties of pleasure, you sit in the house of mourning; instead of happiness, you endure grief; instead of music and laughter, you weep and feel pain; instead of fine houses, you sleep in the dark grave; instead of jewels, you wear a collar on your neck, shackles <note>original: "fetters"</note> on your hands, and chains upon your legs; and instead of praise, you are subjected to mockery <note>original: "derision"</note>. In short, for every joy you feel a corresponding sorrow. But we are exempt from all these evils, because these are the troubles and pains that belong to slaves and miserable creatures.

> “Instead of your cities and houses, we have this boundless plain for our use. From earth to sky, wherever our hearts desire, we fly. We pick and eat the green plants <note>original: "verdure"</note> on the banks of the rivers without any hindrance; free from toil and labor, we eat our suitable food and drink pure water. There is no one to forbid us; we have no need of ropes or buckets, bottles or pitchers—all these things are required for...

# Religious Duties of Men

...you, so that you may take them on your shoulders, carry them about from place to place, and sell the water. You are always <note>original: "ever"</note> liable to labor and trouble; these are all distinctive marks of slaves. How, then, is it proved that you are the masters and we are the slaves?”

The King then asked the representative of the men if he had any other answer left. He replied, “We have many virtues <note>original: "excellences"</note> and distinctions which testify to our claim.” The King told him to state them. A Persian <note>original: "Persian^1"</note> <margin>The printed text says *Ibrání* (a Hebrew), but it is obvious that the speaker is a Muslim, probably an *Irání* or Persian.</margin> among them said, “God Almighty has bestowed upon us distinctions of many kinds—religion, prophecy, and the revealed Word—all these blessings He has given to us. Having made known to us what is lawful and unlawful, good and evil, He has restricted this knowledge to us in order to bring us into paradise. Ritual washing <note>original: "Ablution"</note>, purification, prayer, fasting, sacrifice, almsgiving, saying prayers in the mosques, preaching in pulpits, and many other acts of devotion He has taught us. All these distinctions afford evidence that we are masters and they are the slaves.”

The representative of the birds then said, “If you were to reflect and consider, you would find that these things are actually burdens and hardships for you.” The King inquired, “What kind of trouble?” The bird said, “God Almighty has established all these modes of worship so that their sins may be forgiven and so that they may not go astray. Thus He says in the Quran <note>original: "Kurán"</note>, ‘Good deeds drive away <note>original: "prevent"</note> evil ones.’ <note>original: Kurán, Súrat xi. v. 116. (Quran, Chapter 11, Verse 114 in modern numbering)</note> If they do not act according to the rules of the law, then they are disgraced in the sight <note>original: "estimation"</note> of God. Out of this fear, they remain occupied in devotion. We are pure from sin and have no need of the devotion of which they boast. And God Almighty has sent prophets to those people, although...”

<summary>In this debate, humans argue that their religious laws and rituals prove their superiority, while the animals counter that these duties are actually burdens imposed to correct human sinfulness.</summary>

# Religious Duties of Men

...they are non-believers <note>original: "infidels"</note>, idolaters, and sinners who do not perform worship to Him, but are occupied night and day in debauchery and wickedness. The prophets and messengers of God are like physicians and astrologers. These people find the need for physicians when they are sick and ill, and they entreat the astrologers to tell them of things that are unlucky <note>original: "inauspicious"</note> and ill-fated.

> “Ritual washing <note>original: "Ablution"</note> and purification have been made duties for you because you are always unclean. Night and day you pass your time in adultery and <unclear>. . . . .</unclear> and your bodies have a foul odor; hence the command of purification for you. We abstain from these things. During the whole year, we approach each other only once, and that is not for lust and gratification; we do the deed for the sole object of continuing our species. Prayer and fasting are duties for you for this reason: so that through them your sins may be pardoned. We do not commit sin; then why should these be duties for us? If you spent it on the poor and needy, then why would alms-giving be made a duty for you? We take compassion and pity upon our fellows and never accumulate anything through greed <note>original: "avarice"</note>.

> “When you say that God Almighty has revealed to you texts of the Quran <note>original: "Kurán"</note> relating to things lawful and unlawful, to criminal laws <note>original: "penal laws"</note> and the laws of retaliation <note>original: "laws of retaliation", or *Qisas*, the legal principle of equal retribution</note>, all this was for your instruction because your hearts are dark. Through stupidity and ignorance, you do not know what is beneficial and what is harmful <note>original: "injurious"</note>; consequently, you require teachers and masters. But God Almighty makes all things known to us without the intervention of prophets, as He himself says in the Quran,

# *Garments of Men*

"...jungles like slaves. All these gifts and blessings of God are proofs of our domination; it is right that we should exercise lordly authority over them and do with them as we please—they are all our slaves.”

The King asked the animals if they had any answer to give to this. The jackal, the ambassador of the predatory animals <note>original: "rapacious beasts"</note>, said to the man:

> “As you are boasting so much about your fine and soft clothing, tell me this: Where were all these different garments in the first age? Perhaps you took them by tyranny and violence from the animals.” The man asked what time he was speaking about. He said, “Of all the garments that you possess, the finest and softest are the brocades and the silks. These are made of raw silk, and that comes from the secretion <note>original: "mucus"</note> of a worm. This worm does not belong to the human race <note>original: "race of Adam"</note>, but is a species of insect which, for its own protection, spins a web with its secretion on the trees so that it may be sheltered from the evils of cold and heat. By force and tyranny, you have torn it from these insects. Consequently, God has made you subject to this punishment: you have to take it and, with great labor, spin and weave it, and then have it sewn by tailors and washed by laundrymen. Indeed, you suffer so much trouble and labor over it, and you must keep and sell it so carefully, that you are always writhing in anxiety about it.

> “So there are other garments which you weave from the fur and hair of many animals, and your finest garments are generally made from the wool of animals; with force and violence, you tear them from the animals and then take them for yourselves. To make such a boast of this is improper. If we were to boast of it, it would be fitting for us, because God Almighty produced it on our bodies so that we might make it our covering and dress. He, out of His compassion and kindness, bestowed this dress upon us so that we might be protected from cold and heat. God Almighty brings forth this dress upon our bodies at the time when we are born, and of His kindness...”
