# *Adam and Eve*

“God places everything within our power, without any labor or trouble on our part. But you humans, until your dying breath, are always caught up in trouble over your needs. Your ancestors rebelled against God, and as a punishment <note>original: "requital"</note> for that, you are subjected to this hardship.”

The King said to the jackal, “Tell us the facts about the beginning of the human race <note>original: "race of Adam"</note>.” 

The jackal replied, “When God created Adam and Eve, He provided food and covering for them just as He did for the animals. Consequently, those two lived in the East on the Ruby Mountains <note>In medieval Islamic geography, the "Ruby Mountain" was often identified as a peak in Serendib (modern Sri Lanka) where Adam was said to have landed after being cast out of Paradise.</note>, near the equator. At the time He created them, they were quite naked, but their entire bodies were covered with the hair from their heads, and by means of this very hair, they were protected from the cold and heat. They used to stroll about the garden and eat the fruit of every tree; they suffered no kind of toil or labor like what these people are now forced to endure. 

The divine command was that they were to eat the fruit of the whole garden, with the exception of one tree which they were not to approach. Through the deception <note>original: "beguiling"</note> of Satan, they disregarded God’s instruction. Then they lost all their superiority; the hair on their heads fell off, and they became completely naked. The angels, by divine command, expelled them from that place, in the way that the scholar <note>original: "doctor"</note> of the Jinn <note>original: "Jins"; supernatural spirits in Islamic mythology</note> described the circumstances at length in an earlier chapter.”

When the representative of the predatory animals <note>original: "rapacious beasts"</note> had spoken, a man said, “O beasts, it is not right or proper for you to speak in our presence; it is better for you to be silent.” 

The jackal asked, “For what reason?” 

The man said, “Because among the animals there is no one more wicked and vicious than you; no animal is more hard-hearted, and none so greedy to devour rotting flesh <note>original: "carrion"</note>. You are good for nothing but to harass animals, and you are always intent on their death and destruction.” 

The jackal asked, “How so?”

# Slaughter of Men

...us, it is clear that you benefit from our skins and fur. Even the birds of prey that you keep in captivity <note>original: "bonds"</note> hunt for you and provide you with food. But tell us, what benefit do the animals receive from you? The harm you do is obvious, for you slaughter them and eat their meat. Yet your stinginess toward us is so great that you bury your dead in the earth specifically so we cannot eat them.

“You claim that predatory <note>original: "rapacious"</note> beasts kill and ravage other animals, but those beasts only adopted this practice <note>original: "usage"</note> after seeing you do the same. For example, Rustam, Isfandiyár, Jamshíd, Zuhák, Farídún, Afrásiyáb, Minúchihr, Dárá, and Iskandar <note>These are legendary and historical kings and heroes from Persian tradition and the *Shahnameh* (Book of Kings). If wild beasts were not better than you, why would your holy and devout men seek them out? Pious and disciplined <note>original: "temperate"</note> people do not seek out the wicked; instead, they flee far from them. This proves that wild beasts are better than you.”

# Forbearance of Animals <note>original: "Beasts"</note>

> “Another proof is this: when your tyrannical kings have any doubt about the integrity <note>original: "probity"</note> and purity of any man, they drive him out into the jungle. If the wild animals do not tear him to pieces, the kings understand from that fact that he is righteous and devout. This is because every species recognizes its own kind, and the wild animals, knowing him to be righteous, do not bother him. It is a true saying that ‘One saint recognizes another.’ Truly, there are bad and vicious individuals among the wild animals, but why is this? Are there not good and bad individuals in every species? Even the animals that are bad do not tear the good and righteous to pieces; instead, they devour evil men. Thus, God Almighty declares, ‘I make oppressors <note>original: "tyrants"</note> prevail over other oppressors, so that they may receive the punishment <note>original: "recompense"</note> for their sins.’”¹

When the ambassador of the predatory <note>original: "rapacious"</note> animals finished this speech, a philosopher belonging to the company of the Jinn <note>Supernatural spirits in Islamic mythology, often depicted as being capable of good or evil.</note> said, “He speaks the truth. People who are good avoid the bad and associate with the good, even if they belong to a different species; likewise, those who are bad flee from the good to associate with the wicked. If humans were not evil and vicious, why would their devout and holy men go to live in the wilds and mountains? Why would they grow fond of <note>original: "conceive affection for"</note> wild animals there, even though they belong to a different species? While there is no obvious physical similarity between them, they must surely be partners in their good qualities.” 

All the assembly of Jinn said, “He speaks the truth—there is no doubt or uncertainty about it.” The humans on every side, hearing this criticism and disapproval <note>original: "condemnation and censure"</note>, were greatly ashamed and hung their heads. Meanwhile, evening arrived, the court was dismissed, and everyone took their leave and went to their own homes.

# CHAPTER XXIII

## *Disputation between Man and the Parrot*

In the morning, all the men and animals attended the court of justice. # Skill of Insects

To create lines and circles, it has no need for a ruler or compasses, while men require materials and tools. There is also the spider, which is the weakest of all insects, but which possesses greater skill in spinning and weaving than all of their human weavers. In the first place, it draws out a thread from its saliva; afterwards, having formed it into a kind of ladder, it firmly attaches it to something above and keeps open a small space in the center for the purpose of capturing flies. In this work of art, it has no need of materials, whereas human weavers cannot weave anything without cloth.

> "Similarly, there are silkworms, which are very feeble but possess greater knowledge and skill than *human* workmen. When they have eaten and are satisfied, they come to their dwelling place. Then, first, they spin fine lines from their saliva; these they afterwards attach to something overhead and make firm and strong, so that neither wind nor water can get into it, and here, according to their custom, they sleep. They have acquired all this skill without the teaching of parents or masters; neither do they require needles or thread, yet *human* tailors and menders can do nothing without them. The swallows also build their nests hanging in the air under the eaves and require neither ladders nor anything of the kind to get up to them. So also the white ant <note>original: "white ant"; a historical term for a termite</note> constructs her house without mud or water, requiring nothing for the work.
> 
> "In short, all beasts and birds make houses and nests and support their offspring. They have greater intelligence and skill than man. For instance, there is the ostrich, which is related to both birds and beasts. With what care she <note>The original text represents the male ostrich as doing this, though the translation uses female pronouns.</note> provides for the nourishment of her young! # Dullness of Children

A mother bird hatches many young; to sustain them, she digs in the ground, picks out worms, and feeds her offspring. But among humanity, no woman feeds her children in this way. Nurses and midwives attend to them instead. As soon as they are taken from the womb and are born, these helpers bathe and wash them, wet nurses <note>term: a woman employed to suckle and care for another's child</note> feed them, and they are rocked in cradles—they do everything, while the mother of the child pays no attention to it.

"Their children are also excessively dull <note>original: "stupid"; here meaning lacking in natural instinct or slow to learn</note>; they know nothing at all of what is beneficial or what is harmful. Only after they reach fifteen or twenty years of age do they arrive at the years of discretion <note>term: the age at which a person is considered legally and morally capable of making their own decisions</note>. Even then, they still require masters and teachers; they spend all their days reading and writing, and after all that, they remain the biggest fools in existence. 

"But when our young are born, they are aware of what is good and what is bad from the very first moment. For example, the young of chickens, partridges, and quails begin pecking and running around as soon as they come out of the egg; if anyone tries to catch them, they run away. This knowledge and intelligence has been granted to them by God Almighty, so that they may know what is helpful and what is harmful. 

"The reason for this is that among those birds, the male and female do not help each other feed their young as pigeons and other birds do; among pigeons, the cock and the hen work together to feed their offspring. It is for this reason that God has bestowed such intelligence on young chickens that they do not depend on their parents for support, but instead peck and feed themselves. They do not need to be supplied with milk or fed grain like the young of other animals and birds. So, who is held in higher regard by God Almighty? Night and day, we are occupied in prayer and praise to Him, and consequently, He has shown all this compassion for our condition.

They make statements about some distant country long in advance so that people may think they speak the truth and may have faith in them. # Astrologers

...[as do] rebels against God; such as the tyrannical and violent kings among men who deny there is any future state and who do not believe in destiny—Nimrod <note>A biblical and Quranic figure described as a great king and a rebel against God</note> and Pharaoh, for instance. These kings, acting on the reports of the astrologers, had hundreds, even thousands of children put to death. They used to believe that the regulation of the world rested upon the seven planets <note>In ancient and medieval astronomy, the "seven planets" were the Sun, Moon, Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn</note> and the twelve signs of the zodiac. They did not know that nothing happens without the command of the Lord, who made the constellations and the planets. It is true that:

->No plans can prevail against fate.<- <note>original: "'Gainst fate no plans prevail."</note>

After all, what God willed came to pass.

The history of this matter is that the astrologers had informed Nimrod that a child would be born during his reign who, after being raised, would attain a high position and overthrow the religion of the idol-worshippers <note>original: "idolaters"</note>. When he asked them at what place and in what family the child would be born, and where he would be raised, they could not tell him. So they said to the king, "Give orders that all children born during this year shall be killed"; their idea being that the child would be killed among them. At length, God Almighty created Abraham, the friend of God <note>original: "the friend of God"; a translation of the title "Khalil Allah," traditionally given to Abraham in Islamic tradition</note>, and protected him from the malice of the unbelievers <note>original: "infidels"</note>. Pharaoh dealt out this same kind of treatment to the children of Israel; but here also God Almighty kept the patriarch Moses safe from his malice. 

In short, the predictions of astrologers are all myths; fate is never to be escaped, and yet you boast and say that you have astrologers and wise men in your race. These men are only fit to deceive sinners, and those who put their trust in God pay no attention to their words.

When the parrot had reached this point in his speech, the King asked him, "If preventing evil through the stars is impossible, then why do astrologers study them and try to prove their influence with evidence? And why do they fear them?"

# Predestination

...is certainly possible, but not in the way the astrologers claim. He replied, “By following the commands of the law, by weeping and mourning, by prayer, by fasting, by making offerings and giving charity <note>original: "alms"</note>, and by worshipping with a sincere heart—this is the way to seek help from God. Whenever anyone begs God to prevent the unlucky influence of the stars <note>original: "the inauspicious influences of the stars"</note>, God surely protects him. The astrologers and fortune-tellers <note>original: "soothsayers"</note> provide information before events happen, saying that Almighty God will bring a certain event to pass. But in this matter, is it not better to pray to the Lord to prevent it from happening, rather than acting according to the rules of astrology?”

The King said, “If following the commands of the law can turn away evil, it must follow that what God has predestined <note>predestination: the doctrine that God has ordained all that will happen from the beginning of time</note> can be set aside.” 

He replied, “Divine predestination does not change. However, when people offer their prayers <note>original: "supplications"</note> to God to prevent an outcome, He protects them from that event. For example, when the astrologers told Nimrod that a child would be born who would oppose the religion of idol-worshippers <note>original: "idolaters"</note> and throw all his subjects and his army into chaos, they were referring to Abraham, the friend of God. Once Almighty God created him, He used Abraham to humble and destroy Nimrod and his army. 

But if Nimrod had prayed to God for safety at that time, then the Almighty, in His mercy, would have led him to the religion of Abraham. He and his army would then have been saved from disgrace and destruction. Similarly, when the astrologers announced the birth of Moses to Pharaoh, if Pharaoh had offered his prayers to God for safety, God would have brought him into Moses's religion as well, protecting him from disgrace. This is the same way the Almighty saved Pharaoh’s wife <note>In Islamic tradition, Pharaoh's wife Asiya is considered a righteous believer who was saved by God</note> and granted her His blessing..."

<vocab>predestination, astrology, astrologers, Nimrod, Abraham, Pharaoh, Moses, supplications, law</vocab>

The King said, “It is true—the study of the stars, the prediction of events before they happen, and prayer to God for the prevention of those evils—these all are things whose benefits are well understood. For this reason, Moses gave this warning <note>original: "admonition"</note> to the children of Israel, saying, ‘When you are in dread of any calamity, then pray to God with earnest supplication and mourning, because He, through the sincerity of your prayers, will preserve you from that occurrence.’ Beginning from Adam and coming down to the time of Muhammad the Pure (may the blessing and peace of God be on him!), this practice has always prevailed: for them to give this direction to their followers when any accident befell them. Therefore, it is proper for us to act in this way regarding the influences <note>original: "behests"</note> of the stars, and not as astrologers in these days who delude people into forsaking God and flying to the movements <note>original: "revolutions"</note> of the skies for direction.

“For the restoration of health to the sick, one should also first turn to God, for a perfect recovery may be obtained from His grace and kindness. Some men, at the beginning of their illness, apply to physicians, but receiving no benefit from their remedies, they betake themselves to God in their despair. ### Philosophy

While some argue for the eternal pre-existence <note>original: "kadim"—ancient or eternal</note> of matter, others offer proofs for the priority of form. Some of them say that there are two primary causes, some maintain that there are three, others are convinced that there are four, while some say that there are five, and others increase them to six or even seven. Some are certain of the co-existence of the Creator and the created. Some say that the world is infinite, and others provide evidence of its being finite. Some acknowledge a resurrection, while others deny it. Some believe in prophecy and inspiration, but others object. Some are bewildered and dizzy with doubt, others reject all reason and evidence, and others are convinced that all is a delusion. Besides these, there are many other diverse beliefs to which they are all bound.

“But our religion and path are one. We acknowledge God to be One, and without equal. Night and day we are occupied in His worship and praise. We do not boast over any of His creations; whatever is destined for us, we are thankful for. We do not reject His authority, nor do we ask the why or wherefore of anything, as some men do when they complain about His commands, His will, and His craftsmanship.

“Then as for the mathematicians and geometricians whom you brag about, they perplex themselves night and day pondering mathematical proofs <note>original: "demonstrations"</note>. They make claims about things which are beyond what can be conceived or imagined, though they do not realize it. They show no interest in those sciences which are suitable for them, and instead pursue useless trifles which are quite unnecessary. Some direct their attention to the measurement of physical bodies and distances; some are preoccupied with determining the heights of mountains and clouds; some go about surveying rivers and forests, and some study and labor to discover the system of the heavens and the center of the earth. They take no notice of the functioning <note>original: "physiology"—at this time, referring to the study of the internal workings of nature or the human body</note> or measurement of their own bodies."

<summary>This page critiques the conflicting theories of worldly philosophers and the abstract measurements of scientists, contrasting them with the simple, unified, and humble devotion of the author's religious faith.</summary>

->Physicians. 139<-

They do not know how many internal organs <note>original: "entrails"</note> and intestines they have, what space there is in the chest cavity, or the condition of the heart and brain. They do not know the nature of the stomach, the shape of the bones, or the structure of the joints. They never learn anything about these things, though they are easy to understand and right to investigate. Yet it is through these things that the skill and power of the Almighty are discovered; for, as the Prophet says, "He who knows himself, knows God." Despite all this stupidity and ignorance, they rarely read the Holy Scripture <note>original: "Divine Word"</note> and do not heed the decrees of God's law or the commands of the Prophet.

"The doctors you brag about are not needed by you until you fall sick from eating improper food because of your greed and gluttony. Then you go and wait at their doors with your <term>urine bottles</term> <note>Vessels used to collect samples for medical diagnosis, a standard practice in pre-modern medicine.</note>. A man who goes to the door of the doctor or pharmacist <note>original: "druggist"</note> is sick, just as the crowds who wait upon astrologers are unlucky and unfortunate. But in the end, going to them only makes them more miserable, because astrologers have no power to speed up or delay favorable or unfavorable moments. Notwithstanding this, many astrologers and <term>necromancers</term> <note>People who claim to communicate with the dead or spirits to predict the future.</note> take paper and write nonsense on it to trick their victims.

The same is true of doctors, for sickness is only made worse by going to them with your complaints. They recommend avoiding the very things that often cure sick people. If people would leave it all to nature, the sick man would soon get well. So, boasting about your astrologers and doctors is pure folly. We have no need for them because our diet is of one simple kind. Because of the simplicity of our diet, we do not fall sick; we do not go to the houses of doctors with our complaints, nor do we have any need for <term>draughts</term> <note>Liquid medicinal doses or potions.</note> and <term>electuaries</term> <note>Medicinal pastes made by mixing powders with honey or syrup.</note>. The path of the free is to have no need for anyone, but the way of slaves is to run about to everyone's house."

### Merchants

"The merchants, architects, and farmers that you brag about are worse than slaves and more miserable even than the poor and needy. Night and day they are consumed by toil and labor, and cannot take a moment’s rest. They are constantly building houses, yet they do not live in them themselves. They till the earth and plant trees, but they do not eat the fruit they produce. There are no greater fools than these, for they amass riches and property only to leave them to their heirs, while they themselves live in a state of constant deprivation.

"The merchants are also always intent on collecting ill-gotten gains. They buy grain and hoard it, hoping it will become expensive <note>original: "dear"</note>; then, during times of famine, they sell it at high prices, giving nothing to the poor and needy. Suddenly, the wealth they spent so long gathering is destroyed; it sinks into the sea, thieves carry it off, or some tyrant king seizes it. Then, reduced to misery and disgrace, they go begging from door to door, and the rest of their lives is lost to homelessness <note>original: "vagrancy"</note>. They think they have gained some advantage, but they do not see that they have squandered their most valuable possession—their precious lives—for nothing. They have traded the afterlife for this world, and have not even received what they paid for. They have thrown religion to the winds; they are torn apart by doubts and have found only delusion, but no peace. If you boast about this supposed advantage, then we curse it.

"You claim that your people are kind-hearted, but you are mistaken. For the friends, relatives, and neighbors of these people go about poor and needy, naked and hungry, begging from street to street, and these men ignore their plight. Do they call this 'humanity'—to sit at home in ease and take their pleasure while their friends, relatives, and neighbors live as beggars <note>original: "mendicancy"</note>? You also say that you have writers and tax accountants <note>original: "revenue accountants"</note> among your people, but it is very foolish of you to..."

# *Devotees*

They boast about them, for in the whole world there are no men more wicked and dishonest. They are constantly engaged in the effort to overthrow their rivals through cunning, trickery, insults, and false charm <note>original: "plausibility"</note>. In public, they write friendly letters in a very elaborate and fancy style, but in their hearts they are always focused on their plans to destroy the very foundation of their competitors. Day and night they are consumed by schemes to remove someone from his office and to secure a bribe <note>original: "douceur," a French term for a gratuity or sweetener</note> for the appointment of another; and so, through fraud and deception, they get the first man dismissed.

> "As for the devotees and worshippers whom you, in your self-satisfaction <note>original: "complacency"</note>, consider righteous, and whose prayers and pleas <note>original: "intercessions"</note> you believe are acceptable to God: these men have deceived you by putting on an outward show of devotion and piety. This public worship of theirs—the growing of their beards, the shaving of their upper lips, the wearing of hermit's robes, their feigned satisfaction with coarse clothing, their wearing of rags with patch upon patch, their remaining silent and refusing to speak to anyone, their lecturing others on morals and teaching the requirements of religious law, their performing of such long prayers that their foreheads are bruised <note>original: "indented" from prostration</note>, their eating so little that their lips hang limp, their shriveled brains, their skeletal <note>original: "emaciated"</note> bodies, and their pale complexions—all of this, from beginning to end, is entirely fraudulent and false. Their hearts are so full of bitterness and resentment <note>original: "rancour"</note> that they refuse to acknowledge the existence of anyone but themselves. They are always criticizing God, asking, 'Why did He create the devil and Satan? Why were those who frequent prostitutes <note>original: "whoremongers"</note> and adulterers allowed to exist? And why does He provide them with food?' Such talk is improper, and their hearts are filled with similar demonic <note>original: "Satanic"</note> ideas. In your eyes they seem good, but in the sight of God, none are worse. Why do you brag about them? They are a disgrace and a shame to you."

They demand forced service from the people of God and consider themselves superior to all other men. They prefer this world to the next. In their greed for worldly power, they kill their own friends and brothers. These are not the traits of great men. To brag about those kings and lords is harmful to yourselves; to claim authority over us without evidence or reason is nothing but trickery and deceit from start to finish."

# CHAPTER XXIV.

## *The White Ant.*

When the parrot had finished speaking, the King looked toward the assembly of Jins <term>Jins: supernatural spirits in Islamic and Middle Eastern mythology, often called genies</term> and men, and said, "How is it that the white ant <note>original: "white ant," an old name for a termite</note>, although he has no hands or feet, gathers earth and builds his vaulted home over his body? Explain the facts of this to me." 

A person from the Hebrew group said, "The Jins raise the earth for this insect because these insects performed a great service for them by eating through the staff of Solomon. When it broke, he fell down; the Jins, realizing he was dead, fled from that place and were released from their troubles and hardships."¹

The King asked the Jin sages whether they knew anything about the matter this person had mentioned, and they all replied, "How can we say that the Jins gather the earth and water and give them to this insect? For even if this insect did show the Jins that kindness which this person—

<note>¹ This requires explanation. Solomon, using his supernatural power, forced the Jins to labor in the construction of the Temple. Having been warned of his approaching death, and knowing that the Jins would stop working if they knew he was gone, he prayed that his death might be hidden from them. A staff was miraculously provided for him; leaning upon it while praying in his private cell in the Temple, he died. The staff supported his body, so the Jins believed he was still alive and continued working. Meanwhile, a white ant (termite) slowly ate through the staff. Because the wood was very thick and strong, the staff lasted for a year. When it was finally eaten through, Solomon’s body fell down, and his death was discovered. However, by that time, the Jins had finished their work, and the Temple was complete. See the *Quran* <note>original: "Kurán"</note>, Chapter <note>original: "Súrat"</note> 34; *Tabari*, chapter 97.</note>

A Greek sage said to the King, “One reason for this is known to me.” The King told him to explain, and he said— “The structure of the white ant <note>original: "white ant," an old term for a termite</note> is wonderful and marvelous; its temperament is very cold, and the joints <note>original: "articulations"</note> and pores of its whole body are constantly open. The air which enters into its body is condensed by the extreme cold and becomes water; this oozes <note>original: "exudes"</note> onto the surface of its body. The dirt which falls on its body, being formed into mud, hardens <note>original: "coagulates"</note>. Having collected this, it builds the house over its body for its protection, so that it may be safe from every danger. Its two lips are very sharp, with which it bites fruit, leaves, and wood, and bores holes into bricks and stones.”

The King said to the locust— “The white ant belongs to the insects, and you are the representative of the insects; explain then what this Greek doctor has said.” The locust replied— “He has spoken the truth, but he has not given a complete description of the ant; there is something yet left to say.” The King desired him to explain it fully, and he then said— “When God Almighty made all His creatures and bestowed His blessings upon each one of them, in His wisdom and justice He kept them all equal. To some He gave a body and figure large and heavy, but He made their spirit very lowly and submissive <note>original: "mean and despicable"</note>. To others He gave forms small and frail, but He made their intelligence sharp and wise; thus the excess or lack on either side was balanced. For instance, there is the elephant, who, in spite of his huge form, is so humble in spirit that he is obedient to a child, who climbs upon his shoulders and drives him wherever <note>original: "whither"</note> he will. And there is the camel, who, although his neck and figure are...”

<meta>This page continues the conversation between the King and the locust regarding the nature of small insects versus large animals, focusing on the "white ant" (termite) and the bee.</meta>

# Skill of Insects

...[the elephant], though very large <note>original: "very long," likely referring to the elephant's massive size or lifespan</note>, is still such a fool that he follows the lead of whoever seizes his nose-cord <note>A rope passed through an animal's nose to control it</note>; even a mouse might drive him if it wished. Then there is the scorpion, which is small in size, yet when it stings the elephant, it kills him. In the same way, this insect which they call the white ant <note>termite</note>, although it is very tiny and very feeble, is nonetheless very strong-willed. In short, all those insects which are small are sharp-witted and wise.

The King asked the reason why all the large-bodied animals were stupid, and the little creatures sharp. "Where," said he, "is the divine wisdom in this?" 

The locust replied, "The Creator, by His infinite knowledge <note>original: "omniscience"</note>, perceived that animals of large body were capable of inflicting pain and trouble. Thus, if a high spirit had been given to them, they would never have submitted to anyone; and if the little creatures had not been sharp-witted and wise, they would always have been in trouble and difficulty. Therefore, He gave to the former a submissive <note>original: "abject"</note> spirit, and to the latter a keen intelligence." 

The King directed him to explain this more fully, and he replied, "The chief excellence of every art is this: that the skill of the artist and his methods should not be known to anyone. For example, there is the bee <note>original: "fly"</note>, who in her house constructs all kinds of angles and circles without a ruler or a compass, and nobody knows how she does it, or from where she brings the wax and honey. If her body had been large, this skill of hers would have been revealed.

"Then, again, there is the silkworm, whose method of spinning and weaving no one has discovered. It is the same case with the white ant, for the exact way in which she builds her house has never been revealed, nor is it even known how she lifts the dirt and works. Scholars of philosophy <note>original: "Doctors of philosophy"</note> deny that the existence of the world is possible without matter. God Almighty has made the skill of the bee a proof to refute this—"

<summary>The locust explains to the King that God gave small insects intelligence for survival while making large animals docile to prevent them from becoming uncontrollable. He uses the mysterious skills of bees, silkworms, and termites as examples of divine artistry that defies human understanding.</summary>

-># Powers of Insects<-

...of this, because she, without materials <note>original: "without matter," implying she seems to create her wax from nothing</note>, builds her house of wax and collects her food from honey. If she gathers it from the middle of the water or the air, then if they are so sharp-sighted, why do they not find out how she collects it and build dwellings for themselves?

"In the same way, God has made creatures with tiny bodies to serve as proofs of His power and skill to tyrannical kings who have rebelled and turned away from Him. For instance, a gnat killed Nimrod <note>A biblical and legendary king often portrayed as a tyrant who challenged God</note>, although the gnat is the smallest of all insects. When Pharaoh chose a wicked course and rebelled against the patriarch Moses, God Almighty sent an army of locusts, which went to his country and brought great distress upon him. 

So, when God bestowed on Solomon both the royal and the prophetic office, and made all the <term>Jinn</term> <note>original: "Jins"; supernatural spirits in Islamic mythology, created from smokeless fire</note> and all mankind subject to him, there were some evil persons who doubted his status as a prophet. They declared that he had obtained his sovereignty through fraud and trickery. Although Solomon told them that God, in His grace and bounty, had granted him this distinction, the doubt was still not removed from their hearts. 

At length, God sent the <term>white ant</term> <note>termite</note>, who went and ate through Solomon’s staff. He then fell down in his prayer cell; until that moment, neither man nor <term>Jinn</term> had the courage to face him <note>According to legend, Solomon died while leaning on his staff, and because he remained upright, the Jinn—thinking he was still alive and watching them—continued their forced labor until a termite ate the wood and the body fell</note>. This exercise of divine power was shown as a warning to those people who boast of their own size and dignity. For although they see all His arts and all His powers, they still take no warning from them, and instead boast about kings who are powerless even against the smallest of our worms.

"The <term>pearl oyster</term> <note>original: "shell (pearl-oyster)"</note> in which pearls are produced is smaller in size and more fragile than any other sea creature, but in wisdom and knowledge, she is wiser and more sensible than them all. Obtaining her food and nourishment in the depths of the sea, she lives there; but on rainy days she..."

<summary>The locust argues that the mysterious skills of small insects like bees, and their roles in humbling tyrants like Nimrod and Pharaoh, prove God's supreme power. The text also recounts the legend of Solomon's death, revealed only when a termite ate through his staff.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the locust’s speech to the King regarding the miraculous skills of small creatures, specifically transitioning from the bee to the pearl oyster, as a way to humble human arrogance.</meta>

<page-num>147</page-num>
The Pearl Oyster.

...comes up from the bottom and positions herself on the surface of the water. She has two large ears <note>In the context of shells, this likely refers to the "auricles" or the valves of the oyster shell</note> which she opens, and when a drop of rain falls into one, she instantly closes it so that the salt water does not mix with the rain-drop. Afterward, she returns to the bottom of the sea and for a period keeps the two drops of rain shut away until the water is transformed <note>original: "decomposed"; the footnote notes the original literal translation is "cooked"</note> and becomes a pearl. Now, where is such knowledge to be found in mankind?

"God has placed a love for brocades and silks in the hearts of mankind, even though these are produced from the secretions <note>original: "mucus"</note> of those little worms. Humans consider honey a most delicious food, yet it is produced by bees. In their gatherings, they light their rooms with wax candles; for these, they are also indebted to the bees. The most exquisite of all their decorations is the pearl, and this is produced by the skill of that little creature I have just described. God has created such excellent things through those creatures so that men might see them and acknowledge His art and power. But although they behold all His powers and all His arts, they are still indifferent; they waste their days in wickedness and lack of faith <note>original: "infidelity"</note>, and they show no gratitude for His blessings, but instead practice violence and oppression toward His poor and helpless servants."

When the locust had finished this speech, the King asked the men if they had anything else left to say. They replied, "There are still many excellent qualities in us which prove that we are the masters and they are our slaves." The King directed them to explain these, and a certain man said, "Our forms are all the same, while their forms and shapes are diverse. This proves us to be masters and them to be slaves, because uniformity <note>original: "identity of figure"</note> is proper for rule and mastery, while diversity is indicative of slavery." The King asked the animals what answer they could give to this, and they all remained thoughtful and downcast for an hour.

<summary>The locust describes the miraculous natural origins of luxury goods like silk and pearls to highlight human ingratitude toward God, while the humans counter by arguing that their uniform physical appearance proves their superiority over the diverse animal kingdom.</summary>

## Diversity of Religions

After a little while the nightingale, representing the birds, said, "The man speaks the truth. However, although the physical forms of animals are diverse, the instincts of all are alike. On the other hand, although the physical forms of men are identical, their minds are very different." The King asked for proof of this, and the nightingale replied: 

"The variety of religions and creeds proves this statement, for among men there are thousands of sects—Jews, Christians, Magians <note>original: "Magians"; followers of Zoroastrianism or other ancient Persian religions</note>, polytheists, unbelievers, idolaters, fire-worshippers, and star-worshippers. Beyond this, there are many sects within each religion, just as the opinions of philosophers were diverse in ancient times. 

Among the Jews, there are the Samaritans, the 'Abálí, and the Jalútí <note>likely referring to groups associated with the Exilarch or specific Jewish theological divisions in the medieval Islamic world</note>. Among Christians, there are Nestorians, Eutychians, and the followers of Malká <note>original: "Malká"; likely referring to the Melkites or "Royalists"</note>. Among the Magians are the Zarádushtís <note>Zoroastrians</note>, Zarwánís, Harmís, Mazakís, Bahrámís, and Mánawís <note>Manichaeans</note>. Among the Muslims <note>original: "Musulmáns"</note> there are Shías, Sunnís, Khárijís, Ráfizís, Násibís, Murjís, Kadarís, Jahamís, Mútazilís, Ash'arís, and others. 

What a vast variety of sects exists! All their religions and creeds conflict; the followers of one religion consider the others to be unbelievers and curse them. We animals are free from all such division; our creed and faith are one. In short, all animals are uniform and faithful in their belief. They know nothing of idolatry, wickedness, or sexual immorality. They do not have the slightest doubt or skepticism regarding the power and unity of God. They know Him to be the true Creator and Provider, and they devote themselves day and night to meditation, prayer, and praise. But these men are not even acquainted with our prayers."

An inhabitant of Persia said, "We also believe God to be the Creator, the Provider, the One, and the Peerless." 

At this, the King asked him why there was such great diversity in the religions and creeds of man. The Persian replied, "Religion and belief are the ways and means by which the goals of life may be obtained. The aim and object of all is one and the same..."

<summary>The nightingale argues that while animals are spiritually unified and naturally monotheistic, humans are fractured into countless conflicting religious sects and schools of philosophy. A Persian man responds by suggesting that these diverse religions are simply different paths toward the same ultimate goal.</summary>

# Religion and Politics.

"...by whatever road they reach it. Whatever direction we take, we move only toward God.” The King asked, “If the sole object of everyone is to reach God, then why does one group kill the other?” He replied, “This is not for the sake of religion, for there is no hatred in that; it is rather for the sake of power <note>original: "rule"</note>, which is an institution of religion.”

The King directed him to explain this fully, and he replied, “Power and religion are twins; one cannot exist without the other, but religion comes first, and power is secondary. Religion is necessary for power, so that through its influence all people may be honest; and religion requires a king who, with authority, can enforce the commandments of religion among the people. For this reason, some religious leaders kill other persons to achieve the goals of power and government. Every religious leader desires only this: that all people may adopt his specific creed and religion, and the ordinances of his law. If the King will attend to me and listen, I will provide clear proof of this point.” The King directed him to proceed.

He said, “The subduing of worldly desires <note>original: "mortification of the passions"</note> is a requirement in all religions and creeds, and subduing those desires requires that the seeker of religion should sacrifice himself. Similarly, in politics, it is the practice to kill any other rival for power.” The King said, “The killing of kings in the pursuit of power is obvious; but why do followers of religion stifle their own desires? Tell me this.” 

He said, “In the religion of Islam, this is certainly the clearest command, for God says, ‘God Almighty has purchased the souls <note>original: "passions"</note> and property of true believers, and has provided paradise for them, so that in the service of God they may fight, killing others and being killed themselves.’¹ There are many other texts of the...”

¹ Quran <note>original: "Kurán"</note>, Surah 9, verse 111 <note>The text cites verse 112, but this passage is found in verse 111 in modern numbering</note>. <header>Self-Devotion.</header>

The Quran, besides this, contains other passages that are explicit on this point; in one place, according to the commands of the Old Testament, God says, "If you will turn toward God, humble yourselves <note>original: "mortify yourselves"—in this context, to discipline the body or "kill" worldly desires through fasting and self-denial</note>, for this is best for you in the sight of God."¹

"And when the Lord Jesus said, 'Who will be my assistants in the service of God?' all his friends replied, 'We will be your helpers in God’s service.' Then the Lord Jesus replied, 'If you will be my assistants, then be ready for death and the scaffold <note>the executioner's platform</note>, so that you may go to heaven with me and live with your brothers. And if you will not be my assistants, then you are not of my flock.' Finally, they were all killed in the service of God and did not abandon the religion of Jesus. In the same way, the people of India, the Brahmans <note>the priestly class in Hindu society</note> and others, sacrifice themselves, and in the pursuit of their religion, burn themselves alive. It is their belief that the highest of all services in the sight of God is that repentant sinners should destroy their physical forms and burn their bodies so that their sins may be forgiven.

"Thus men learned in theology <note>the study of religious belief and the nature of God</note> restrain their passions, appetites, and lusts, and take upon themselves the burden of devotion until their passions are subdued and not the slightest desire or craving for this world is left. In short, all religious men sacrifice <note>original: "immolate"</note> their passions, looking upon this as the highest act of devotion through which they find deliverance from the fires of hell and reach heaven. But there are good and bad people to be found in all religions and creeds; yet of all the wicked, that man is by far the worst who denies that there is a day of resurrection, who has no hope for the reward of virtue, no fear of the punishment for sin, and who does not acknowledge the oneness of God, although all men return to Him."

God Almighty supplies them all with food and keeps them under his protection. This vast number, variety of circumstances, and difference of pursuits and goals prove that the human race is better than any other species. Furthermore, humans hold superiority over every other race of animals that exists. From this, it is clear that men are the masters and all the animals are their slaves. There are still further excellences in us, the details of which would be very tedious to explain.”

The Frog said to the King, “This man has presented the great number of men as an argument and boasts about it; but if he were to see the aquatic animals and behold their many forms and figures, then in comparison with them, men would seem very few. The cities and countries which he has mentioned would also look even smaller. This is because in the habitable quarter of the world, there are fifteen large seas: the Mediterranean, the Caspian, the Lake of Gilan, the Red Sea, the Persian Gulf, the Sea of Hind <note>the Indian Ocean near India</note>, the Sea of Sindh, the Sea of China, the Sea of Yajuj <note>The Sea of Gog and Magog, often identified with the remote North or Arctic</note>, the Indian Ocean, the Western Sea <note>the Atlantic</note>, the Northern Sea, the Sea of Abyssinia, the Southern Sea, and the Eastern Sea. And there are five hundred small rivers and two hundred large ones, such as...

# Vast Numbers of Animals.

...the Oxus <note>modern-day Amu Darya in Central Asia</note>, the Tigris, the Euphrates, the Nile, and others, the length of each of which is from a hundred to a thousand <term>kos</term> <note>a *kos* is an ancient Indian measure of distance, roughly 1.5 to 2 miles</note>. In addition to these, there are the small and large streams, rivers, lakes, and pools found in the woods and wilderness, which are impossible to count. Within these waters live fish, turtles, crocodiles, porpoises, alligators, and thousands of other kinds of aquatic animals known only to God and beyond human estimation.

> “Some say there are seven hundred species of aquatic animals, not counting all the specific varieties and sub-species. And among the animals that dwell on dry land—beasts of prey, livestock, and others—there are five hundred species, also not counting varieties and sub-species. All of these are the servants and subjects of God. He created them by His power, and He supports and constantly protects them from every harm. Nothing is hidden from Him. If humans would consider this and could grasp the massive swarms of animals, it would be clear that the sheer number and gathering of men is no proof that they are the masters and we are the slaves.”

# CHAPTER XXV.

## *On the World of Spirits.*

When the frog finished speaking, a sage from among the Jins <note>original: Jins; supernatural beings in Islamic mythology often translated as genies, created from smokeless fire</note> said, “O you groups of men and beasts, you are limited in your knowledge of the great variety of creatures. You do not know those beings who are spiritual and ethereal, and share no connection with physical forms. They are bodiless souls and elemental spirits who dwell in the regions of the sky. Some of them, who belong to the angelic class, are placed in the highest zone of the heavens; and those who dwell in the broad expanse of the cold sphere <term>cold sphere</term> <note>in medieval cosmology, this refers to the *Zamharir*, a layer of the atmosphere thought to be intensely cold, located between the Earth and the Moon</note> are the Jins and the tribe of devils.

“So, if you could only imagine the full scale of created beings, you would know that, compared to them, men and beasts have no significant existence. This is because the extent of the cold sphere is ten times greater than the terrestrial sea and land; and in the same way, the lunar sphere <note>the celestial sphere containing the Moon</note> is ten times larger than both these spheres combined. Every higher sphere bears the same relation in its size to the one beneath it. All these spheres are filled with spiritual beings—not a single inch of space is left empty. All the bodiless souls dwell there, as the Prophet <note>referring to the Prophet Muhammad</note> (may the peace and blessings of God be upon him!) has declared:

> ‘In the seven heavens, there is not a single space where the angels are not either standing, bowing, or prostrating themselves in the worship of God.’

Therefore, you humans, if you could only see their vast armies, you would realize that your race is of no consequence compared to them, and that your num—

<header>God’s Promises to Man.</header>

Neither your large numbers nor your gathering provide any proof that you are the masters and all others are your slaves. All the servants of God are His soldiers and subjects, though some are subordinate to others. In short, He has issued the orders for their regulation among themselves according to what He, in His supreme wisdom, deemed right. Praise and glory are due to Him in all circumstances.”

When the Jin sage finished his speech, the King turned toward the men and said, “The animals have given an answer to the claim you were boasting about. If you have anything left to say, speak.” 

An orator of the Hijjaz <note>original: Hijjáz; the western region of the Arabian Peninsula, including Mecca and Medina</note> then said, “There are yet many excellences in us by which it is proved that we are masters and they are slaves.” The King directed him to speak, and he said, “God Almighty has promised us many blessings: resurrection from the grave; being gathered across the whole face of the earth; the accounting on the Day of Judgment; the passage over the bridge of Sirat <note>original: Sirát; in Islamic theology, the hair-thin bridge spanning across Hell that souls must cross to enter Paradise</note>; entrance into Paradise; heaven; the Garden of Delights; the Garden of Eternity; the Garden of Eden; the Garden of Refuge; the Mansion of Peace; the Mansion of Permanence; the Home of Rest; the Glorious Abode; the Tuba tree <note>a blessed tree in Paradise said to provide shade and fruit</note>; the fountain of Salsabil <note>a spring in Paradise mentioned in the Quran</note>; streams running with wine, milk, honey, and water; elevated dwellings; the society of the *houris* <note>original: húrís; beautiful companions found in Paradise</note>; and nearness to God. These and many other blessings, which are mentioned in the Quran, God has prepared for us. Can the animals attain these things anywhere? This is proof that we are the masters and they are our slaves. Besides these blessings and excellences, there are many other great qualities in us which I have not yet mentioned.”

<meta>This page concludes the final argument in the debate between humans and animals before the King of the Jinn. <page-num>156</page-num>
<header>The Decision.</header>

...you will entirely perish, and no name or trace will remain of you."

On hearing these words, all the representatives of the animals and all the Jinn <note>original: Jin; in Islamic mythology, supernatural beings created from smokeless fire</note> sages said, "Now you have presented an effective argument and have provided conclusive proof. Boasters may brag about such things. But now tell us: what is the nature of the attributes, excellent qualities, and virtues of those beings whom you have praised and celebrated? If you know, tell us fully." The men all reflected for a while and remained silent; no one could answer.

After a little while, a holy person said, "O just King! The truth of the men's claim has been established in the royal presence. It has also appeared that there is a class among them who are near to God, whose graces are praiseworthy, whose virtues are worthy of respect, whose characteristics are pleasing, whose temperaments are angelic, whose habits are holy, and whose nature is wonderful and miraculous—so much so that no tongue can describe them. The mind fails to understand the nature of their qualities. All the preachers and orators spend their entire lives trying to describe their excellence, and yet they never truly reach a complete understanding. Now, just King, what order do you give regarding these foreign men, whose slaves the animals are?" 

The King said, "Let all animals be submissive and obedient to man, and let none depart from their allegiance." The animals consented, and being satisfied, they all returned in security and peace to their homes.

<center>->Publications of Trübner & Co.<-</center>

**Crosskey.**—**A DEFENCE OF RELIGION.** By **Henry W. Crosskey.** 48 pages. <term>12mo</term><note>Duodecimo: a book size of approximately 5 by 7.5 inches.</note>. <term>sewed</term><note>A pamphlet or book with a paper cover, stitched rather than bound in hardboard.</note>. 1854. *1 shilling.*

**Current (The) Gold and Silver Coins of all Countries**, their Weight and <term>Fineness</term><note>The proportion of pure precious metal in an alloy.</note>, and their Intrinsic Value in English Money, with Facsimiles of the Coins. By **Leopold C. Martin**, of Her Majesty’s Stationery Office, and **Charles Trübner.** In one volume, <term>medium 8vo</term><note>A book size of about 6 by 9 inches.</note>, 141 Plates, printed in Gold and Silver, and representing about 1,000 Coins, with 160 pages of Text, handsomely bound in embossed cloth, richly gilt, with Emblematical Designs on the Cover, and gilt edges. 1863. *£2 2 shillings.*

This work, which the Publishers have much pleasure in offering to the Public, contains a series of the Gold and Silver Coins of the whole world current during the present century. The collection amounts to nearly a thousand specimens; including their <term>reverses</term><note>The back side of a coin.</note>, there are twice that number. The Publishers feel confident that it is as nearly complete as was possible to make it.

It seems almost unnecessary to mention the usefulness of this work. It is evident that it must become a highly valuable handbook for the <term>bullion-dealer</term><note>A person who trades in gold or silver in bulk, such as bars or ingots.</note> and the money-changer. For the merchant and banker, it will prove a reliable work of reference due to its extremely accurate details and calculations. 

The elegance and splendor of the work also make it an excellent and instructive ornament for the drawing-room table; the enjoyment found in comparing the artistic tastes of different countries as shown in their coins may help pass many a half-hour of <term>ennui</term><note>original: "ennui"; boredom or dissatisfaction.</note>.

In the valuation of the physical coins and the calculation of their worth in English money, the highest level of accuracy has been maintained. This includes the statements regarding their purity, which are provided according to the legal Mint regulations of each country. In exceptional cases where official information regarding weight and fineness was unavailable, <term>assays</term><note>Chemical tests to determine the content or quality of a metal.</note> from various locations are reported. <note>The word "reported" has broken or poorly inked type: "repor'ed."</note>

The exact weight of the coins is expressed in both English troy grains and French grams. Their purity is shown using both English technical terms and French <term>thousandths</term><note>original: "milliémes"; a metric system for measuring the purity of precious metals.</note>. The modern trend toward the decimal metric system makes such notation almost essential. For easier reference, tables converting British purity reports into thousandths for gold and silver have been included.

**Da Costa.**—**MEDICAL DIAGNOSIS**: with Special References to Practical Medicine. By **J. M. Da Costa, M.D.** 2nd edition, revised. 8vo. cloth, 784 pages. 1866. *24 shillings.*

**Dadabhai.**—**THE EUROPEAN AND ASIATIC RACES.** Observations on Mr. Crawfurd's Paper read before the Ethnological Society. By **Dadabhai Naoroji.** 8vo. sewed, 32 pages. 1866. *1 shilling.*

**Dalton.**—**A TREATISE ON HUMAN PHYSIOLOGY.** For the Use of Students and Practitioners of Medicine. By **John C. Dalton, Jr., M.D.** Third Edition, revised and enlarged. 8vo. cloth, 706 pages. 1866. *21 shillings.*

**Dana.**—**A TEXT-BOOK OF GEOLOGY**, designed for Schools and Academies. By **J. D. Dana, LL.D.** Illustrated by 375 Woodcuts. <term>Crown 8vo</term><note>A book size of approximately 5 by 7.5 inches.</note>, cloth, 354 pages (vi and 354). 1864. *7 shillings and 6 pence.*

**Dana.**—**MANUAL OF GEOLOGY**; treating of the Principles of the Science, with Special Reference to American Geological History. For the Use of Colleges, Academies, and Schools of Science. By **James D. Dana, M.A., LL.D.** Illustrated by a Chart of the World and over one thousand figures, mostly from American sources. 8vo. cloth, 798 pages. 1866. *21 shillings.*

<center>->Publications of Trübner & Co.<-</center>

**Dana.**—**A SYSTEM OF MINERALOGY: DESCRIPTIVE MINERALOGY**, comprising the most recent discoveries. By **James Dwight Dana** and **George Jarvis Brush**. Fifth edition. 1 volume. <term>8vo</term><note>Octavo: a book size of approximately 6 by 9 inches.</note>, 874 pages. 1868. *£1 16 shillings.*

**Darby.**—**RUGGIERO Vivaldi and other Lays of Italy.** By **Eleanor Darby**. <term>8vo</term>. cloth. 208 pages. 1865. *5 shillings.*

**Day.**—**THE LAND OF THE PERMAULS**, or Cochin, its Past and its Present. By **Francis Day**, Esq., <term>F.L.S.</term><note>Fellow of the Linnean Society, a prestigious society for the study of natural history.</note> <term>8vo</term>. cloth, 577 pages. 1863. *25 shillings.*
<note>The "Permauls" refers to the ancient Cheraman Perumal rulers of the Malabar Coast in India.</note>

**Deichmann.**—**NEW TABLES** to facilitate the practice of **Great Circle Sailing** <note>The practice of navigating a ship along the shortest distance between two points on a sphere.</note>, together with an application of the theory of the great circle on the globe to sailing, and an appendix containing several mathematical demonstrations. This work is accompanied by a scale of great circles on a blank chart, which allows a navigator to determine—without manual calculation—the great circle that passes through two given places. It also shows the locations where changes in the spherical course (expressed in <term>fourths of the point</term><note>A "point" on a compass is 11.25 degrees; a fourth of a point is roughly 2.8 degrees, used for precise navigation.</note>) take place along the great circle's arc between those two places. By **A. H. Deichmann**. <term>8vo</term>. boards. 88 pages. 1857. *5 shillings.*

**Delepierre.**—**LITERARY HISTORY OF THE INSANE** <note>original title: *Histoire Littéraire des Fous*</note>. By **Octave Delepierre**. 184 pages. 1860. *5 shillings.*

**Delepierre.**—**ANALYSIS OF THE WORKS OF THE PHILOBIBLON SOCIETY OF LONDON** <note>original title: *Analyse des Travaux de la Société des Philobiblon de Londres*</note>. By **Octave Delepierre**. Small <term>4to</term><note>Quarto: a book size of approximately 9.5 by 12 inches.</note>, 134 pages, bound in the <term>Roxburghe style</term><note>A bookbinding style with a leather back, cloth or paper sides, and a gilded top edge.</note>. 1862. *10 shillings and 6 pence.*

> "It is probably not generally known that among the many learned associations of London, there exists one called the **Philobiblon Society**. This somewhat exclusive group of book-loving philosophers was established in 1853 under the patronage of the late Prince Consort <note>Prince Albert, husband of Queen Victoria.</note>. It was modeled after the French Academy—it being one of the fundamental rules of the Society never to exceed the 'immortal Forty' in membership <note>The French Academy is famously limited to 40 members, known as 'the immortals.'</note>. . . . The statutes of the Philobiblon provided for the annual issue of a volume of historical, biographical, critical, and other essays contributed by any of the forty members. These were printed in a very limited edition, and not a single book was to be sold for money. To increase the value of the works, it was arranged that every member should receive only two copies of each volume, signed by the president and secretary of the society, with the owner's name on the title page. . . . The contents of these half-dozen rare and somewhat mysterious works have just been revealed in a curious little book published by Messrs. Trübner and Co., produced in an exact imitation of the books from <term>Caxton’s press</term><note>William Caxton was the first person to introduce a printing press into England in 1476.</note>. This work is dedicated by the author, Mr. Octave Delepierre, to the Duke D’Aumale, who has been the patron of the Philobiblon since the death of Prince Albert and one of its leading members from the start."—*Spectator*.

> "Two modest but very useful books have been lately compiled by Mr. Delepierre and Mr. Nichols. The former, whose ‘History of Flemish Literature’ has already been noted in these columns, has printed a summary of the various works issued by the Philobiblon Society of London, of which, by the way, he is the honorary secretary. . . . Only those who have lacked them can truly estimate how great a benefit such catalogs are to historical and literary researchers. . . The gentlemen whose works we have named at the head of this article have performed a service to literature comparable to that rendered by the compilers of the <term>Calendars of the State Papers</term><note>Official catalogs of historical government documents in the UK National Archives.</note>."—*Saturday Review*.

> "Mr. Delepierre, the secretary, is also a very important contributor. By way of teasing the public, he issues this ‘Analysis,’ which is a descriptive catalog of the precious rarities collected by the society."—*Literary Budget*.

> "The account which Mr. Delepierre gives of these volumes makes us regret that their contents have remained, as he tells us, almost unknown to the public, since many of the contributions appear to be of much interest."—*Parthenon*.

**Delepierre.**—**ADDITIONAL MACARONIC WORKS** <note>original: *Macaroneana Andra*</note>; or, **New Miscellanies of Macaronic Literature** <note>original: *Nouveaux Mélanges de Littérature Macaronique*</note>. By **Octave Delepierre**. Small <term>4to</term>., 180 pages, printed by Whittingham, and handsomely bound in the <term>Roxburghe style</term>. 1862. *10 shillings and 6 pence.*
This volume, together with the one published by the author in 1852, forms the most complete collection of that unique form of <term>Macaronic poetry</term><note>A style of verse that mixes Latin words with vernacular languages, often for humorous or satirical effect.</note> in existence.

**Fichte.**—**THE CHARACTERISTICS OF THE PRESENT AGE.** By Johann Gottlieb Fichte. Translated from the German by William Smith. Post octavo <term>Post 8vo: a book size approximately 5 by 8 inches</term>, cloth, pages xi and 271. 1847. 6 shillings.

> "We accept these lectures as a true and most admirable description <note>original: "delineation"</note> of the present age; and on this ground alone we would give <note>original: "bestow on"</note> them our heartiest recommendation. However, it is because they teach us how we may rise above the age that we give them our most emphatic praise.
>
> "He makes us think, and perhaps more grandly <note>original: "sublimely"</note> than we have ever formerly thought, but it is only so that we may act more nobly.
>
> "As a majestic and most stirring utterance from the lips of the greatest German prophet, we trust that the book will find a response in many an English soul, and powerfully help to renew <note>original: "regenerate"</note> English society."—*The Critic.*

**Fichte.**—**THE VOCATION <note>In this context, "vocation" refers to a person's calling or life's purpose</note> OF A SCHOLAR.** By Johann Gottlieb Fichte. Translated from the German by William Smith. Post octavo, cloth. Pages 78, paper-bound <note>original: "sewed"; books sold in paper covers rather than hard bindings</note>, 1847. 2 shillings.

> "'The Vocation of a Scholar' is distinguished by the same high moral tone and manly, vigorous expression which characterize all Fichte's works in German, and it loses nothing in Mr. Smith's clear, straightforward <note>original: "unembarrassed"</note>, and thoroughly English translation."—*Douglas Jerrold's Newspaper.*
>
> "We are glad to see this excellent translation of one of Fichte's best works presented to the public in a very neat form. No class needs an earnest and sincere spirit more than the literary class; therefore the 'Vocation of the Scholar,' the 'Guide of the Human Race,' written in Fichte's most earnest and commanding style, will be welcomed in its English version by public writers and will benefit the cause of truth."—*The Economist.*

**Fichte.**—**THE VOCATION OF MAN.** By Johann Gottlieb Fichte. Translated from the German by William Smith. Post octavo, cloth, pages xii and 198. 1848. 4 shillings.

> "In the progress of my present work, I have taken a deeper look into religion than ever I did before. In me, the emotions of the heart proceed only from perfect intellectual clarity; it must be that the clarity I have now attained on this subject shall also take possession of my heart."—*Fichte's Correspondence.*
>
> "'The Vocation of Man' is, as Fichte truly says, understandable <note>original: "intelligible"</note> to all readers who are really able to understand a book at all; and as the history of the mind in its various phases of doubt, knowledge, and faith, it is of interest to all. A book of this caliber is sure to teach you much because it provokes thought. If it rouses you to challenge <note>original: "combat"</note> his conclusions, it has done a good work; for in that very effort, you are stirred to consider points which have previously escaped your lazy acceptance <note>original: "indolent acquiescence"</note>."—*Foreign Quarterly.*
>
> "This is Fichte's most popular work, and is in every way remarkable."—*The Atlas.*
>
> "It appears to us the boldest and most emphatic attempt that has yet been made to explain to man his restless and invincible <note>original: "unconquerable"</note> desire to win the True and the Eternal."—*The Sentinel.*

### **Fichte.—ON THE NATURE OF THE SCHOLAR, AND ITS MANIFESTATIONS.**
By Johann Gottlieb Fichte. Translated from the German by William Smith. Second Edition. Cloth. Post octavo <term>Post 8vo: a book size approximately 5 by 8 inches</term>. Pages vii and 131. 1848. 3 shillings.

> “With great satisfaction, we welcome this first English translation of an author who occupies the most exalted position as a profound and original thinker; as an irresistible orator in the cause of what he believed to be the truth; as a thoroughly honest and heroic man... The appearance of any of his works in our language is, we believe, a complete novelty... These orations are admirably fitted for their purpose; so grand is the position taken by the lecturer, and so irresistible is their eloquence.” —*The Examiner*.

> “This work must inevitably capture the attention of the scientific physician by the grand spirituality of its doctrines and the pure morality it teaches... Shall we be presumptuous if we recommend these views to our professional colleagues? Or if we say to the enlightened, the thoughtful, and the serious: 'This—if you be true scholars—is your vocation'? We know of no higher morality than this, nor any more noble principles: they are full of truth.” —*British and Foreign Medico-Chirurgical Review*.

### **Fichte.—MEMOIR OF JOHANN GOTTLIEB FICHTE.** 
By William Smith. Second Edition. Post octavo. Cloth. Pages 168. 1848. 4 shillings.

> “...A Life of Fichte, full of nobility <note>original: "nobleness"</note> and instruction, of grand purpose, tender feeling, and brave effort! ...the compilation of which is executed with great judgment and fidelity.” —*Prospective Review*.

> “We state Fichte’s character as it is known and admitted by men of all parties among the Germans when we say that so robust an intellect—a soul so calm, so lofty, so massive, and immovable <note>original: "immoveable"</note>—has not participated in philosophical discussion since the time of Luther... Fichte’s opinions may be true or false; but his character as a thinker can be slightly valued only by those who know it poorly. As a man, proven by action and suffering in both his life and death, he ranks with a class of men who were common only in better ages than ours.” —*The State of German Literature, by Thomas Carlyle*.

### **Fichte.—THE WAY TOWARDS A BLESSED LIFE; OR, THE DOCTRINE OF RELIGION.** 
By Johann Gottlieb Fichte. Translated by William Smith. Post octavo. Cloth. Pages viii and 221. 1849. 5 shillings.

**Francis.—LOWELL HYDRAULIC EXPERIMENTS.** Being a selection from experiments on Hydraulic Motors, on the Flow of Water over Weirs, in Open Canals of Uniform Rectangular Section, and through Submerged Orifices and Diverging Tubes, made at Lowell, Massachusetts. ### Grammatography.—**A MANUAL OF REFERENCE** to the Alphabets of Ancient and Modern Languages. Based on the German Compilation of F. Ballhorn. In one volume, royal octavo <term>Royal octavo: A book size approximately 6.25 by 10 inches.</term>, cloth. 80 pages. 1861. 7 shillings, 6 pence.

The “Grammatography” is offered to the public as a concise yet comprehensive introduction to reading the most important ancient and modern languages. Simple in its design, it will be a useful resource for the student of linguistics, the amateur linguist, the bookseller, the proofreader, and the diligent typesetter <term>original: "Compositor"; a person who sets type for printing.</term>.

**Gutenberg, Johann** <note>original: "John"</note>, **FIRST MASTER PRINTER**, his life, most remarkable speeches, and his death. Translated from the German. By C. W. Octavo <term>Octavo (8vo): A book size approximately 6 by 9 inches.</term>, cloth, 141 pages. 1860. 10 shillings, 6 pence.

**Hagen.—NORICA**; or, **TALES FROM THE OLDEN TIME**. Translated from the German of August Hagen. Foolscap octavo <term>Foolscap octavo (Fcap. 8vo): A book size approximately 4.25 by 6.75 inches.</term>, ornamental binding, suitable for a gift. 14 preliminary pages and 374 pages. 1850. 5 shillings.

> “This pleasant volume is produced in the style of books from a century ago, which has recently become very popular. The printing and physical quality of the volume show the great taste and effort put into it. Simple in style, concise, and reasonably sharp—the book strongly reflects the picturesque old days it describes. A long study of the art history of Nuremberg <note>original: "Nürnberg"</note>, and a deep knowledge of the records, letters, and memoirs still preserved from the times of Albrecht Dürer <note>original: "Albert Durer"</note> and his great fellow artists, have enabled the author to present a vividly drawn and highly finished picture of art and domestic life in that wonderfully artistic and art-respecting old German city.”—*Atlas*.

> “A delightful little book. It is full of charming, talkative detail and marked by an earnest simplicity of thought and language. This perfectly conveys to the reader the domestic and artistic German life during the era of Emperor Maximilian, Albrecht Dürer, and Hans Sachs—the famous shoemaker and 'master singer'—as well as most of the famous artists of Nuremberg in the 16th century. Art is the primary focus of this little history. It is praised with a quiet devotion that explains the religious passion of those early creators of the ideal and the beautiful; perhaps this deeper concentration of thought was the secret of their success.”—*Weekly Dispatch*.

> “A volume full of interest for lovers of history; the way it is presented may inspire many to think about art and its many wonderful influences with a serious curiosity they haven't felt before. It also teaches a lesson: that people can be led to take an interest in what is refined and beautiful just as easily as in what is crude and degrading.”—*Manchester Examiner*.

**JENKINS'S VEST-POCKET LEXICON. A Dictionary of all except the common Words which everybody knows.** By omitting these it contains the less familiar words, and the principal scientific and technical terms, foreign currencies, weights and measures; also the common Latin and French phrases of two and three words, law terms, etc.

> "A little book, entitled 'Jenkins's Vest-pocket Lexicon,' has just been published by Messrs. Trübner and Co., of Paternoster Row, which has a somewhat novel but very useful design. The object of the work is to compress within the smallest practicable compass a full lexicon of all the words used in writing or speaking English, except—and in the exception lies the essence of the undertaking—those which may fairly be considered familiar to everyone. Thus every word which any reader but the most ignorant, and any reader but the most amazingly learned, could need to look for in a dictionary, is to be found in the columns of this little book. We have put it to several tests by looking up peculiar scientific words, terms used in art, legal phrases, names of foreign coins, titles of foreign officials, etc., and, so far as our examination went, we have not found it to fail in any instance. We can, therefore, cordially recommend it to writers as well as to readers. It really does fit in the waistcoat pocket and may literally be made a constant companion. — *Morning Star*, July 17."

64mo <term>64mo: A very small book size, approximately 2 by 3 inches.</term>, limp morocco leather, 560 pages. 1861. 2 shillings and 6 pence.

**JEWISH** (A) **REPLY TO DR. COLENSO'S CRITICISM ON THE PENTATEUCH.** Issued by the Jewish Association for the Diffusion of Religious Knowledge. Octavo, cloth binding, pages ix and 147. 1865. 3 shillings.
