--- Page 222 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of Trübner & Co.’s publications, specifically providing a collection of critical reviews and testimonials from 1857 and 1858 regarding the "Bibliotheca Glottica," a comprehensive bibliography of indigenous American languages.</meta>

<page-num>56</page-num>
<header>Publications of Trübner & Co.</header>

"This is the first installment of a work which will be of the greatest value to philologists; it is a compendium of the indigenous languages of the American continents and a digest of all known literature concerning those languages. Mr. Trübner's hand has been involved *throughout*, and in his preface, he claims credit for about one-sixth of the whole. We have no doubt that the encouragement scholars will give this portion of the work will inspire Mr. Trübner with enough confidence to persevere in his difficult and most honorable task."—*The Critic, December 15th, 1857*.

"Few would believe that a large octavo volume would be necessary to cover the subject; yet it is so, and this handsome, useful, and curious volume—carefully compiled by Mr. Ludewig, assisted by Prof. Turner, and edited by the careful hand of Mr. Trübner, the well-known publisher—will certainly find a place in many libraries." —*Bentley's Advertiser, November 6th, 1857*.

"Lovers of American linguistics will find that Mr. Trübner’s work omits almost nothing intended to help the comparative philologist in tracing the various languages of the great Western Continent."—*Galway Mercury, January 30th, 1858*.

"Only those deeply experienced in philological studies can appreciate this book at its full value. It shows that there are upwards of seven hundred and fifty indigenous American languages."—*Gentleman's Magazine, February 1858*.

"The work contains an account of no fewer than seven hundred different native dialects of America, with an introductory chapter of bibliographical information; under each dialect is an account of any grammars or other works that illustrate it."—*The Bookseller, January 1858*.

"We have here a list of existing monuments of an almost innumerable series of languages and dialects of the American Continent. Most Indian grammars and vocabularies exist only in manuscript and were compiled chiefly by missionaries of the Christian Church. We are, therefore, even more indebted to Dr. Ludewig and Mr. Trübner for the great care with which they have pointed out where such works can be found, as well as for listing those that have been printed—either in a separate volume, in collections, in accounts of voyages and travels, or elsewhere."—*Leader, September 11th, 1858*.

"I do not have time, nor is it my purpose, to review this admirable work or attempt to show the extent and value of its contents. It is perhaps enough to say that, besides a concise but clear list and notice of the various general philological works that treat American indigenous languages with more or less detail, or which mention them incidentally, it provides bibliographical notices of grammars, vocabularies, etc., of the native languages of America. A unique and valuable feature of the work is that it gives not only the titles of printed or published grammars and vocabularies but also notices unpublished or manuscript works of these kinds wherever they are known to exist—especially those that disappeared among the remains of the suppressed convents and religious institutions of Spanish America."—*E. G. Squier, in a paper read before the American Ethnological Society, January 12th, 1858*.

"Because the author died before he had finished the revision of the work, it has been carefully examined by competent scholars, who have also made many valuable additions." —*American Publishers' Circular, January 30th, 1858*.

"It contains 256 closely printed pages of titles, printed books, and manuscripts, along with notices of American indigenous languages. It includes references to nearly everything that has been written or published regarding them, whether in specialized works or mentioned incidentally in travel books, periodicals, or the proceedings of learned societies."—*New York Herald, January 26th, 1858*.

"The way this contribution to the bibliography of American languages has been executed—both by the author, Mr. Ludewig, and the skilled writers who have edited the work since his death—is spoken of in the highest terms by gentlemen most familiar with the subject." —*American Historical Magazine, Vol. II, No. 5, May 1858*.

"I will conclude by announcing the first volume of a publication destined to be of great service to comparative philology and general linguistics. I am speaking of the *Bibliotheca Glottica*, a work intended to contain a list of all known dictionaries and grammars of languages, both printed and manuscript. The editor of this precious bibliography is Mr. Nicolas Trübner, whose name is honorably known in the Oriental world. The first volume is dedicated to American idioms; the second will deal with the languages of India. The work is done with the most conscientious care and will bring honor to Mr. Nicolas Trübner, especially if he continues his work with the same passion he showed in starting it." —*L. Léon de Rosny. Revue de l'Orient, February 1858*.

"Mr. Trübner's most important work on the bibliography of the indigenous languages of America deserves all praise as being eminently useful to those who study that branch of literature. The value of the book, and the effort its compilation must have cost, will not be diminished by the fact that it is the first in this field of linguistic literature." —*Petermann's Geographische Mittheilungen, p. 79, February 1858*.

"Undoubtedly, this volume of Trübner's *Bibliotheca Glottica* ranks among the most valuable additions that have enriched our bibliographical literature in recent years. To us Germans, it is most gratifying that the initiative has been taken by a German bookseller—one of the most intelligent and active of our countrymen abroad—to produce a work with higher aims than mere financial profit, and that he himself has worked on its production with his own hands. This is because it is becoming increasingly rare for an individual to make their primary goal serving the cause of literature rather than enriching themselves." —*P. Tromel, Börsenblatt, January 4th, 1858*.

<summary>This page contains a series of positive reviews and testimonials from various 19th-century publications and scholars regarding Nicolas Trübner’s "Bibliotheca Glottica." The work is celebrated for its comprehensive bibliography of over seven hundred indigenous American languages and its inclusion of rare manuscript sources.</summary>
<keywords>Trübner &amp; Co., Bibliotheca Glottica, indigenous languages, American philology, linguistics, Hermann Ludewig, Nicolas Trübner, manuscripts, bibliography, 19th-century reviews</keywords>

--- Page 223 ---
<meta>This page continues the collection of testimonials and critical reviews for Trübner & Co.’s linguistic publications, specifically Ludewig’s "Literature of American Aboriginal Languages," followed by new catalog listings for works on mathematics, agriculture, and philology.</meta>

<page-num>57</page-num>
<header>Publications of Trübner & Co.</header>

> “In the compilation of this work, the editors have used not only the labors of Vater, Barton, Duponceau, Gallatin, De Souza, and others, but also the manuscript sources left by the missionaries, and many books which even the library of the British Museum lacks; they provide the fullest account of the literature of no fewer than 525 languages. The value of the work, so necessary to the study of ethnology, is greatly enhanced by the addition of a good Index.” — *Berliner National-Zeitung*, November 22nd, 1857.

> “The name of the author—to all those who are acquainted with his former works and who know the thoroughness and profound character of his investigations—is a sufficient guarantee that this work will be a standard authority, and one that will fully meet the demands of the present time.” — *Petzholdt's Anzeiger*, January, 1858.

> “The chief merit of the editor and publisher is to have completed the work carefully and clearly in both content and form, and thus to have established a new and greatly expanded edition of ‘*Vater's Index of all the Languages of the World*,’ after Professor Jülǵ’s revision of 1847. In order to continue and complete this work, the editor requires the assistance of all those who are acquainted with this new branch of science, and we sincerely hope it may be granted to him.” — *Magazin für die Literatur des Auslandes*, No. 38, 1858.

> “As the general title of the book indicates, it will be extended to the languages of the other continents if it meets with a favorable reception, which we most cordially wish for it.” — *A. F. Pott, Preussische Jahrbücher*, Vol. II., part 1.

> “This scholarly compilation is, without question, the most important bibliographic work that our era has seen emerge concerning the indigenous nations of America.” — *Nouvelles Annales des Voyages*, April, 1859.

> “The *Bibliotheca Glottica*, the publication of which Mr. Nicolas Trübner has begun, is one of the most useful books ever written to facilitate the study of comparative philology. The first volume of this great linguistic bibliography includes the textual list of all grammars, all dictionaries, and even the shortest vocabularies that have been printed in the different dialects of the two Americas; furthermore, it makes known the manuscript works of the same nature held in the principal public and private libraries. This work must have required long and patient research; therefore, it deserves to attract the special attention of philologists. May the other volumes of this library be prepared with the same care and soon be in the hands of all the scholars to whom they can render invaluable services.” — *Revue Américaine et Orientale*, No. 1, Oct., 1858.

> “To every fresh addition to the bibliography of language, of which we have a most admirable specimen in this work, the thoughtful linguist will ever turn with increasing satisfaction and hope, as the great problem of the unity of human speech approaches its full solution.”

> “Mr. Nicolas Trübner, however, has perhaps, on the whole, done the highest service of all to the philologist by the publication of ‘The Literature of American Aboriginal Languages.’ He has, with the aid of Professor Turner, greatly enlarged and at the same time most skillfully edited the valuable materials acquired by his deceased friend, Mr. Ludewig. We do not, indeed, at this moment, know of any similar work deserving of full comparison with it. In its ample enumeration of important reference works and careful record of the most recent facts in the literature of its subject, it—as might have been expected—greatly surpasses Jülǵ’s ‘*Vater*,’ valuable and trustworthy though that learned German’s work undoubtedly is.” — *North British Review*, No. 59, Feb., 1859.

The Editor has also received very kind and encouraging letters regarding the work from Sir George Grey, the Chevalier Bunsen, Dr. Th. Goldstücker, Mr. Watts (of the Museum), Professor A. Fr. Pott (of Halle), Dr. Julius Petzholt (of Dresden), Privy Councilor Dr. Grasse (of Dresden), Mr. F. F. de la Fighanière (of Lisbon), E. Edwards (of Manchester), Dr. Max Müller (of Oxford), Dr. Buschmann (of Berlin), Dr. Jülǵ (of Cracow), and other linguistic scholars.

### Luvini.—TABLES of LOGARITHMS with Seven Places of Decimals.
By John Luvini. Crown octavo, cloth, pages VIII. and 368. 1866. 5s.

### Lyman.—Cotton Culture. By Joseph B. Lyman, late of Louisiana.
With an additional chapter on Cotton Seed and its Uses. By J. R. Sypher.
Cloth, pages VII. and 190. 6s.

general, with a few words on Gloucestershire in particular. Delivered before
the Literary and Scientific Association at Gloucester, January 17th, 1868, with
an Appendix containing tables of the world-wide affinity of Languages. By the
Rev. Samuel Lysons, M.A., F.S.A., Honorary Canon of Gloucester Cathedral and
Rector of Rodmarton, Author of “Our British Ancestors,” “The Model
Merchant of the Middle Ages,” etc., etc. octavo, stiff cover, pages 51 and 62. 1868.
2s. 6d.

<meta>This page transitions from a list of individual book entries to a series of press reviews and testimonials for "Trübner’s Bibliographical Guide to American Literature." These reviews, dated from early 1859, praise Nicolas Trübner's work for its comprehensive cataloging of American books over the previous forty years.</meta>

# Publications of Trübner & Co.

> “*Trübner’s Bibliographical Guide to American Literature* deserves praise for the great care with which it is prepared, and the wonderful amount of information contained in its pages. It is compiled and edited by Mr. Nicolas Trübner, the publisher, of Paternoster Row. It comprises a classified list of books published in the United States during the last forty years, with Bibliographical Notes, and an Alphabetical Index. The introduction is very elaborate and full of facts, and must be the work of a gentleman who has spared no pains in making himself master of all that is important in connection with American literature. It certainly supplies much information not generally known in Europe.”—*Morning Star*, January 31, 1859.

> “Mr. Trübner deserves much credit for being the first to arrange bibliography according to the received rules of the art. He began the labor in 1855, and the first volume was published in that year; constituting, in fact, the earliest attempt, on this side of the Atlantic, to catalog American books. The present volume, of course, is enlarged, and is more perfect in every respect. The method of classification is exceedingly clear and useful.
> “In short, it presents the actual state of literature, as well as the course of its development from the beginning. Into the subject matter of this section we shall have to look hereafter; we are now simply explaining the composition of Mr. Trübner’s most valuable and useful book.”—*Spectator*, February 5, 1859.

> “Mr. Trübner’s book is by far the most complete American bibliography that has yet appeared, and displays an amount of patience and research that does him infinite credit. We have tested the accuracy of the work upon several points demanding much care and inquiry, and the result has always been satisfactory. Our American brethren cannot fail to feel complimented by the production of this volume, which in quantity almost equals our own London catalog.”—*The Bookseller*, February 24, 1859.

> “To say of this volume that it entirely fulfills the promise of its title page is possibly the highest and most truthful commendation that can be awarded to it. Mr. Trübner deserves, however, something beyond general praise for the patient and intelligent labor with which he has elaborated the earlier forms of the work into that which it now bears. What was once but a scanty volume has now become magnified, under his care, to one of considerable size; and what was once little better than a dry catalog may now take rank as a biographical work of first-rate importance. His position as an American literary agent has, doubtless, been very favorable to Mr. Trübner by throwing matter in his way; and he confesses, in his preface, that it is to this source that he is mainly indebted for the materials which have enabled him to construct the work before us. Mr. Trübner’s object in compiling this book is, he states, twofold: ‘On the one hand, to suggest the necessity of a more perfect work of its kind by an American, surrounded, as he necessarily would be, with the needed appliances; and, on the other, to supply to Europeans a guide to Anglo-American literature—a branch which, by its rapid rise and increasing importance, begins to force itself more and more on our attention.’ It is very modest in Mr. Trübner thus to treat his work as a mere suggestion for others. It is much more than this: it is an example which those who attempt to do anything more complete cannot do better than to follow as a model, which they will do well to copy if they would combine fullness of material with that admirable order and arrangement which so facilitates reference, and without which a work of this sort is all but useless.
> “All honor, then, to the literature of Young America—for young she still is, and let her thank her stars for it—and all honor also to Mr. Trübner for taking so much pains to make us acquainted with it.”—*The Critic*, March 19, 1859.

> “This is not only a very useful, because well executed, bibliographical work—it is also a work of much interest to all who are connected with literature. The bulk of it consists of a classified list, with date of publication, size, and price, of all the works, original or translated, which have appeared in the United States during the last forty years; and an alphabetical index facilitates reference to any particular work or author. On the merits of this portion of the work we cannot, of course, be expected to form a judgment. It would require something of the special erudition of Mr. Trübner himself to say how far he has succeeded or fallen short of his undertaking—how few, or how many, have been his omissions. There is one indication, however, of his careful minuteness, which suggests the amount of labor that must have been bestowed on the work—namely, the full enumeration of all the contents of the various Transactions and Scientific Journals. Thus, ‘the Transactions of the American Philosophical Society,’ from the year 1769 to 1857—no index to which has yet appeared in America—are in this work made easy of reference, every paper of every volume being mentioned <term>seriatim</term> <note>In a series; one after another.</note>. The naturalist who wishes to know what papers have appeared in the *Boston Journal of Natural History* during the last twenty years—that is, from its commencement—has only to glance over the five closely printed pages of this guide to satisfy himself at once.”—*The Saturday Review*, April 2, 1859.

> “We have never seen a work on the national literature of a people more carefully compiled than the present, and the bibliographical <term>prolegomena</term> <note>A preliminary discussion or introduction to a book.</note> deserves attentive perusal by all who would study either the political or the literary history of the greatest republic of the West.”—*The Leader*, March 26, 1859.

> “The subject of my letter today may seem to be of a purely literary character, but I feel justified to claim a more general interest for it. That subject is connected with the good reputation of the United States abroad. It is likewise connected with the general topic of my two former letters. I have spoken of the friends and the antagonists of the United States among European nations, and among the different classes of European society. I have stated that the antagonists are chiefly to be found among the aristocracy, not only of birth, but ‘of

# Publications of Trübner & Co.

mind"—as it has been called—likewise; not only among the privileged classes and those connected with government interests, but among those who live in the sphere of literature and art, and look down with contempt on a society in which utilitarian motives are believed to be paramount. And I have asserted that, leaving aside these differences in the opinions of certain classes, the Germans, as a whole, take a more lively and deeper interest in American affairs than in any other nation. Now, I am going to speak of a book just ready to leave the press of a London publisher, which, while it is a remarkable instance of the truth of my assertion regarding the Germans, must be considered as serving the interests of the United States by promoting the good reputation of American life to an uncommon degree.

> “The London book trade has a firm, Trübner and Co., of whose business transactions American literature, as well as literature about America, forms a principal branch. It is the firm that has lately published the bibliography of American languages. Mr. Nicolas Trübner is a German who has never lived in the United States, and yet he risks his time, labor, and money in literary publications for which efforts to find an American publisher would have been made in vain.
> 
> “The new publication by Mr. Trübner, to which I have referred, is a large <term>8vo</term> volume of 800 pages, under the title of ‘Bibliographical Guide to American Literature: A Classified List of Books Published in the United States of America, from 1817 to 1857. With Bibliographical Introduction, Notes, and Alphabetical Index. Compiled and edited by Nicolas Trübner.’
> 
> “This last remark has only too much truth in it. The United States, in the opinion of the great mass of even the well-educated people of Europe, is a country inhabited by a nation lost in the pursuit of material interests; a country in which the practical branches of certain sciences may be cultivated to a degree, but a country essentially without literature and art; a country not without newspapers—all the worse for it—but almost without books. Now, here Mr. Trübner, a German, comes out with a list of American books filling a thick volume, containing only American publications from the year 1817 onwards, from which time he dates the period of a more decided literary independence for the United States.
> 
> “Since no native-born, or even adopted American, has taken the trouble of compiling, arranging, digesting, editing, and publishing such a work, who else but a German could undertake it? Who else among the European nations would have thought American literature worth the labor, the time, and the money? And let me add that a smaller work of a similar character, ‘The Literature of American Local History,’ by the late Dr. Hermann Ludewig, was also the work of a German. It may be that the majority of the American public will find only a low degree of interest in works of this kind. The majority of the public of other nations will do the same, as it cannot be everyone’s business to understand the usefulness of bibliography, and of books containing nothing but the listing and description of books. One thing, however, must be apparent: the deep interest taken by some foreigners in the more intellectual spheres of American life; and if it is true that a clear historical insight into its own development—intellectual as well as material—is one of the most valuable acquisitions of a nation, future American generations will acknowledge the good services of those foreigners who, through their literary dedication, helped to prevent the national misfortune of the origins of American literary independence becoming lost in obscurity.” — *New York Daily Tribune, Dec., 1858.*
> 
> “It is remarkable and noteworthy that the most valuable manual of American literature should appear in London and be published by an English house. *Trübner’s Bibliographical Guide to American Literature* is a work of extraordinary skill and perseverance, providing an index to all the publications of the American press for the last forty years.”—*Harper’s Weekly, March 28th, 1859.*
> 
> “Mr. Trübner deserves all praise for having produced a work that is satisfactory in every way. No one who takes an interest in the subject it treats can do without it; and we have no doubt that booksellers in this country will come to consider it necessary as a professional manual, and second in importance for their trade only to the London Catalogue itself. That a foreigner and a London bookseller should have accomplished what Americans themselves have failed to do is most creditable to the compiler. The volume contains 149 pages of introductory material, featuring by far the best record of American literary history yet published, and 521 pages of classified book lists, to which a 33-page alphabetical index is added. This alphabetical index alone may claim to be one of the most valuable aids for enabling the student of literary history to form a just and perfect estimate of the great and rising importance of Anglo-American literature—the youngest and most unconstrained of all literatures illustrating the gradual development of the human mind.”—*The Press, Philadelphia, Oct. 11, 1858.*
> 
> “In this notice, we do not so much express the wish that Mr. Trübner may not find a public ungrateful for his labors, as congratulate American book-lovers in particular on the advantages within their reach through the acquisition and use of what Mr. Trübner has so opportunely supplied.”—*Washington National Intelligencer, March 22nd, 1859.*
> 
> “This volume contains a well-classified list of books published in the United States of America during the last forty years, preceded by a fairly comprehensive survey of American literary enterprise during the first half of the nineteenth century. The value of such a guide, which is fairly evident in itself, becomes even more so upon glancing over the five hundred and forty pages of close print that display the literary activity pervading the country of Prescott and Motley,

> of Irving and Hawthorne, of Poe and Longfellow, of Story and Wheaton, of Moses Stuart and Channing. This volume will be useful to the scholar, but to the librarian it is indispensable." —*Daily News*, March 24th, 1859.

> "There are hundreds of men of moderate scholarship who would gladly stand on some higher and more assured point. They feel that they have acquired much information, but they also feel the need of that subtle discipline, literary education, without which all mere learning is the *rudis indigesta moles* <note>a raw and unorganized mass</note>, as much of a stumbling block as an aid. To those in such a condition, works on bibliography are invaluable. For direction in classifying all reading, whether English or American, Allibone's Dictionary is admirable; but, for particular information as to the American side of the house, the recently published Bibliographical Guide to American Literature, by Nicholas Trübner of London, may be conscientiously commended. A careful reading of this truly remarkable work cannot fail to give any intelligent person a clear and complete idea of the whole state of American book-making, not only in its literary aspect, but in its historical, and, added to this, in its most mechanical details." —*Philadelphia Evening Bulletin*, March 5th, 1859.

> "But the best work on American bibliography yet published has come to us from London, where it has been compiled by the well-known bibliophile, Trübner. The work is remarkable for condensation and accuracy, though we have noted a few errors and omissions, upon which we should like to comment, had we now space to do so." —*New York Times*, March 26th, 1859.

> "Some of our readers, whose attention has been particularly called to scientific and literary matters, may remember meeting, some years since, in this country, a most intelligent foreigner, who visited the United States for the purpose of extending his business connections, and making a personal investigation into the condition of literature in the New World. Mr. Nicholas Trübner—the gentleman to whom we have made reference—although by birth a German, and by education and profession a London bookseller, could hardly be called a 'stranger in America,' for he had sent before him a most valuable 'letter of introduction,' in the shape of a carefully compiled register of American books and authors, entitled 'Bibliographical Guide to American Literature,' etc., pp. xxxii., 108. This manual was the germ of the important publication, the title of which the reader will find at the commencement of this article. Now, in consequence of Mr. Trübner's admirable classification and minute index, the inquirer after knowledge has nothing to do but copy from the Bibliographical Guide the titles of the American books which he wishes to consult, dispatch them to his library by a messenger, and in a few minutes he has before him the coveted volumes, through whose means he hopes to enlarge his acquisitions. Undoubtedly it would be a cause of well-founded reproach, of deep mortification to every intelligent American, if the arduous labors of the learned editor and compiler of this volume (whom we almost hesitate to call a foreigner), should fail to be appreciated in a country to which he has, by the preparation of this valuable work, proved himself so eminent a benefactor." —*Pennsylvania Enquirer*, March 26th, 1859.

> "The editor of this volume has acquired a knowledge of the productions of the American press which is rarely exhibited on the other side of the Atlantic, and which must command the admiration of the best informed students of the subject in this country. His former work on American bibliography, though making no pretensions to completeness, was a valuable index to various branches of learning that had been successfully cultivated by our scholars; but, neither in comprehensiveness of plan nor thoroughness of execution, can it be compared to the elaborate and minute record of American literature contained in this volume. The duty of the editor required extensive research, vigilant discrimination, and untiring diligence; and in the performance of his task we are no less struck with the accuracy of detail than with the extent of his information. The period to which the volume is devoted comprises only the last forty years; but within that time the literature of this country has received its most efficient impulses, and been widely unfolded in the various departments of intellectual activity. If we were permitted to speak on behalf of American scholars, we should not fail to congratulate Mr. Trübner on the eminent success with which he has accomplished his plan, and the ample and impartial justice with which he has registered the productions of our native authorship. After a careful examination of his volume, we are bound to express our high appreciation of the intelligence, fairness, and industry which are conspicuous in its pages; for exactness and precision it is no less remarkable than for extent of research; few, if any, important publications are omitted on its catalogue, and although, as is inevitable in a work of this nature, an erroneous letter has sometimes crept into a name, or an erroneous figure into a date, no one can consult it habitually without learning to rely on its trustworthiness, as well as its completeness." —*Harper's Magazine*, April, 1859.

> "Nor is the book a dry catalogue only of the names and contents of the publications of America. Prefixed to it are valuable bibliographical <term>prolegomena</term> <note>preliminary remarks or introductory sections</note>, instructive to the antiquarian, as well as useful to the philologist. In this portion of the work, Mr. Trübner had the assistance of the late Dr. Ludewig, whose early death was a great loss to philological science. Mr. Moran, the assistant-secretary to the American Legation, has added to the volume a historical summary of the literature of America; and Mr. Edward Edwards is responsible for an interesting account of the public libraries of the United States. To Mr. Trübner's own careful superintendence and hard work, however, the student must ever remain indebted for one of the most useful and well-arranged books on bibliographical lore ever published. In addition to this, it is right to congratulate Mr. Trübner on the fact, that his present work confirms the opinion passed on his 'Bibliotheca Glottica,' that among the booksellers themselves honorable literary eminence may exist, without clashing with business arrangements. The booksellers of old were authors, and Mr. Trübner emulates their example." —*Morning Chronicle*, March 22nd, 1859.

# Publications of Trübner & Co.

"Mr. Trübner, who is not only a bookseller but a book lover, has, in this work, significantly increased the claim he already had upon the respect of all book lovers everywhere, but especially in the United States, to whose literature he has now made such an important and useful contribution. Being much larger than a previous book under a similar title which he published in 1855, and much more comprehensive in every respect, the present work constitutes a new tool for our libraries, as well as the most valuable existing aid for those students who, without libraries, have an interest in knowing their contents." —*Baltimore American*, April 2nd, 1859.

"Lastly, published only the other day, is Trübner's Bibliographical Guide to American Literature, which provides a categorized list of books published in the United States during the last forty years, with a bibliographical introduction, notes, and an alphabetical index. This octavo volume has been compiled and edited by Mr. Nicolas Trübner, the well-known head of one of the great foreign publishing and importing houses of London, who is also the editor of Ludewig and Turner's Literature of American Aboriginal Languages. Besides containing a categorized list of books with an alphabetical index, Mr. Trübner's book has an introduction in which he treats the history of American literature with considerable depth, including newspapers, periodicals, and public libraries. It is fair to state that Mr. Trübner's Bibliographical Guide was published after Allibone's Dictionary, but was printed at about the same time." —*Philadelphia Press*, April 4th, 1859.

"This is a valuable work for book buyers. For its compilation, we are indebted to a foreign book enthusiast, but one who has made himself familiar with American literature and has obtained the most extensive sources of information. The volume contains: I. Bibliographical Prolegomena; II. Contributions toward a History of American Literature; III. Notices of Public Libraries of the United States. These three sections form the introduction and occupy one hundred and fifty pages. IV. A categorized list of books; V. An alphabetical list of authors. This plan is somewhat similar to that adopted in Watts's celebrated 'Bibliotheca Britannica,' a work of immense value whose compilation took some forty years. The classified portion of the present work enables the reader to find easily the names of all books on any one subject. The alphabetical index of authors enables the reader to find instantly the names of all authors and all their works, including the numerous periodical publications of the last forty years. Mr. Trübner deserves the thanks of the literary world for his plan and its skillful execution." —*New York Courier and Enquirer*, April 11th, 1859.

"The author, in a ten-page preface, explains the ideas that led him to undertake his book and the plan he chose to adopt. In a scholarly introduction, he provides a critical review of various works relating to America; he highlights those that have contributed most to the establishment of a specialized American literature and recounts its history. This part of his work is intended to bring him honor; it is methodically divided into the colonial period and the American period and contains very interesting information—which one is pleased to find gathered for the first time—on the progress of printing in America, authors' salaries, the book trade, and periodical publications. This introduction, which is no less than 150 pages, ends with a statistical table of all the public libraries in the different states of the Union.

"The methodical and annotated catalog of works occupies no fewer than 521 pages; it forms 32 sections, each dedicated to one of the branches of the human sciences. The section giving the list of works concerning geography and travel (section XVI) includes nearly 600 items, and among them are found references to several works whose existence in Europe we did not even suspect. A general alphabetical index by authors' names, which concludes this book, allows for the shortening of research that is often quite arduous. Mr. Trübner's bibliographical guide is a monument raised to American scientific and literary activity and, as such, it is worthy of taking its place alongside works of the same kind published in Europe by the likes of Brunet, Lowndes, and Ebert. (V. A. Malte-Brun.)" —*Nouvelles Annales des Voyages*, April 1859.

(A to D) <term>8vo.</term><note>Octavo, a standard book size</note> cloth, pp. xxiv. 508, 14s.; Vol. II. (E to P) <term>8vo.</term><note>Octavo, a standard book size</note> cloth, pp. 578, 14s.; Vol. III., Part I. (Q to Sy), <term>8vo.</term><note>Octavo, a standard book size</note> pp. 366, 10s. 6d.; Vol. III., Part II. > "Dictionaries are a class of books not usually considered light reading; but no intelligent man would deserve pity if he found himself shut up on a rainy day in a lonely house in the dreariest part of Salisbury Plain, with no other means of recreation than that which Mr. Wedgwood's Dictionary of Etymology could provide for him. He would read it through from cover to cover in one sitting, and only regret that he did not have the second volume to begin upon immediately. It is a very impressive book, of great research, full of delightful surprises, and a storehouse of the 'fairy tales' of linguistic science."—*Spectator*.

# Publications of Trübner & Co.

**Xenos.**—EAST AND WEST; a Diplomatic History of the Annexation of the Ionian Islands to the Kingdom of Greece. Accompanied by a Translation of the Dispatches exchanged between the Greek Government and its Plenipotentiary at London, and a Collection of the Principal Treaties, Conventions, and Protocols concerning the Ionian Islands and Greece, concluded between 1797 and 1864. By Stefanos Xenos. <term>Royal 8vo.</term><note>Royal Octavo, a book size of about 6.25 by 10 inches</note> cloth, pp. iv. and 304. 1865. 12s.<note>12 shillings</note>

**Yates.**—A BENGALÍ GRAMMAR. By the late Rev. W. Yates, D.D., Reprinted, with improvements, from his Introduction to the Bengálí Language. Edited by I. Wenger. <term>Fcap. 8vo.</term><note>Foolscap octavo, a book size of about 4.25 by 6.75 inches</note>, boards, pp. iv. and 150. 1864. 3s. 6d.<note>3 shillings and 6 pence</note>

**Yates.**—THE ELEMENTS OF THE SCIENCE OF GRAMMAR, put forward and explained in a totally different manner from what they have ever before been, and based on rigid definitions, incontrovertible axioms, and general principles, illustrated by a comparison of the structure of the English and Turkish languages, so as to be at once an introduction to the science of grammar, for all who wish to learn that science, and a complete Turkish grammar for the student of that language. By Edward Yates, B.A., Barrister-at-Law. Most kindly and valuably assisted by Captains Mahmood and Hussein, of the Imperial Guard of the Sultan, and by Hiry Bey. <term>12mo.</term><note>Duodecimo, a book size of about 5 by 7.5 inches</note> cloth, pp. ii. and 226. 1857. 5s.<note>5 shillings</note>

**Yates.**—DESCRIPTIVE CATALOGUE OF A COLLECTION OF CURRENT COINS OF ALL COUNTRIES, in the International Exhibition. Class 13, North Gallery. By James Yates, M.A., F.R.S. <term>12mo.</term><note>Duodecimo</note> <term>sewed</term><note>Bound with thread but without a hard cover</note>, pp. 69. 1862. 6d.<note>6 pence</note>

**Zeller.**—STRAUSS AND RENAN. An Essay by E. Zeller. Translated from the German, with Introductory Remarks by the Translator. <term>Post 8vo.</term><note>Post octavo, a book size of about 5 by 8 inches</note> cloth, pp. 110. 1866. 2s. 6d.<note>2 shillings and 6 pence</note>
